A Theory for Probabilistic Polynomial-Time Reasoning
Lijie Chen, Jiatu Li, Igor C. Oliveira, Ryan Williams

Abstract
In this work, we propose a new bounded arithmetic theory, denoted 𝖠𝖯𝖷 1 {\mathsf{APX}}_{1} , designed to formalize a broad class of probabilistic arguments commonly used in theoretical computer science. Under plausible assumptions, 𝖠𝖯𝖷 1 {\mathsf{APX}}_{1} is strictly weaker than previously proposed frameworks, such as the theory 𝖠𝖯𝖢 1 \mathsf{APC}_{1} introduced in the seminal work of Jeřábek (2007). From a computational standpoint, 𝖠𝖯𝖷 1 {\mathsf{APX}}_{1} is closely tied to approximate counting and to the central question in derandomization, the 𝗉𝗋𝖡𝖯𝖯 \mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}} versus 𝗉𝗋𝖯 \mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}} problem, whereas 𝖠𝖯𝖢 1 \mathsf{APC}_{1} is linked to the dual weak pigeonhole principle and to the existence of Boolean functions with exponential circuit complexity. A key motivation for introducing 𝖠𝖯𝖷 1 {\mathsf{APX}}_{1} is that its weaker axioms expose finer proof-theoretic structure, making it a natural setting for several lines of research, including unprovability of complexity conjectures and reverse mathematics of randomized lower bounds. In particular, the framework we develop for 𝖠𝖯𝖷 1 {\mathsf{APX}}_{1} enables the formulation of precise questions concerning the provability of 𝗉𝗋𝖡𝖯𝖯 = 𝗉𝗋𝖯 \mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}} in deterministic feasible mathematics. Since the (un)provability of 𝖯 {\mathsf{P}} versus 𝖭𝖯 {\mathsf{NP}} in bounded arithmetic has long served as a central theme in the field, we expect this line of investigation to be of particular interest. Our technical contributions include developing a comprehensive foundation for probabilistic reasoning from weaker axioms, formalizing non-trivial results from theoretical computer science in 𝖠𝖯𝖷 1 {\mathsf{APX}}_{1} , and establishing a tailored witnessing theorem for its provably total 𝖳𝖥𝖭𝖯 {\mathsf{TFNP}} problems. As a byproduct of our analysis of the minimal proof-theoretic strength required to formalize statements arising in theoretical computer science, we resolve an open problem regarding the provability of 𝖠𝖢 0 {\mathsf{AC}}^{0} lower bounds in 𝖯𝖵 1 \mathsf{PV}_{1} , which was considered in earlier works by Razborov (1995), Krajíček (1995), and Müller and Pich (2020).



1
Introduction

1.1
Overview

Bounded arithmetic extends traditional complexity theory by capturing not only the computational resources (e.g., running time or circuit size) required by algorithms, but also the complexity of proving their correctness. By integrating computational and proof complexity within a unified framework, it opens new angles on foundational questions in theoretical computer science. The area has a long history (see
(
35
;
58
;
28
;
59
)
and references therein) and has seen renewed momentum through new formalizations
(
12
;
32
;
52
;
7
)
; unprovability results
(
76
;
63
;
8
;
20
;
21
;
87
)
; connections to
\mathsf{TFNP}

(
66
)
, complex analysis
(
49
)
, reverse mathematics
(
19
;
9
)
, complexity lower bounds
(
34
;
17
)
, and propositional proof complexity
(
60
)
; and applications in cryptography
(
42
;
50
;
51
;
41
)
, among other developments. We refer to
(
16
;
74
)
for background and for connections to algorithms and complexity theory.

Two central and extensively studied theories are Cook’s
\mathsf{PV}_{1}

(
29
;
57
)
(see also
(
59
;
65
)
) and Jeřábek’s
\mathsf{APC}_{1}

(
43
;
44
;
46
)
. The theory
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
formalizes polynomial-time reasoning and captures many classical results in algorithms and complexity. Since it is unclear whether randomized algorithms can, in general, be derandomized,
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
is not well-suited for reasoning about probabilities or analyzing randomized algorithms. The theory
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
extends
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
by adding the dual weak pigeonhole principle
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
, yielding a convenient framework for reasoning about probabilities and randomized constructions. In particular,
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
(and its mild extensions) is sufficient to formalize several nontrivial results, including the correctness of randomized algorithms for graph problems
(
61
)
, polynomial identity testing
(
9
)
, and circuit lower bounds
(
69
)
. However, the axioms of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
may be stronger than necessary: many results of interest could plausibly be provable in a weaker theory closer to
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
.

There are concrete reasons to expect
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
to exceed the minimal strength required for probabilistic polynomial-time reasoning. On the one hand,
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
is tied to
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
and to the
existence
of functions of exponential circuit complexity; from a computational perspective, the
explicit construction
of such functions (i.e., circuit lower bounds) is a widely used derandomization assumption that may be stronger than the derandomization of
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}
(see, e.g.,
(
30
;
33
;
24
)
). On the other hand, even if
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
with a “feasible” proof,
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
need not collapse to
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
; indeed, under plausible cryptographic assumptions,
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
is strictly stronger than
\mathsf{PV}_{1}

37
.

The search for a weaker theory that still supports the broad class of probabilistic arguments used across theoretical computer science is motivated by several considerations:

•

Unprovability of complexity-theoretic conjectures.
A central objective in this area is to identify frameworks that both formalize existing tools in complexity theory and remain amenable to unprovability results.
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
is likely strictly stronger than
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
by
37
, and its witnessing functions cannot in general be made deterministic even if
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
, which complicate unprovability arguments and pose significant challenges (see, e.g.,
63
;
18
). In particular, the introduction of the strong principle
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
is the main obstacle to extending unprovability of complexity lower bounds in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}

76
to
\mathsf{APC}_{1}

63
.

•

Bounded reverse mathematics with probabilistic reasoning.
Following recent developments such as
19
;
9
(see also
(
28
)
for related background), one can hope to pursue a systematic reverse mathematics of algorithms and complexity theory that classifies “probabilistic proofs” by the axioms they use. Similarly, it suggests the possibility of classifying randomized algorithms by the complexity of their
correctness proofs
, supplementing the standard classification via space (see, e.g.,
72
) or circuit complexity (see, e.g.,
4
). This perspective is potentially insightful for derandomization, namely, derandomization based on the proof complexity of correctness proofs. Because
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
is itself strong,
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
is an overly powerful base theory for fine-grained correspondences weaker than
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
.

•

Correctness proofs in cryptography.
Jain and Jin
(
42
)
and subsequent papers
(
50
;
51
;
41
;
68
)
explore
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
and its connection to propositional proofs to help construct
i\mathcal{O}
and other cryptographic primitives, highlighting that the logical complexity of proving certain statements can play an important role in cryptographic systems and their efficiency. In particular,
41
;
51
rely heavily on cryptographic primitives with
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
proofs of
correctness
. However, existing work typically considers “perfect correctness” because
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
cannot natively talk about approximate counting and randomness, whereas
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
seems both too strong and inconvenient for this purpose.

•

Feasible provability of probabilistic statements.
It is natural to formulate precise, feasible notions of the provability of
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
and related questions. Yet even formulating
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
feasibly is nontrivial, as it seems to require defining probabilistic computation within the theory in the first place. Given that the (un)provability of
{\mathsf{P}}={\mathsf{NP}}
in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
has long been central to bounded arithmetic (see, e.g.,
(
27
;
74
)
), this direction holds significant potential for advancing the study of the interplay between randomized computations and mathematical proofs.

These considerations point to a common objective: designing a
minimal theory
for reasoning about probabilities and randomized constructions in feasible mathematics.

Summary of contributions.

We propose a theory corresponding to “probabilistic polynomial-time reasoning” in a strong sense. Our main conceptual and technical contributions are:

1.

Theory
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
and its relative strength.
We introduce
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, establish its basic properties, and develop core probabilistic tools. The theory extends
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
and is contained in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, in the sense that all of its consequences in the language of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
are also provable in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
. Moreover, under plausible assumptions,
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is strictly weaker than
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
.

2.

Advanced formalizations.
We formalize in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
several nontrivial results from algorithms and complexity, including the Blum-Luby-Rubinfeld linearity testing, Schwartz-Zippel lemma
1
1

1

It is worth noting that the standard proof of Schwartz-Zippel lemma (see, e.g.,
(
6
, Lemma A.36)
) is not known to be formalizable even in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
. In this work, we formalize an alternative proof due to Atserias and Tzameret
9
in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.
, and an average-case
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
lower bound for Parity. Additionally, as a byproduct of our refined analysis of
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
circuits in bounded arithmetic, we describe a matching worst-case lower bound in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. The latter formalization addresses a problem considered by Razborov
(
81
)
, Krajíček
(
58
, Section 15.2)
, and Müller-Pich
(
69
)
, which was only known for stronger theories.

3.

Tailored witnessing theorem.
We show that the provably total
{\mathsf{NP}}
relations of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
deterministically reduce to a natural
\mathsf{TFZPP}
problem
2
2

2

A
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
problem
R(x,y)
is said to be in
\mathsf{TFZPP}
if, for every input
x
, at least an inverse-polynomial fraction of strings
y
are valid solutions, i.e.,
R(x,y)=1
. It is clear that
\mathsf{TFZPP}
problems admit simple zero-error randomized algorithms running in polynomial time.
we introduce,
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
. Moreover, if
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
, then
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
admits deterministic polynomial-time witnessing.

4.

Feasible derandomization.
Using the new framework, we put forward a natural formalization of the fundamental question: Is
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}
feasibly provable? In other words, is there a deterministic feasible proof of general derandomization?

5.

Reverse mathematics of randomness.
Finally, we show that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
serves as a suitable base theory for developing the reverse mathematics of average-case and randomized lower bounds, illustrated here through the study of randomized communication protocols and their communication complexity.

Before presenting our results in more detail, we provide additional context and background.

Dual use of
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
.

Why does
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, until now the weakest known theory capable of formalizing probabilities and randomized algorithms, appear stronger than necessary? By looking into the construction of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}

43
;
44
;
46
, we observe two different reasons for introducing
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
.

•

First, it is used to implement approximate counting. Jeřábek
43
shows that
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
proves the existence of an exponentially hard Boolean function, and by formalizing a form of correctness of the Nisan-Wigderson PRG
71
in the theory
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
, we can approximately compute the acceptance probability of circuits by instantiating the PRG with the hard Boolean function.

•

Second, it also serves as a counting principle to derive tools in combinatorics and probability theory, including the inclusion-exclusion principle, union bound, and Chernoff bound
(
46
, Section 2)
.

The first role appears essential, as approximate counting is the foundation for the formalization of probabilistic polynomial-time algorithms. However,
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
, as a counting principle, appears to be overly powerful and not necessary for many applications.

Remark 1.1

(Computational Aspects of
\mathsf{dWPHP}
)
.

To add more context,
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
asserts that for any function
f
implemented by circuits whose co-domain is much larger than its domain, say
f:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{n+1}
, there exists a string in the co-domain that does not have a pre-image. The computational aspect of the principle, namely the search problem of finding such a string given a function
f
, has recently drawn attention in computational complexity (see
56
for a survey). This problem, which is now called the
Range Avoidance Problem

53
;
54
;
82
, is known to be hard even for nondeterministic search algorithms under plausible assumptions
37
;
25
.

Axiomatizing approximate counting in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

Since
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
fulfills two essential functions in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, devising a weaker theory is nontrivial — one must find a way to relax the counting principle without sacrificing the capacity to formalize approximate counting.

Our approach, which in hindsight appears quite natural, is to put
approximate counting
at the foundation, elevating it to a central primitive rather than deriving it from stronger principles such as
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})

43
;
44
;
46
. Starting from
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
as the base theory, we directly introduce an oracle that is intended to perform approximate counting, and govern it with appropriate axioms. Through this approach, we decouple
the concept of approximate counting
from
counting principles
.

The main technical challenge is to select an appropriate set of axioms. These axioms should be sufficiently strong to carry out our advanced formalizations, reverse mathematics results, and potentially more results in theoretical computer science. At the same time, the set of axioms should be minimal. The contradictory objectives make it hard to select appropriate axioms; indeed, it is not even a priori clear whether a suitable
finite
set of axioms exists without resorting to variants of
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
.

Perhaps surprisingly, we distill four simple and intuitive axioms that suffice to implement all our results, among which the only nontrivial axiom captures the “local” behavior of the approximate counting oracles. Arguably, this makes
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
a plausible candidate for the minimal theory of probabilistic polynomial-time reasoning.

Remark 1.2

(Minimal Assumption for Derandomization)
.

The conjectured inclusion
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}\subseteq\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
is a central question in derandomization.
The celebrated results of Impagliazzo, Nisan, and Wigderson
71
;
40
give a positive answer under
{\mathsf{E}}\nsubseteq\text{i.o.-}{\mathsf{SIZE}}[2^{\Omega(n)}]
, a plausible worst-case circuit lower bound. Conversely, derandomization results are also known to imply weaker circuit lower bounds such as
{\mathsf{NTIME}}(n^{\omega(1)})\nsubseteq{\mathsf{P}}_{/{\mathsf{poly}}}
(see, e.g.,
38
;
89
;
84
). Yet it has been a longstanding open problem whether the strong circuit lower bounds used in
71
;
40
are
necessary
for proving
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
. Indeed, there has been significant progress indicating that derandomization may
not
require strong circuit lower bounds, see, e.g.,
30
;
33
;
23
. Moreover, several
characterizations
of
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
have been recently discovered
67
;
55
;
24
;
22
;
64
, motivated by the question of understanding the
minimal
assumption required for derandomization.

In a sense, our results attempt to address a similar question in the context of proof complexity. We aim to propose a
minimal theory
that is strong enough to carry out meaningful feasible proofs on probabilistic polynomial-time algorithms. In particular, we provide evidence that
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
and the existence of hard Boolean functions, which are at the foundation of Jeřábek’s theory
\mathsf{APC}_{1}

(
43
;
44
;
46
)
, might not be necessary in a minimal theory for probabilistic polynomial-time reasoning.

1.2
Main Contributions

We now describe our contributions and their implications in detail.

1.2.1
Theory
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}

As alluded to above, rather than deriving probabilities from stronger combinatorial principles (as in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
via
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
), we axiomatize approximate counting directly. Our aim is a weaker theory in which the probability of any
feasibly definable event
3
3

3

In other words, an event
E\subseteq\{0,1\}^{m}
for which there is a polynomial-size Boolean circuit
C
such that
C(x)=1
if and only if
x\in E
.
can be named and reasoned about with additive slack, while keeping proof-theoretic strength low.

To achieve this, we introduce a first-order bounded arithmetic theory,
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, whose central primitive is an
approximate counting
function
{\mathsf{P}}
. Intuitively, given a Boolean circuit
C
on
n
input bits and a
precision parameter

\Delta
, the term
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
returns a rational number in
[0,1]
that approximates the acceptance probability of
C
within additive error
1/|\Delta|
, where
|\Delta|
denotes the bitlength of the input parameter
\Delta
. For convenience, we often write
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)
instead of
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
, where
\delta=1/|\Delta|
.

The equational core of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, called
{\mathsf{APX}}
, is obtained by extending Cook’s equational theory
\mathsf{PV}
with the oracle symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
(the new language is denoted
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
) and its governing axioms.
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is then the usual first–order closure of
{\mathsf{APX}}
, i.e., universal closures of
{\mathsf{APX}}
-equations together with the standard
\mathsf{PV}
-style induction on notation.

Remark 1.3

(
\mathsf{PV}
and
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
)
.

\mathsf{PV}

(
29
)
is an equational theory whose intended model is
\mathbb{N}
with the usual interpretation of basic symbols such as
0
,
+
, and
\times
. Its language contains a function symbol for every polynomial-time algorithm
f\colon\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
(for any fixed
k
); these symbols and their defining axioms are given via Cobham’s characterization of the polynomial-time functions. The theory includes an induction scheme formalizing binary search and, in particular, proves induction for quantifier-free formulas (i.e., polynomial-time predicates). A standard first-order strengthening is
\mathsf{PV}_{1}

(
57
)
. While the formal definition of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
is fairly technical, the theory is robust: distinct presentations yield the same theorems. For example,
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
has an equivalent axiomatization that avoids Cobham’s theorem
(
45
)
; alternatively, it can be presented as the set of all
\forall\Sigma^{b}_{1}
-sentences provable in Buss’s theory
{\mathsf{S}}^{1}_{2}

(
14
)
. We refer to
(
74
)
for a brief overview and to
(
65
)
for a detailed introduction.

A key aspect of the definition of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is to employ “local” constraints governing the behavior of
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}
, which together enforce the “global” desired behavior, i.e., that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}
approximates the acceptance probability of any input circuit up to an additive error term
\delta
. The entire probabilistic machinery of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
(random variables, expectation, tail bounds, etc.) is built on top of the axioms below.

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
axioms governing
{\mathsf{P}}
.

All axioms are universal
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
-equations; below
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
is a freely available ‘‘slack’’ parameter used to absorb routine finite-precision effects.
4
4

4

The expression
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
is standard notation in bounded arithmetic used to denote that
\beta=1/|y|
for some variable
y
, where
|y|
is the bitlength of
y
.
We sketch the statements at an informal level; the formal version appears in
Section

2
.

•

Basic Axiom.
For every Boolean circuit
C
and
\Delta
, the value
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
is a rational in
[0,1]
(encoded in
\mathsf{PV}
) and all
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
-provable equations hold. Together with an output-length bound for
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
, this forces feasibility of approximate counting at any requested precision.

•

Boundary Axiom.
If
C
is syntactically constant (reads no inputs), then
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)\in\{0,1\}
agrees with the output bit of
C
. Thus
{\mathsf{P}}
is
exact
on trivial cases.

•

Precision Consistency.
For any two precisions
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
and circuit
C
,

\big|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta_{1}}(C)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta_{2}}(C)\big|\ \leq\ \delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\beta.

Hence asking for finer precision can only move the reported probability by the sum of the specified error parameters (up to
\beta
).

•

Local Consistency.
If
C
has at least one input bit, and
\mathsf{Fix}_{b}(C)
denotes the circuit obtained by fixing the rightmost input bit to
b\in\{0,1\}
, then

\Big|\,{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)\ -\ \tfrac{1}{2}\big({\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{0}(C))+{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{1}(C))\big)\,\Big|\ \leq\ 2\delta+\beta.

Thus the reported acceptance probability of
C
is (up to additive slack of
\beta
) the average of the reported probabilities after fixing a fresh random bit. This aims to capture the intended semantics of counting over the uniform hypercube.
5
5

5

In other words, for every (feasibly definable) set
X\subseteq\{0,1\}^{n}
, as
X
is the disjoint union of
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
, where
X_{b}=\{x\in X\mid x_{n}=b\}
, we expect
|X|\approx|X_{0}|+|X_{1}|
.

In practice, one can think of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
as the theory extending
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
with the symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
and its governing axioms, together with induction over quantifier-free formulas in the language
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
. Everything else – such as random variables, expectation, union bound, etc. – will be introduced and derived from the language and axioms inside
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

Soundness of approximate counting in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

We say that a
\mathsf{PV}
-standard model (i.e.,
\mathbb{N}
) where the function symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
is interpreted by any correct approximate counting function (returning a rational within
\pm 1/|\Delta|
and exact on syntactically constant circuits) is a
standard model
of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. A simple but central result shows that these are
exactly
the models satisfying the axioms (“admissible models”), yielding semantic soundness for the intended interpretation and a correct axiomatization of approximate counting when the underlying model is
\mathbb{N}
(see
Section

2.3
).

Minimality of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

We believe that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is a good candidate of the
minimal theory
for probabilistic polynomial-time reasoning. This is not a formal assertion from a mathematical perspective. However, the axioms above appear close to the weakest workable base theory that can consistently
define
and
operate on
approximate probabilities of feasibly described events. Specifically:

•

Any theory that reasons about probabilistic polynomial time algorithms should be able to
define
the acceptance probability of the algorithms. This requires the capability of approximate counting with an additive error, i.e., the symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
.

•

Because the
Basic Axiom
and the
Boundary Axiom
are rather syntactic promises of the oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
, we expect them to be available. Arguably, the
Precision Consistency Axiom
, which asserts the consistency of
{\mathsf{P}}
on different precision parameters, should also be available. Note that these three axioms are not sufficient, as one can easily specify a trivial and incorrect polynomial-time function such that these axioms are provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
.

•

Therefore, we use the
Local Consistency Axiom
to capture the correctness of
{\mathsf{P}}
— it shows that the approximate counting oracle withstands a simple statistical test with three queries made throughout the proof. It seems unlikely that one can make nontrivial use of an oracle for the purpose of approximate counting that may fail this test; subsequently, the axiom also seems necessary.

Another evidence of the minimality of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is that, computationally, the function symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
aligns with the
Circuit Acceptance Probability Problem
(
\mathsf{CAPP}
), which is complete for
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}
(see, e.g.,
(
88
)
). In contrast, the
Range Avoidance Problem
, which corresponds to
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
and is relevant for
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, is likely hard even against nondeterministic algorithms
37
;
25
.

1.2.2
Probabilistic Reasoning in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}

We develop a self-contained “probabilistic calculus” inside
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
using the approximate counting function
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}
. As a preliminary step, we show that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}
behaves in the expected way on feasibly described events. Concretely,
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
establishes the following properties (each up to an arbitrarily small additive slack
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
):

•

Semantic invariance.

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}
respects semantic equivalence, i.e., if
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves that circuits
C
and
D
compute the same function, then
\bigl|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(D)\bigr|\leq 2\cdot\delta+\beta
(
Lemma

3.2
).

•

Permutation invariance.
Permuting input bits does not noticeably change the value of
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}
. In other words, for any circuit
C
and permutation
\pi
of input bits,

\bigl|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C\circ\pi)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)\bigr|\leq 2\cdot\delta+\beta

(
Lemma

3.5
).

•

Existence via the probabilistic method.
Suppose that strings accepted by a circuit
C
are considered
good
. Then if good strings are abundant, i.e., more than
(\delta+\beta)
-fraction with respect to precision parameter
\delta
, there must exist a good string (
Lemma

3.6
). A bit more formally,

\mathsf{APX}_{1}\vdash\forall n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}~\forall C~(\beta>0\land{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)>\delta+\beta\to\exists x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C(x)=1).

•

Consistency on concrete circuits.

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}
agrees with simple tests. For instance, for a naturally defined threshold circuit
C_{<t}(x)
on
n
-bit inputs that accepts if and only if
x
(viewed as an integer) is less than
t
,
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{<t})\approx t/2^{n}
(see
Section

3.1.4
).

These meta-properties ensure that the definitions and inequalities developed in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
inherit the intended probabilistic behavior with only small, explicitly controlled additive losses.

With these guarantees in place, we now introduce
feasible random variables
. A random variable
X
is specified by an explicit support
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
, a seed length
n
, and a multi-output sampler circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to V
. Its
approximate expectation
is defined by querying
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}
on the indicator Boolean circuits
\{C_{v}\}_{v\in V}
, where
C_{v}(z)
accepts
z
if and only if
C(z)=v
. In other words:

\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]\;\triangleq\;\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{v}).

We observe that there exists a
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
function
{\mathsf{E}}(V,n,C,\Delta)
that computes
\Ex_{|\Delta|^{-1}}[X]
for the random variable
X
defined by
(V,n,C)
. Specifically,
{\mathsf{E}}
enumerates all
v\in V
, constructs the corresponding circuit
C_{v}
, queries the oracle to obtain
p_{v}\leftarrow{\mathsf{P}}(C_{v},\Delta)
, and outputs the sum
\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot p_{v}
.

We introduce a central technical tool that provides a general version of the
averaging argument for expectation
(
Section

3.2.3
): Given random variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}
on the same seed and coefficients
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}
,
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
can
search
for a suffix
z
of the seed such that a lower bound on the value
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]
is approximately preserved after fixing that suffix. This is used repeatedly to move between
global
and
pointwise
statements and underlies the proof of several results. We explain this technique in more detail in
Section

1.3
.

Using the tool described above, together with some additional ideas,
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
derives approximate formulations of several standard probability inequalities. In particular, it establishes the
linearity of expectation
for linear combinations of feasible random variables, the
union bound
for polynomially many events, and
Markov’s inequality
for non-negative variables with the usual
1/k
decay.
6
6

6

We note that some results suffer an approximation loss that depends on
\|V\|\triangleq\sum_{v\in V}|v|
and on the magnitude of the involved coefficients, where here
|\cdot|
denotes absolute value. In applications where these quantities are polynomially bounded, this can be mitigated by taking sufficiently small parameters
\delta
and
\beta
in the application of
\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]
.

Remark 1.4

(Example: Union Bound in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
; see
Theorem

3.20
)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,m,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}
be single-output circuits, and
V=\{0,1\}
. Suppose that
\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
i\in[m]
,
C_{i}(x)\in V
, and let
Y,X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be random variables defined as follows.

•

For each
i\in[m]
,
X_{i}
is defined by
(V,n,C_{i})
.

•

Y
is defined by
(V,n,S)
, where
S(x)\in\{0,1\}
is a circuit such that
S(x)\leq C_{1}(x)\lor\dots\lor C_{m}(x)
.

Then we have
\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\dots+\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}]+(2\delta+\beta)\cdot m
.

Additionally,
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
defines an
approximate variance

\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X]\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}\!\left[(X-\mu)^{2}\right],

with
\mu\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]
,
and shows an identity of the form
\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X]\approx\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X^{2}]-\mu^{2}
, which leads to a natural formulation of
Chebyshev’s inequality
.
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
also formalizes
(almost) pairwise independence
via approximate covariance, and proves that the variance of a sum is (approximately) the sum of variances for (almost) pairwise independent variables.

Finally, we address
independence and concentration
. We work in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
with
explicit
independence: variables are sampled by disjoint parts of the seed. Under this notion, the theory proves a
multiplication principle

\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[XY]\;\approx\;\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y],

and, for Bernoulli variables, a convenient product bound

\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}\!\Bigl[\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}\Bigr]-\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]\Bigr|\;\leq\;8\delta\cdot m.

These yield
one-sided error reduction
. Moreover,
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves a
Chernoff bound
for sums of
m=O(\log n)
i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables; the bound has the standard exponential tail with controlled additive slack.

Remark 1.5

(Example: One-Sided Error Reduction in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
; see
Theorem

3.33
)
.

For a Boolean circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
, let
C^{\lor k}:\{0,1\}^{nk}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit defined as
C^{\lor k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\triangleq\bigvee_{i\in[k]}C(x_{i})
. The following statement is provable in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. For any
n,k,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
, if
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\lnot C)\leq\varepsilon
then
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\lnot C^{\lor k})\leq(\delta+\beta+\varepsilon)^{k}+\delta+\beta
.

We refer to
Section

3
for a detailed description of how these different notions and results are implemented in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

1.2.3
Theoretical Computer Science in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}

As explained above, approximate counting – as axiomatized in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
– suffices to build the typical probabilistic toolkit (such as existence arguments, linearity of expectation, averaging argument, union bound, Markov, Chebyshev, limited independence, error reduction, and a version of Chernoff for logarithmically many samples). This lightweight yet robust framework can be exploited to formalize several nontrivial results. We illustrate this point through a set of detailed formalizations of influential results from different areas of theoretical computer science:

•

Yao’s distinguisher-to-predictor transformation via the hybrid argument, a central tool in computational pseudorandomness (see
Theorem

4.1
);

•

the Schwartz-Zippel Lemma (as stated in
(
9
)
), an algebraic result for polynomial identity testing with broad applications in randomness and complexity (see
Theorem

4.4
);

•

the classical lower bound for the parity function against bounded-depth polynomial-size circuits in circuit complexity (see
Section

4.4
);

•

the correctness of the Blum-Luby-Rubinfeld linearity test from sublinear time algorithms and property testing (see
Section

4.5
).

For concreteness and in order to contrast our results with previous work, we focus here on the formalization of circuit lower bounds for the
n
-bit parity function, denoted
\oplus_{n}
. In fact, we show that a stronger
average-case
lower bound against depth-
d
Boolean circuits (
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}_{d}
) can be proved in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

Theorem 1.6

(Average-Case
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
Lower Bound for
\oplus_{n}
in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
)
.

For all constants
k,d\geq 1
, there exists a constant
n_{0}\geq 1
such that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
n>n_{0}
, and
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be an
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}_{d}
circuit of size at most
n^{k}
. Let
T_{C}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that, given
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, outputs
1
if and only if
C(x)=\oplus_{n}(x)
. Then

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C})\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{n^{k}}+\delta+\beta.

(1.1)

The main technical challenge is to avoid “encoding-based counting arguments” (pigeonhole-principle variants) unavailable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, such as those used in Razborov’s proof of the switching lemma
81
. Instead, our proof builds on a technique of Furst, Saxe, and Sipser
31
. The approach was refined by
2
(see also
3
), who gave a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm that outputs an appropriate restriction supplied by the switching lemma. One of our contributions is to show that the correctness of the algorithm in
2
can be established within
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Combined with the probabilistic tools above and other ideas, this yields the
average-case
lower bound in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

As a consequence of our refined proof-theoretic framework, and with some additional effort, we can extract from the above formalization a
worst-case
lower bound within the weaker theory
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
.

Theorem 1.7

(Worst-Case
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
Lower Bound for
\oplus_{n}
in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
)
.

For all constants
k,d\geq 1
, there exists a constant
n_{0}\geq 1
such that
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
proves the following statement. For every
n\in\mathsf{Log}
,
n>n_{0}
, and
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}_{d}
circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
of size at most
n^{k}
, there exists a string
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
C(x)\neq\oplus_{n}(x)
.

Earlier formalizations of the worst-case parity lower bound for bounded-depth circuits required stronger theories. In particular,
(
69
)
and
(
58
)
formalize different proofs in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}=\mathsf{PV}_{1}+\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
, while
(
81
)
works in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
but in the
\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
regime – i.e., with
n
of doubly logarithmic order – so the proof can manipulate exponentially large objects (see
(
69
)
for details).

These formalizations reinforce the intuition that a substantial portion of results in algorithms and complexity theory are already captured within
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
or its mild extensions, and that establishing unprovability results would therefore be of considerable significance (see
(
74
)
for related discussions).

In
Section

1.3
below, we elaborate on the proofs of
Theorem

1.6
and
Theorem

1.7
. For further details about these and other formalizations, see
Section

4
.

1.2.4
Witnessing, Relative Strength of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, and Provability of
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}

We now discuss relations between theories
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
,
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, and
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, and connections to the
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}
versus
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
problem. We also introduce a new computational problem called
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
, and provide a tailored witnessing theorem for the
\forall\Sigma_{1}^{b}(\mathsf{PV})
-consequences of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
(i.e. provably total
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
problems in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
).

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
versus
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
.

By construction, every sentence provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
is also a theorem of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. It is also possible to show that if
\varphi
is a sentence in the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
(i.e., without the approximate counting symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
) provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, then it is provable in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
(see
Corollary

5.10
). This means that, modulo the difference in languages (i.e.
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
does not have the symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
),
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is a sub-theory of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
.
7
7

7

Indeed, there is a conservative extension of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
known as
\mathsf{HARD}^{\mathsf{A}}

46
that contains
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
in a stronger sense — the symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
can be simulated by a term in
\mathsf{HARD}^{\sf A}
such that all axioms governing
{\mathsf{P}}
are provable (see
Theorem

5.9
).

On the other hand, under plausible computational assumptions, there are sentences provable in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
that are not provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
(see
Corollary

5.14
).
8
8

8

More formally, there is a
\forall\Sigma^{b}_{2}(\mathsf{PV})
-sentence provable in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
that is not provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, under the existence of indistinguishability obfuscation and
{\mathsf{coNP}}
not contained infinitely often in
{\mathsf{NP}}_{/{\mathsf{poly}}}
(see
Corollary

5.14
).
This is obtained by adapting a technique from
37
. In other words,
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
is contained in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, while
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is likely strictly weaker than
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
.

Subsequently, a fundamental research direction is to determine whether
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is stronger than
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
, a question closely connected to the
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}
versus
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
problem and to understanding the role of randomness in feasible proofs.

\mathsf{PV}_{1}
versus
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
and feasible derandomization.

From a meta-mathematical standpoint, it is natural to ask whether
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
is
(
un
)
provable
in a weak arithmetic theory such as
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. A key obstacle is formalization: the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
is tailored to
deterministic
polynomial-time functions, whereas the statement
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
quantifies over acceptance probabilities of circuits on an exponentially large space. We propose the following question.

Open Problem 1
.

Is there a
\mathsf{PV}
function symbol
\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}(C,\Delta)
for which the
basic
,
boundary
,
precision consistency
, and
local consistency
axioms (
Section

1.2.1
) are provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
?

An unconditional positive answer seems out of reach at present, as it would immediately imply
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
by the soundness of the approximate counting axioms and the polynomial running time of
\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}
(see
Theorem

2.5
). Intuitively, this would amount to a
deterministic polynomial-time proof
of the collapse. At the moment, it is unclear whether a positive or a negative answer is more plausible.

A weaker possibility is that, even if no such
\mathsf{PV}
function symbol
\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}(C,\Delta)
exists with the axioms provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
, adding the approximate counting oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
might nonetheless be conservative for
deterministic
statements in the base language. Formally:

Open Problem 2
.

Is
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
conservative over
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
? Equivalently, does every first-order sentence in the language of
\mathsf{PV}
that is provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
already have a proof in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
?

A positive answer to
1
would imply a positive answer here. The relationship between
2
and
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
appears incomparable. If
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
holds but only via a non-feasible proof,
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
need not be conservative over
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. Conversely, even if
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is conservative over
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
, it is not clear to us whether
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
follows. At a high level, we are interested in the relationship between
derandomization of computations
and
derandomization of proofs
. While we are currently unable to provide definite answers, we believe these questions are fundamental and merit further study. We refer to
(
60
)
and references therein for related questions in the context of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
versus
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
.

A Witnessing Theorem for
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
: Reductions to
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
.

A key characteristic of bounded theories is to have a suitable
witnessing theorem
corresponding to certain computational problems (see, e.g.,
14
;
57
). We isolate a
certain (total) search problem as a key computational task for producing witnesses for the
\forall\Sigma^{b}_{1}(\mathsf{PV})
-consequences of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
:
An instance fixes the following parameters: input length
n
, multiset size
m
, predictor circuit description size
s
, and advantage
\delta>0
. The input is a
predictor generator
, i.e., a circuit

G\colon\{0,1\}^{nm}\to[n]\times\{0,1\}^{s}

which, on a flat distribution
\mathcal{D}\in(\{0,1\}^{n})^{m}
(i.e., an
m
-tuple of
n
-bit strings), returns an index
i\in[n]
and the description of a predictor circuit
P\colon\{0,1\}^{i-1}\to\{0,1\}
of size
s
.
A
solution
is any flat distribution
\mathcal{D}
such that, writing
(i,P)=G(\mathcal{D})
,

\Pr_{x\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}\!\big[P(x_{<i})=x_{i}\big]<\tfrac{1}{2}+\delta.

Thus a solution
\mathcal{D}

refutes
that
G
can produce predictors for any distribution with advantage
\delta
. When parameters satisfy
(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m\geq s+\lceil\log n\rceil+1
,
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})

lies in
\mathsf{TFZPP}
; in other words, uniformly random distribution is likely a solution. The problem is called
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
, as
G
is intended to output a predictor like the standard “distinguisher
\to
predictor” transformation of Yao
90
.

Remark 1.8

(
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
and Derandomization)
.

Note that
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
requires generating a distribution
\mathcal{D}
that is unpredictable with respect to a
given
predictor generator
G
– a deterministic procedure that attempts to produce a predictor
P
for
\mathcal{D}
. The distribution
\mathcal{D}
need not be pseudorandom (or equivalently, unpredictable) against
all small circuits
; it only needs to fool the specific generator
G
. This can be viewed as a special case of constructing
targeted pseudorandom generators
, a task known to be
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}
-complete (see
33
;
23
;
64
).

We establish the following result for the provably total
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
problems of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

Theorem 1.9

(Witnessing for
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
)
.

Let
\varphi(x,y)
be a quantifier-free formula in the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. If
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}\vdash\forall x~\exists y~\varphi(x,y)
, there exists a deterministic polynomial-time Turing reduction from the search problem defined by
\varphi
to
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
with parameters satisfying
(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m\geq s+\lceil\log n\rceil+1
.

In
Section

1.3
below, we provide an overview of the proof of
Theorem

1.9
.

Relation to
\mathsf{LossyCode}
and
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
.

Recall the definition of the search problem
\mathsf{LossyCode}

55
: given a compressor circuit
C\colon\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{n-1}
and a decompressor circuit
D\colon\{0,1\}^{n-1}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
, output
x
with
D(C(x))\neq x
. Similarly to
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
, this problem is total and in
\mathsf{TFZPP}
.

We observe the existence of a deterministic polynomial-time mapping reduction from
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
to
\mathsf{LossyCode}
whenever the input instances of
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
satisfy

(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m\;\geq\;s+\lceil\log n\rceil+1.

Therefore, in the stated regime, derandomizing
\mathsf{LossyCode}
subsumes derandomizing
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
. Since every
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
-provably total
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
problem reduces to
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
, under the above parameter condition it further reduces to
\mathsf{LossyCode}
.

Recall that Wilkie (unpublished) and Thapen
(
85
)
(see
(
43
, Proposition 1.14)
and
(
64
, Theorem D.1)
) proved that
\mathsf{LossyCode}
captures the
\forall\Sigma_{1}^{b}
-fragment of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
. Consequently, these results organize the
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
landscapes of the two theories:
\mathsf{LossyCode}
witnesses the
\forall\Sigma^{b}_{1}
-consequences of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, while
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
witnesses those of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

We return to these topics in
Section

5
, providing detailed proofs of all results mentioned above and further discussions.

1.2.5
Reverse Mathematics of Randomized and Average-Case Lower Bounds

The
retraction weak pigeonhole principle
(
\mathsf{rWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
)
47
;
66
;
19
is one of the most important combinatorial principles known to be provable in

\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, but whose provability in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}

remains unclear. Recall that
\mathsf{rWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
asserts that for every
n,m\in\mathsf{Log}
with
m<n
and for all (deterministic) circuits
C\colon\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{m}
(“compressor”) and
D\colon\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
(“decompressor”), there is
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
D(C(x))\neq x
.

In other words,
\mathsf{rWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
captures the combinatorial principle underlying the total search problem
\mathsf{LossyCode}
discussed above. Its provability in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
would mean that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
and
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
prove the same
\forall\Sigma^{b}_{1}(\mathsf{PV})
sentences, and by the witnessing theorem (see
Theorem

1.9
), this would further imply that
\mathsf{LossyCode}
and
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
are equivalent with respect to deterministic polynomial-time Turing reductions.

We study
counting variants
of the retraction weak pigeonhole principle and characterize their
equivalence class
with respect to provability in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. We show that this class encompasses certain
communication complexity lower bounds against randomized protocols
, establishing that these results are all equivalent (over the base theory
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
) to suitable variants of the retraction pigeonhole principle.

Counting Variants of
\mathsf{rWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
.

We consider the following statements:

•

Approximate Counting
\mathsf{rWPHP}
:
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[m,\varepsilon]
.

For any
deterministic
compressor-decompressor pair with encoding length
m<n
, an
\varepsilon
-fraction of inputs cannot be correctly decompressed.

•

Randomized Compression
\mathsf{rWPHP}
:
\mathsf{rrWPHP}[m,\varepsilon]
.

For a
randomized
compressor and a deterministic decompressor with encoding length
m<n
, there is some input on which the pair has error probability at least
\varepsilon
.

These principles are formalized in a natural way using the probabilistic framework provided by
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

One-Way Communication Complexity.

We prove an equivalence result involving communication complexity (CC) lower bounds against randomized
one-way protocols
with either
public randomness
or
private randomness
. Recall that the Set Disjointness function
\mathsf{SetDisj}(x,y)
outputs
1
if and only if for every index
i\in[n]
, either
x_{i}=0
or
y_{i}=0
, i.e.,
x
and
y
have no common
1
-index. The following statements, presented informally for clarity, are relevant to our result:

•

Public Randomized CC Lower Bound for Set Disjointness
:
\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[m,\varepsilon]
.

Every public-coin one-way protocol computing
\mathsf{SetDisj}
with communication complexity
m
must have error probability at least
\varepsilon
on some input pair
(x,y)
.

•

Private Randomized CC Lower Bound for Set Disjointness
:
\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[m,\varepsilon]
.

Every private-coin one-way protocol computing
\mathsf{SetDisj}
with communication complexity
m
must have error probability at least
\varepsilon
on some input pair
(x,y)
.

•

Public Randomized CC Lower Bound for Some Function
:
\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[m,\varepsilon]
.

For every
n\in\mathsf{Log}
, there exists
f:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
such that
\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{f}[m,\varepsilon]
holds.

•

Private Randomized CC Lower Bound for Some Function
:
\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[m,\varepsilon]
.

For every
n\in\mathsf{Log}
, there exists
f:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
such that
\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{f}[m,\varepsilon]
holds.

We leave the details about the formalization of the corresponding lower bound sentences to
Section

6
. We note that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is able to show that some concrete functions admit low-cost communication protocols. For instance, using linear hashing, it proves that
\mathsf{Equality}
admits public-randomness one-way communication protocols of cost
O(\log n)
.

We can state an informal version of our equivalence result as follows.
9
9

9

In particular, the simplified formulation given here omits considerations about the number of random bits employed in the randomized protocols, which plays a role in the parameters of some statements.

Theorem 1.10

(Main Equivalence Result (Informal); see
Theorem

6.7
)
.

The following statements are equivalent over
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
, for suitable relations between the constants
k\geq 1
and
0<\varepsilon<1
, quantified outside the theory:

(1)

\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k}]

(2)

\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon},n^{-k}]

(3)

\mathsf{rrWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k}]

(4)

\mathsf{rrWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon},n^{-k}]

(5)

\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n-1,n^{-k}]

(6)

\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n^{\varepsilon},n^{-k}]

(7)

\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n-1,n^{-k}]

(8)

\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n^{\varepsilon},n^{-k}]

(9)

\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[n-1,n^{-k}]

(10)

\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[n^{\varepsilon},n^{-k}]

(11)

\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[n-1,n^{-k}]

(12)

\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[n^{\varepsilon},n^{-k}]

As a consequence, one of these statements is provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
if and only if every statement in
Theorem

1.10
is provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. This result provides evidence that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
can serve as a suitable base theory for developing the reverse mathematics of average-case and randomized lower bounds.

For more details and additional discussion, we refer to
Section

6
.

1.3
Techniques

We next outline some of the main techniques used in our proofs, starting with a recurring argument that establishes basic probabilistic inequalities in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

1.3.1
Probabilistic Reasoning in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
: The “Pointwise to Global” Technique (
Section

1.2.2
)

At the core of our probabilistic reasoning is a simple but powerful seed-fixing lemma that lets us pass from
global
inequalities to
pointwise
statements about suitably chosen restrictions of the randomness. Recall that a feasible random variable
X
is specified by an explicit support
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
, a seed length
n
, and a multi-output circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to V
. Its approximate expectation is

\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]\triangleq\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{v}),

where
C_{v}
is the indicator circuit for the event
C(x)=v
. This is a
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
-computable quantity.

A general averaging argument for expectation (
Theorem

3.17
).
The following holds in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}
be random variables over the same seed with support
V
, and fix coefficients
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}\in\mathbb{Q}
. Write

\mu\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\,\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}],\qquad\mu\!\upharpoonright z\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\,\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}\mid z],

where
X_{i}\mid z
denotes
X_{i}
after fixing a suffix of the seed to
z
. Then, for every desired suffix length
k
, there exists
z\in\{0,1\}^{k}
such that

\mu\!\upharpoonright z\;\geq\;\mu\;-\;(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|\lambda\|_{1}\cdot\|V\|_{1}.

Thus a lower bound on
\mu
can be
witnessed
(up to controlled additive slack) by conditioning on a partial assignment of the seed.

The proof iteratively fixes one seed bit at a time. By a form of Local Consistency for expectation, the average of
\mu\!\upharpoonright(0\circ z)
and
\mu\!\upharpoonright(1\circ z)
is close to
\mu\!\upharpoonright z
; hence it is possible to prove that one of the two extensions preserves the current value up to an additive loss
O(\eta\cdot\|\lambda\|_{1}\cdot\|V\|_{1})
, where
\eta
is an auxiliary parameter in the proof. Greedily repeating this for
k
steps yields a
k
-bit suffix with total loss
O(k\cdot\eta\cdot\|\lambda\|_{1}\|V\|_{1})
. A precision-smoothing argument (switching from
\eta
to
\delta
via precision consistency) then gives the stated
(2\delta+\beta)
-type bound, independent of
k
. The greedy construction is formally captured by a
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
-procedure
\mathsf{AvgSampler}
. Conceptually,
\mathsf{AvgSampler}
searches for a good suffix using calls to the approximate counting oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
, and its correctness is established using (polynomial) induction on
k
over a quantifier-free
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
-formula, which is available in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

Remark 1.11

(Example: Consistency of Complementation (
Corollary

3.18
).)
.

Let
E_{1},E_{2}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be complementary predicates, i.e.,
E_{1}=\neg E_{2}
as Boolean circuits. Let
X_{1},X_{2}
be their indicator variables. We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Given an arbitrary
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, we set
\eta\triangleq\beta/C
, for a large enough constant
C
. Pointwise, for
every full assignment

\rho
to the seed, using the relation between
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
we have

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}\mid\rho]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}\mid\rho]-1\;=\;0.

Set
\lambda\triangleq(1,1,-1)
and consider
\mu\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]-1
. Applying
Theorem

3.17
with
k=n
, we obtain a
\rho
such that
\mu\leq\mu\!\upharpoonright\rho+O(\eta)
. But
\mu\!\upharpoonright\rho
is exactly
0
by the pointwise identity above, yielding
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]-1=O(\eta)
. Similarly, one can show that
-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]+1=O(\eta)
. Translating expectations back to probabilities via the indicator correspondence, and applying a standard precision-smoothing argument, one can conclude that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}
is consistent with complementation, i.e.,

\big|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(E_{1})+{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(E_{2})-1\big|\;\leq\;2\delta+\beta.

To summarize,
Theorem

3.17
provides a way to fix randomness while preserving lower bounds on linear combinations of expectations. It turns equalities or inequalities that hold
for each seed
into quantitative global bounds in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
with explicit additive slack depending only on
\delta,\beta
and the natural
\ell_{1}
norms of the supports and coefficients. This mechanism is the engine that drives many of our probability inequalities and applications in
Section

3
.

Remark 1.12
.

The bit-by-bit fixing trick is a standard technique in computational complexity theory. For instance, it is used in the search-to-decision reduction for
{\mathsf{SAT}}

(
6
, Section 2.5)
and to derive circuit lower bounds from derandomization
1
, among other results. In particular, our approach is inspired by a new proof of
{\mathsf{BPP}}\subseteq{\mathsf{MA}}\subseteq\Sigma_{2}^{p}
via a bit-by-bit “dueling argument”
(
64
, Lemma A.10)
.

1.3.2
Provability of Circuit Lower Bounds (
Theorem

1.6
and
1.7
)

Theorem

1.6
gives an
average-case
lower bound for the parity function against depth-
d

{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
circuits, formalized in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, while
Theorem

1.7
gives a corresponding
worst-case
lower bound, formalized in the weaker theory
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. As alluded to above, the challenge is to avoid encoding-based arguments that rely on pigeonhole principles (or frameworks that build on them), since they are unavailable in these theories. The first result showcases how
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
approximate-probability calculus supports average-case arguments in a more sophisticated setting, while the second shows that, with additional derandomization work, we can carry a corresponding worst-case lower bound argument entirely within
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
.

At a high level, the formalizations implement a simplification and derandomization
2
of the Furst–Saxe–Sipser
(
31
)

random restriction approach
to
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
lower bounds. Recall that the argument proceeds in stages, where at each stage we fix a suitable partial restriction
\rho\colon[n]\to\{0,1,*\}
that sets all input variables in
T\triangleq\rho^{-1}(\{0,1\})\subseteq[n]
. The crucial point is that a depth-
d
circuit
C

simplifies
when restricted by
\rho
, leading to a not much larger circuit
C\!\upharpoonright\rho
of depth
d-1
, while the parity function
retains its hardness
.

Two central lemmas employed in the specification of
\rho
drive the proofs of
Theorem

1.6
and
Theorem

1.7
.

Subset Selection Lemma (
Lemma

4.8
).
The first step is to algorithmically choose the set
T\subseteq[n]
of variables with a “
narrow-or-wide
” guarantee: for every bounded-width CNF/DNF
F
at the bottom of the circuit
C
, after fixing the variables in
T
,
F
either already depends on few literals (narrow) or contains many disjoint subclauses supported on
T
(wide). Crucially, this subset selection is
constructed and proved correct
in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
using a delicate potential-function argument that simulates the method of derandomization via conditional expectations. This makes the selection of the subset
T\subseteq[n]
feasible in our theories, not merely existential.

Restriction Selection Lemmas (
Lemma

4.9
and
4.10
).
Given the narrow-or-wide structure exposed by the subset selection step, we then
choose values for variables in
T
so that all relevant gates in the circuit
C
simultaneously simplify after applying the resulting restriction
\rho
. There are two versions, matching our two theorems:

•

In the
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
setting of
Theorem

1.6
, we consider a
random assignment of bits
(
Lemma

4.9
) and then fix a good selection of the values via
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
’s “pointwise-to-global”
averaging argument for expectation
explained above. This lets us form a partial restriction
\rho
and obtain a corresponding circuit
C\!\upharpoonright\rho
that approximately retains the relative advantage of
C
when computing parity.

•

In the more constrained
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
setting of
Theorem

1.7
, we
derandomize
the same choice (
Lemma

4.10
). Again, this is implemented by a potential argument that feasibly simulates the method of conditional expectations within
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. A crucial aspect of the proof that facilitates the formalization is that the relevant expectations depend on at most
O(\log n)
input coordinates and thus can be efficiently computed by
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
terms.

In both settings, the circuit lower bound is obtained by an inductive application of the restriction technique, as in the standard proof of the result. The details appear in
Section

4.4
.

The novelty is not in the combinatorics of
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
versus Parity but in the underlying
proof-theoretic framework
.
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
supplies a minimal yet sufficient probabilistic infrastructure that lets us carry out the average-case lower bound argument internally. In our proofs, this lets us define and reason about the agreement tester
T_{C}
in the statement of
Theorem

1.6
, define and analyze appropriate events, quantify advantage and pass from randomized restrictions to concrete choices, and keep track of the small additive losses accumulated across iterations — all within
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

Moreover, this streamlined setup and the perspective it provides clarify the boundary with the weaker theory
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. The
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
framework and our formalization isolate exactly where probabilistic reasoning is used and where the argument is purely combinatorial. This separation indicates which components can be replaced by deterministic potential-based arguments available in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
, thereby guiding the adaptation that yields our worst-case formalization in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
.

1.3.3
The Witnessing Theorem (
Theorem

1.9
)

Theorem

1.9
states that every
\forall\Sigma^{b}_{1}(\mathsf{PV})
-sentence provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
admits a deterministic polynomial-time Turing reduction to the total search problem
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
, with parameters obeying
(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m\geq s+\lceil\log n\rceil+1
. Recall that an instance of
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
gives a
predictor generator

G
and asks for a flat distribution
\mathcal{D}
such that the predictor
(i,P)=G(\mathcal{D})
fails to predict the
i
-th bit of
\mathcal{D}
with advantage
\delta
. (For the stated parameter range,
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
is in
\mathsf{TFZPP}
and map-reduces to
\mathsf{LossyCode}
.)

Suppose that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}\vdash\forall x\,\exists y\,\varphi(x,y)
, where
\varphi
is a quantifier-free
\mathsf{PV}
-formula. Given
x
of length
n
, we describe a predictor generator
G_{x}
such that a solution
\mathcal{D}
to
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
over
G_{x}
allows us to compute
y
such that
\varphi(x,y)
holds.

Starting from an
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
-proof of
\forall x\,\exists y\,\varphi(x,y)
, we first apply Herbrand’s theorem over the universal axiomatization of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
to obtain finitely many
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
-terms
t_{1},\dots,t_{c}
such that
\bigvee_{i}\varphi(x,t_{i}(x))
holds, and encode this disjunction by a single term
t_{\varphi}
with the equational core
{\mathsf{APX}}
proving
t_{\varphi}(x,t_{1}(x),\ldots,t_{c}(x))=1
. In standard models (
Section

1.2.1
), these terms are polynomial-time oracle algorithms for the approximate-counting oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
.

Next, we describe the construction of
G_{x}
. Given a candidate flat distribution
\mathcal{D}
(say, over
n^{\prime}
-bit strings and of support size
m^{\prime}
, where
n^{\prime},m^{\prime}={\mathsf{poly}}(n)
are large enough), we
simulate
each oracle call
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
inside any
t_{i}
by
empirical counting on
\mathcal{D}
:

{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)\triangleq\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}\big[C(u_{\leq\ell})\big],

where
C\colon\{0,1\}^{\ell}\to\{0,1\}
and
\ell\leq n^{\prime}
. Let
t_{i}^{\mathcal{D}}(x)
and
t_{\varphi}^{\mathcal{D}}(x,\cdot)
denote the resulting outputs. Note that these can be computed in
deterministic
polynomial time, since
\mathcal{D}
is explicitly given as a collection of
{\mathsf{poly}}(n)
strings of length
{\mathsf{poly}}(n)
, and
t_{\varphi}
,
t_{1}
,
\ldots
,
t_{c}
run in polynomial time.

Predictor Extraction Lemma (
Lemma

5.6
).
The key technical step says: if under this simulation the
{\mathsf{APX}}
-provable equation fails, i.e.,
t_{\varphi}^{\mathcal{D}}(x,\cdot)\neq 1
, then we can algorithmically extract a small predictor
P
of size
s^{\prime}
that achieves advantage at least
\delta^{\prime}
for an explicitly computed bit position of
\mathcal{D}
, where
(\delta^{\prime 2}/10)\cdot m^{\prime}\geq s^{\prime}+\lceil\log n^{\prime}\rceil+1
.

Conceptually, an
{\mathsf{APX}}
proof asserts: for
every
interpretation of
{\mathsf{P}}
, either the equation holds or one of the approximate-counting axioms (
Basic
,
Boundary
,
Precision Consistency
,
Local Consistency
) is violated. Under our empirical interpretation, the first three axioms continue to hold, so any failure must exhibit a
Local Consistency
violation of the form

\Big|\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{\leq\ell})]-\tfrac{1}{2}\!\left(\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<\ell}0)]+\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<\ell}1)]\right)\Big|\;>\;\tfrac{2}{|\Delta|}+\tfrac{1}{|B|},

for a circuit
C
and strings
\Delta,B
produced in the
{\mathsf{APX}}
proof.

Similarly to the analysis of Yao’s distinguisher-to-predictor lemma, such a gap yields a predictor for the next bit via a deterministic transformation of
C
; here the “signal” comes not from distinguishing
\mathcal{D}
from uniform, but from detecting a
local inconsistency
of empirical counts across bit-fixings. Thus predictors arise not only from distinguishers, but also from the ability to spot local inconsistencies when
\mathcal{D}
is used as a random source for approximate counting -- a viewpoint that might be of independent interest.
10
10

10

In particular, Yao’s distinguisher to predictor transformation requires
randomness
(unless we have
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
)
64
. The construction of predictors from local inconsistency, however, is deterministic.

Formally, the lemma is established by a
proof-theoretic analysis
, proceeding by induction on the steps of the
{\mathsf{APX}}
proof and tracking the parameters
(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},s^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
through the final rule used.

Wrapping up the argument, for an input
x
, the reduction outputs the predictor generator
G_{x}
obtained from
Lemma

5.6
. Note that any solution
\mathcal{D}
to the resulting
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
instance
cannot
trigger a successful predictor extraction, since by definition
G_{x}
fails to produce a predictor on
\mathcal{D}
. Hence given a solution
\mathcal{D}
to this instance of
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
, it must make the simulated identity true, i.e.,

t_{\varphi}(x,t^{\mathcal{D}}_{1}(x),\ldots,t^{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(x))=1.

In other words, some
t_{i}^{D}(x)
is a valid witness
y
for
\varphi(x,y)
. Finally, as observed above, because
\mathcal{D}
is explicit, all simulated calls and
t_{i}^{\mathcal{D}}(x)
are computable in deterministic polynomial time.

This completes the sketch of the proof of
Theorem

1.9
. For the details, see
Section

5
.

1.4
Related Work

Below we provide a representative, though not exhaustive, list of related developments and references.

Probabilistic arguments in bounded arithmetic.

Paris, Wilkie, and Woods
(
75
)
(see also Pudlák
(
80
)
) observed that many probabilistic arguments can be formalized using variants of the weak pigeonhole principle rather than exact counting. An early explicit link between the weak pigeonhole principle and randomized algorithms is due to Wilkie (cf.
(
58
)
), who showed that randomized polynomial-time algorithms witness all
\forall\Sigma^{b}_{1}
-consequences of
{\mathsf{S}}^{1}_{2}+\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
.

Ojakian
(
73
)
undertakes a general study of how probabilistic methods from combinatorics can be formalized in bounded arithmetic. While such proofs can often be recast as purely counting-based arguments, the naive translation still leaves exponentially many objects to count. The central idea, again, is to use the weak pigeonhole principle to simulate the probabilistic counting argument and thereby avoid this blow-up. The formalizations are carried out in
{\mathsf{S}}^{1}_{2}
augmented with suitable variants of the pigeonhole principle.

Jeřábek
(
43
)
showed that within
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
one can compare the sizes of two bounded
{\mathsf{P}}/{\mathsf{poly}}
-definable sets by constructing a surjection from one onto the other; he used this to formalize descriptions of algorithms in
{\mathsf{ZPP}}
and
{\mathsf{RP}}
. He further showed
(
43
;
44
)
that
\mathsf{APC}_{1}=\mathsf{PV}_{1}+\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
is strong enough to formalize sophisticated derandomization results. In
(
46
)
, Jeřábek developed a more systematic framework, showing in particular that for any bounded
{\mathsf{P}}/{\mathsf{poly}}
-definable set,
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
proves that a suitable pair of surjective counting functions exists that approximates its cardinality up to a polynomially small error. (The notation
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
follows the terminology of
(
13
)
.)

Built on Jeřábek’s framework, Lê
62
formalizes more results in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
and its extensions, including randomized matching algorithms, the Lovász Local Lemma, and the Goldreich-Levin theorem. Throughout these formalizations, Lê provides formulations of concepts in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
such as
expectation
,
Markov inequalities
, and
pairwise-independence
, which we also consider in this project. The formalization of random variables and expectation in
62
heavily relies on the machinery of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
and is thus inadequate for our purposes.

Remark 1.13
.

A concrete open problem is to state and prove a stronger form of the Chernoff bound in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. In this work, we show that the Chernoff bound with
O(\log n)
variables (i.e., strings of length
n
are considered feasible) can be formalized in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. It is unclear whether we could state a clean and meaningful Chernoff bound with
O(n)
variables: the error probability will be exponentially small, which could be much smaller than the approximate counting error of the function
{\mathsf{P}}
. Moreover, even if a meaningful formalization exists, it is unclear whether existing proofs of the Chernoff bound can be formalized in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Note that a strong form of Chernoff bound with
O(n)
variables can be formalized in
Jeřábek
’s theory
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
(see
(
46
, Proposition 2.18)
).

The proof complexity of
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
.

There is evidence that the pigeonhole-based axioms used throughout these frameworks exceed what can be proved in purely polynomial-time theories: while
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
is available in
\mathsf{T}^{2}_{2}
, relativized variants are unprovable already in
{\mathsf{S}}^{2}_{2}

(
83
)
. As noted above, under cryptographic assumptions,
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
does not prove
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})

(
37
)
. This supports the common stance that
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
is too weak to derive the
\mathsf{WPHP}
-style principles exploited in the above formalizations. For a comprehensive investigation of
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
and its provability in bounded arithmetic, see
(
60
)
.

Jeřábek’s approximate-counting toolbox includes general principles such as inclusion-exclusion and strong Chernoff-type estimates, all formalized inside
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
. By contrast, the development of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
deliberately starts from weaker primitives: while we recover Markov/Chebyshev-style reasoning, error reduction, and other basic probabilistic tools, we do not reprove all of Jeřábek’s strongest concentration bounds here. It remains an interesting direction to test the limits of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
: which stronger probabilistic inequalities (e.g., full-strength Chernoff) are intrinsically beyond its axioms?

Beyond approximate counting with additive error.

Certain combinatorial proofs—e.g., of Ramsey’s theorem—typically require counting
sparse sets
, which is unavailable both in our framework and in Jeřábek’s theory
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
. In our setting, for
X\subseteq\{0,1\}^{n}
we can estimate
|X|
only to within an additive error that is a polynomial fraction of
2^{n}
, whereas these arguments require accuracy within a polynomial fraction of
|X|
. Such counting becomes possible in theories stronger than
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, as developed in
(
48
)
.

In a concurrent work, Thapen
86
introduces a framework to formulate stronger complexity classes (such as
\oplus{\mathsf{P}}
and
\#{\mathsf{P}}
) in the theory of
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
in a way that is similar in spirit to our axiomatization of approximate counting. For instance, given an oracle that is intended to compute
\oplus{\mathsf{P}}
, Thapen considered the relativized
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
problem that searches for a “local inconsistency” of the oracle. Note that the
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
framework in
86
considers only
query complexity
, while we additionally consider proofs in bounded arithmetic. Nevertheless, it is conceivable that results in these two directions may have analogues in each framework given the similarity in the setup.

Theories with explicit counting.

In
(
44
, Chapter 6)
, Jeřábek studies bounded theories with explicit counting, revisiting the Impagliazzo-Kapron
(
39
)
second-order logic for formalizing cryptographic reasoning. The logic is multi-sorted: first-order variables range over strings, while
second-order variables of sort
k>0
range over
k
-ary (intended polynomial-time) functions. In particular, functions are second-order objects rather than function symbols in the language. The theory includes recursive counting constructs for expressing the sizes of definable bounded sets.

The same chapter also introduces a feasible theory of approximate counting using a 3-valued semantics based on Kleene’s logic, equipped with an LPF-style implication to support induction-like reasoning. Counting is approximate: the semantics distinguishes between having many versus few solutions (while using 3-valued logic to allow an explicit indeterminate region). The resulting “
\Sigma^{c}_{1}
-consequences” admit probabilistic polynomial-time witnessing (see
(
44
, Theorem 6.2.20)
).

The counting framework in these theories is considerably different from ours, relying on exact
counting terms or approximate counting quantifiers in different logical settings. We refer to
these references for details.

Bounded reverse mathematics.

Cook and Nguyen
(
28
)
provide a thorough exposition of the bounded reverse mathematics program, systematically developing theories of bounded arithmetic and presenting formalizations of key combinatorial and algorithmic results, with the goal of identifying the weakest axioms sufficient to prove them.

Finally, we refer to
(
62
;
77
;
69
;
9
)
and references therein for numerous examples of results from theoretical computer science that can be formalized in bounded arithmetic. It would be interesting to further investigate which of these formalizations can be carried out in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

Acknowledgements.
We would like to thank Jan Krajíček for discussions related to the
\forall\Sigma^{b}_{1}
-conservativity of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
over
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
and for bringing some references to our attention. We also thank Dimitrios Tsintsilidas for comments on an earlier version of the paper. We would also like to thank Surya Mathialagan, Shuo Pang, and Hanlin Ren for helpful discussions. Finally, we thank the anonymous STOC reviewers for useful comments about the presentation.

2
Formal Definition of the Theory

In this section, we formally define the equational theory
\mathsf{APX}
and its first-order counterpart
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. We assume basic familiarity with Cook’s Theory
\mathsf{PV}

29
. The necessary background can be found in
(
58
, Chapter 12)
,
(
59
, Chapter 12)
, and
(
65
)
.

2.1
Notation

Base Theory.

Let
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
be the theory
\mathsf{PV}
relative to a fresh function symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
with the axiom

\mathsf{ITR}({\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta),C\sm\Delta\sm\Delta)=\varepsilon

that bounds the output length of the function symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
. Intuitively, the axiom means that the output length of
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
given strings
C
and
\Delta
as its input is at most
|C|\cdot|\Delta|^{2}
. This axiom ensures that
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
-terms are feasible functions in the standard model. Interested readers are referred to
43
;
46
;
47
for more examples of relativized
\mathsf{PV}
.

Slightly different from Cook’s original notation, we will define
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
with constant symbol
\varepsilon
(rather than
0
) and replace the initial functions
s_{1}(x)
and
s_{2}(x)
by
s_{1}(x)
and
s_{0}(x)
, respectively. Other functions
\mathsf{TR}
,
\mathsf{ITR}
,
\circ
, and
\#
are defined as in Cook’s original definition. Let
\hat{\mathsf{P}}
be a function over Boolean strings. The standard model of
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
with respect to
\hat{\mathsf{P}}
, denoted by
\mathbb{M}(\hat{\mathsf{P}})
, is defined as follows:

•

The universe consists of all Boolean strings of finite length.

•

The constant symbol
\varepsilon
is interpreted as the empty string.

•

s_{b}(x)
(for
b\in\{0,1\}
) is interpreted as the function that appends
b
to the right of the string
x
.

•

\mathsf{TR}(x)
is interpreted as the function that trims the rightmost bit of
x
;
\mathsf{ITR}(x,y)
is interpreted as the function that trims
x
for
|y|
times.

•

\circ
is interpreted as string concatenation, while
\#(x,y)
is interpreted as the function that concatenates
|y|
copies of
x
.

•

The function symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
is interpreted as
\hat{\mathsf{P}}
.

A function introduced by one of the rules in
\mathsf{PV}
(i.e. introduction by terms or introduction by limited recursion on notation) is interpreted as the unique function over the universe that satisfies its introduction rule.

Circuits.

We define a few
\mathsf{PV}
functions that manipulate Boolean circuits. Let
\mathsf{IsCkt}(C,z)
be the
\mathsf{PV}
function that outputs
1
if
C
is a circuit with input length
|z|
, and outputs
0
otherwise;
\mathsf{IsConst}(C)
be the
\mathsf{PV}
function that outputs
1
if
C
is a circuit that does not read its input (i.e., there is no path from the output gate to an input variable);
\mathsf{Bool}(C)
outputs
1
if
\mathsf{IsConst}(C)
and
C
outputs
1
and outputs
0
if
\mathsf{IsConst}(C)
and
C
outputs
0
(otherwise, outputs, e.g.,
\varepsilon
);
\mathsf{Fix}_{b}(C)
be the function that, given a circuit
C
, output the circuit obtained from
C
by fixing the rightmost input bit to be
b\in\{0,1\}
;
\mathsf{Eval}(C,x)
be the function that evaluate the circuit
C
on the input
x
. One may think of any straightforward implementations of these functions in
\mathsf{PV}
as
\mathsf{PV}
is a robust theory.

For simplicity, we use the following abbreviations:

•

For
n\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C\in B_{n}
denotes
\mathsf{IsCkt}(C,1^{n})
, i.e.,
C
is a circuit with
n
input bits. Moreover,
\forall C\in B_{n}~\varphi(C)
denotes
\forall C~(\mathsf{IsCkt}(C,1^{n})\to\varphi(C))
and
\exists C\in B_{n}~\varphi(C)
denotes
\exists C~(\mathsf{IsCkt}(C,1^{n})\land\varphi(C))
.

•

For a circuit
C
,
C(x)
denotes
\mathsf{Eval}(C,x)
.

•

We use
\mathsf{Null}_{n}
