to denote the circuit with
n
input bits that does not read its input bits and outputs
0
, and
\mathsf{True}_{n}
to denote the circuit with
n
input bits that does not read its input bits and outputs
1
.

\mathsf{PV}
-Terms and Functions.

We say that a
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
-term is a
\mathsf{PV}
-term if its construction indicates that it does not call the
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle. Formally, the set of
\mathsf{PV}
-terms is the minimum set that contains all base functions and is close under composition and the function formulation rules in
\mathsf{PV}
, that is:

•

Base functions
s_{i}(x)
,
\mathsf{TR}(x)
,
\mathsf{ITR}(x)
,
\circ(x,y)
are
\mathsf{PV}
-terms.

•

If
t
is a
\mathsf{PV}
-term, the function
f_{t}
introduced with the defining axiom
f_{t}=t
is also a
\mathsf{PV}
term.

•

A term formulated from
\mathsf{PV}
-terms by composition is a
\mathsf{PV}
term.

•

If
g,h_{0},h_{1},k_{0},k_{1}
are
\mathsf{PV}
-terms, the function
f_{\Pi}
constructed by limited recursion on notation from
\Pi=(g,h_{0},h_{1},k_{0},k_{1})
is a
\mathsf{PV}
term.

We say that a function symbol
f
is a
\mathsf{PV}
-function if it is a
\mathsf{PV}
-term.

By the Cook-Levin theorem (see
79
for a formalization in
\mathsf{PV}
),
\mathsf{PV}
terms can be converted into polynomial-size Boolean circuits on any given input length
n\in\mathsf{Log}
, and the correctness can be proved in
\mathsf{PV}
. Similarly,
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
terms can be converted into polynomial-size
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle circuits on any given input length
n\in\mathsf{Log}
with
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
-provable correctness.

Encoding Conventions and Arithmetic Operations.

For functions and multi-output circuits, we will treat
\varepsilon
as
false
and any other value as
true
when we define the acceptance probability of the circuit. Let
\mathsf{Bool}(x)
be the
\mathsf{PV}
function that outputs
0
if
x
is
\varepsilon
and outputs
1
otherwise.

We assume that natural numbers are encoded in binary in a straightforward way. For instance, one can encode a natural number in dyadic notation as in
29
so that basic arithmetic operations such as addition, multiplication, and comparison can be defined naturally. We assume that the encoding can be verified efficiently, i.e., there is a
\mathsf{PV}
function symbol
\mathsf{IsNumber}(x)
that outputs
1
if
x
is the encoding of a natural number, and outputs
0
otherwise, and use
[x\in\mathbb{N}]
as the shorthand of
\mathsf{IsNumber}(x)
. We use
[x]_{\mathbb{N}}
to denote the natural number encoded by
x
when we want to be explicit about the interpretation of
x
as a natural number.

Elementary arithmetic operations, such as addition, multiplication, and comparison, can be defined naturally. Moreover, basic properties of the
\mathsf{PV}
function symbols representing these operations can be established in
\mathsf{PV}
(whenever the operations involve a feasible number of elements).

We specify a standard encoding of rational numbers in
\mathsf{PV}
: We use the pair
(x,y)
to denote the rational number

\sum_{i=1}^{|x|}x_{i}2^{i-1}+\sum_{j=1}^{|y|}y_{j}2^{-j}.

Similarly to the encoding of natural numbers, we assume a
\mathsf{PV}
function symbol
\mathsf{IsRational}(x)
that tests whether
x
encodes a rational number, and we use
[x\in\mathbb{Q}]
as shorthand for
\mathsf{IsRational}(x)
. We write
[x]_{\mathbb{Q}}
to denote the rational number encoded by
x
. We might directly treat
x
as a rational number if this is clear from the context.

Data Structures and Explicit Sets.

We assume a straightforward encoding of
explicit
sets (and multisets), i.e., sets of feasible size, that supports operations such as selection, union, intersection, and membership query. An explicit set
S
may be encoded as a list containing all the elements in it. Note that this is different from the
feasibly definable
sets in
43
, which may be of infeasible size. When discussing explicit sets, we use
|S|
to denote the size of
S
, i.e., the number of elements contained in
S
. When
S=\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\}
is a set of rational numbers, we use
||S||=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}|q_{i}|
to denote its
\ell_{1}
-norm, i.e., the sum of the absolute values of the elements in
S
.
11
11

11

While we abuse notation and employ
|\cdot|
to denote both length and absolute value, the meaning will be clear in each context.

Moreover, for an explicit set
S
and a quantifier-free formula
\varphi(x)
in the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
, we can define the universal quantification over
S
, denoted by
\forall x\in S:\varphi(x)
, as a
quantifier-free
formula in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
that is true if and only if every element
x\in S
satisfies
\varphi(x)
(in the standard model). This is possible as
S
is explicitly encoded, and thus there is a straightforward feasible algorithm that given the encoding of
S
, enumerates
S
and checks whether there is an
x\in S
such that
\varphi(x)
is false. Similarly, we can define the existential quantification over
S
, denoted by
\exists x\in S:\varphi(x)
. All relevant deduction rules about quantification over sets should be admissible in
\mathsf{PV}
assuming standard encoding, e.g.,

\displaystyle(\exists_{i}):\quad\frac{\Gamma\vdash\varphi[y/t]\quad\Gamma\vdash t\in S}{\Gamma\vdash\exists y\in S:\varphi}

(2.1)

\displaystyle(\exists_{e}):\quad\frac{\Gamma\vdash\exists y\in S:\varphi\quad\Gamma,z\in S,\varphi[y/z]\vdash\psi}{\Gamma\vdash\psi}

(2.2)

where in
(\exists_{i})

t
is an arbitrary term, and in
(\exists_{e})

z
must be a fresh variable that has no occurrence in
\Gamma,\varphi,\psi,y
. This ensures that most natural mathematical proofs regarding explicit sets can be easily formalized in
\mathsf{PV}
; see
(
65
, Chapter 4)
for more discussions. In the rest of the paper, we will only informally describe the proof and pinpoint the key idea to formalize it in
\mathsf{PV}
if it is unclear.

2.2
Theory
\mathsf{APX}

Intuitively, we will define the theory as
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
together with additional axioms intended to formalize that
{\mathsf{P}}
approximately computes the acceptance probability of a given circuit up to a specified precision. In other words, for every deterministic circuit
C
and any
\Delta
,
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
outputs the encoding of a rational number in
[0,1]
guaranteed to lie within the interval
[p-1/|\Delta|,p+1/|\Delta|]
, where
p
denotes the acceptance probability of
C
. For simplicity, we will also denote
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
by
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)
, where
\delta^{-1}\triangleq|\Delta|\in\mathsf{Log}
is the precision of counting.

Language of
\mathsf{APX}
.

\mathsf{APX}
is an equational theory whose language extends that of
\mathsf{PV}
by including the new function symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
and every additional function symbol that can be introduced through the usual function symbol introduction rules of
\mathsf{PV}
(including composition and limited recursion on notation).

Although
\mathsf{PV}
is an equational theory operating over strings, propositional connectives, arithmetic operations, and arithmetic relations (e.g., comparison between rational numbers) can be encoded by appropriate equations with desired properties (see, e.g.,
(
65
)
). This allows us to formulate the following axioms.

Axioms of
\mathsf{APX}
.

The axioms involve only universally quantified variables and can therefore be expressed as
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
equations:

•

(
Basic Axiom
). Any provable equation in
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
is an axiom of
\mathsf{APX}
. Moreover,
[{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)\in\mathbb{Q}]=1
,
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)\leq 1
,
0\leq{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
are axioms of
\mathsf{APX}
, where “
x\leq y
” is formalized by an appropriate
\mathsf{PV}
equation that is valid if and only if
[x]_{\mathbb{Q}}\leq[y]_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

•

(
Boundary Axiom
). For any
C\in B_{n}
,
\mathsf{IsConst}(C)\to{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)=\mathsf{Bool}(C)
. This axiom indicates that the acceptance probability of a syntactically constant circuit
12
12

12

In other words, there is no path from the output gate to an input variable, i.e., the relevant part of the circuit consists of Boolean operations applied to constant input bits.
that always outputs
b\in\{0,1\}
is equal to
b
.

•

(
Precision Consistency Axiom
). For every
n,\delta_{1}^{-1},\delta_{2}^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and every
C\in B_{n}
,

\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta_{1}}(C)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta_{2}}(C)\right|\leq\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\beta.

(2.3)

Intuitively, this axiom states that the approximate counting function
{\mathsf{P}}
should be consistent with different precision parameters.

•

(
Local Consistency Axiom
). For every
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and every
C\in B_{n}
,

\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)-\frac{{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{0}(C))+{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{1}(C))}{2}\right|\leq 2\cdot\delta+\beta.

(2.4)

Intuitively, this axiom states that the approximate counting function
{\mathsf{P}}
should be self-consistent in the sense that the acceptance probability of a circuit
C
is close to the average acceptance probability of the circuit obtained by randomly fixing the rightmost input bit of
C
.

Rules of
\mathsf{APX}
.

Finally, the theory contains the following derivation rules:

•

(
Logical Rules
). We include the logical rules of
\mathsf{PV}
:

1.

t_{1}=t_{2}\vdash t_{2}=t_{1}

2.

t_{1}=t_{2},t_{2}=t_{3}\vdash t_{1}=t_{3}

3.

t_{1}=t_{2}\vdash t_{1}(x/t)=t_{2}(x/t)

4.

u=v\vdash t(x/u)=t(x/v)

•

(
Structural Induction Rule
). Let
f_{1}(x,\vec{y})
and
f_{2}(x,\vec{y})
be
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
functions. For
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
functions
g(\vec{y})
,
h_{0}(x,\vec{y},z)
, and
h_{1}(x,\vec{y},z)
, if the following equations are provable for
j\in\{1,2\}
and
i\in\{0,1\}

\displaystyle f_{j}(\varepsilon,\vec{y})=g(\vec{y})

(2.5)

\displaystyle f_{j}(s_{i}(x),\vec{y})=h_{i}(x,\vec{y},f_{j}(x,\vec{y}))

(2.6)

then we can deduce the equation
f_{1}(x,\vec{y})=f_{2}(x,\vec{y})
. This rule is analogous to the original induction rule in
\mathsf{PV}
. Intuitively, it means that if
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are both identical to the function recursively defined from
g,h_{0},h_{1}
, they are the same function.

Remark 2.1

(Nested Probability Symbols)
.

We stress that the function symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
does not take
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle circuits as input, and therefore sentences involving nested probability symbols such as

\Pr_{x}[\,\Pr_{y}[\varphi(x,y)]>\varepsilon\,]>\delta

cannot be formalized directly in
{\mathsf{APX}}
. Nevertheless, for predicates
\varphi(x,p)
and
\psi(y)
that do not share inputs, the nested probability

\Pr_{x}\left[\psi\!\left(x,\Pr_{y}[\varphi(y)]\right)\right]

can be expressed in
{\mathsf{APX}}
, as we can define a
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle algorithm that first calculates
p=\Pr_{y}[\varphi(y)]
by calling the
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle, and then calculates
\Pr_{x}[\psi(x,p)]
by calling the
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle again.

Remark 2.2

(Elementary Functions and Precision Issues)
.

In our formalizations, we sometimes employ elementary functions over the reals, such as
\sqrt{x}
,
\ln(x)
, or
\exp(x)
(typically involving constants or for an
x
of the form
a/b
with
a,b\in\mathsf{Log}
). As the output of these functions may be an irrational number, to implement them in
\mathsf{PV}
, we need to define each function by taking an additional parameter that determines the number of digits of precision. When the functions are defined appropriately, appropriate formulations of basic inequalities (e.g.,
\exp(x)\geq 1+x
) can be proved in
\mathsf{PV}
by directly formalizing a standard mathematical proof. In this paper, in the context of the use of such values and inequalities, we always have a margin to tolerate any potential precision issue (e.g., the error term
\beta\in\mathsf{Log}^{-1}
in axioms). For this reason, and following standard practice, we will not elaborate on the actual implementation of such functions and their basic properties.

2.3
Models of
\mathsf{APX}

Let
\mathbb{M}
be the standard model of
\mathsf{PV}
, i.e., the universe is
\{0,1\}^{*}
,
\varepsilon
is interpreted as the empty string, and
s_{0}(x)
,
s_{1}(x)
are interpreted as the functions that append
0
and
1
to
x
, respectively. For every function
\hat{\mathsf{P}}:\{0,1\}^{*}\times\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}^{*}
,
\mathbb{M}(\hat{\mathsf{P}})
is the model of
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
where the function symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
is interpreted as the function
\hat{\mathsf{P}}
.

Definition 2.3

(Standard Models)
.

Let
\hat{\mathsf{P}}\colon\{0,1\}^{*}\times\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}^{*}
be any
correct approximate counting function
, i.e.,

1.

\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
outputs (the encoding of) a rational number
q\in[0,1]
within the interval
[p-1/|\Delta|,p+1/|\Delta|]
for every circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
and
\Delta\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, where
p
is the acceptance probability of
C
and
q
is of length at most
|C|\cdot|\Delta|^{2}
; and

2.

\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
outputs the correct value in
\{0,1\}
whenever the input circuit
C
satisfies
\mathsf{IsConst}(C)
.

We say that
\mathbb{M}(\hat{\mathsf{P}})
is
a standard model
of
\mathsf{APX}
.

Definition 2.4

(Admissible Models)
.

Let
\hat{\mathsf{P}}\colon\{0,1\}^{*}\times\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
be a function. We say that
\mathbb{M}(\hat{\mathsf{P}})
is
an admissible model
of
{\mathsf{APX}}
if it satisfies all axioms and rules of
{\mathsf{APX}}
.

The crucial observation is that a model is standard if and only if it is admissible. The proof of the theorem is highly constructive; indeed, similar induction arguments occur multiple times in the development of basic probability theory in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
(see
Section

3
).

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
\hat{\mathsf{P}}:\{0,1\}^{*}\times\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}^{*}
be any function. Then
\mathbb{M}(\hat{\mathsf{P}})
is a standard model if and only if it is an admissible model.

Proof.

We will only prove the
(\Leftarrow)
direction, as the converse is straightforward. Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
\mathbb{M}(\hat{\mathsf{P}})
is admissible but is not standard. Then there is a circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
and
\Delta\in\{0,1\}^{*}
such that

\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)\notin p(C)\pm\frac{1}{|\Delta|},

(2.7)

where
p(C)\triangleq\Pr_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}[C(x)=1]
is the acceptance probability of
C
.
Note that
C
and
\Delta
are encoded by finite strings (in the standard model
\mathbb{M}
of
\mathsf{PV}
), and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

As
\mathbb{M}(\hat{\mathsf{P}})
is admissible, it must satisfy the
2.2
. Subsequently,
Equation

2.7
does not hold when
n=0
. It suffices to consider the case that
n>0
. Suppose for contradiction we have

\left|\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)-p(C)\right|>\frac{1}{|\Delta|}+\varepsilon,

(2.8)

where
\varepsilon>0
. Let
\Xi\triangleq 1^{10(n+1)/\varepsilon}
. As
\hat{\mathsf{P}}
satisfies the
2.2
, we have

\left|\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Xi)-p(C)\right|\geq\left|\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)-p(C)\right|-\left(\frac{1}{|\Delta|}+\frac{2}{|\Xi|}\right)>\varepsilon-\frac{2}{|\Xi|}.

(2.9)

Let
C_{0},C_{1}
be the circuits obtained by fixing the rightmost input bit of
C
to be
0
and
1
, respectively. As
\hat{\mathsf{P}}
must satisfy the
2.2
, we have

\left|\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Xi)-\frac{\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C_{0},\Xi)+\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C_{1},\Xi)}{2}\right|\leq\frac{3}{|\Xi|},

(2.10)

Also,
p(C)=(p(C_{0})+p(C_{1}))/2
by its definition. Subsequently, there exists
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
such that

\left|\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C_{\sigma},\Xi)-p(C_{\sigma})\right|\geq\left|\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Xi)-p(C)\right|-\frac{3}{|\Xi|}.

(2.11)

Recall that
n\in\mathbb{N}
is a standard integer. Let
C^{(0)}\triangleq C
, and
C^{(1)}\triangleq C_{\sigma}
. By the procedure defined above, for every
1\leq i\leq n
, we can define
C^{(i)}
as the circuit obtained from
C^{(i-1)}
by fixing the rightmost input bit such that

\left|\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C^{(i)},\Xi)-p(C^{(i)})\right|\geq\left|\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C^{(i-1)},\Xi)-p(C^{(i-1)})\right|-\frac{3}{|\Xi|},

and therefore by
Equation

2.9
we eventually have

\left|\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C^{(n)},\Xi)-p(C^{(n)})\right|\geq\varepsilon-\frac{6\cdot(n+1)}{|\Xi|}>0.

Note that the circuit
C^{(n)}
has input length
0
and as a consequence computes a constant function. The value
p(C^{(n)})\in\{0,1\}
is its acceptance probability. Since
\mathbb{M}(\hat{\mathsf{P}})
is admissible, it satisfies the
2.2
, and consequently
\hat{\mathsf{P}}(C^{(n)},\Xi)=p(C^{(n)})
. This contradicts the above inequality.
∎

Definition 2.6
.

Among the standard models of
\mathsf{APX}
, the one that interprets
{\mathsf{P}}
by the exact counting function is called the
exact standard model
of
\mathsf{APX}
, denoted by
\mathbb{M}^{*}
.

Proposition 2.7

(Soundness of
{\mathsf{APX}}
)
.

Provable equations in
{\mathsf{APX}}
are true in any standard model of
{\mathsf{APX}}
.

Proof.

This can be verified by induction on the proof.
∎

2.4
First-Order Theory
\mathsf{APX}_{1}

In analogy with the first-order theory
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
, we will introduce a first-order theory
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
that includes all
\mathsf{APX}
provable equations as well as convenient deduction rules.

Language of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
.

The language of the first-order theory
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
includes all
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
symbols.

Axioms of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
.

The theory is axiomatized by the standard first-order logic with equality together with the following non-logical axiom schemes:

•

For any provable equation
s(\vec{x})=t(\vec{x})
of
\mathsf{APX}
,
\forall\vec{x}~s(\vec{x})=t(\vec{x})
is an axiom of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
.

•

\forall x~\forall y~(x=y\leftrightarrow s_{i}(x)=s_{i}(y))
,
i\in\{0,1\}
, is an axiom of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
.

•

\forall x~\varepsilon\neq s_{i}(x)
is an axiom of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
.

•

\forall x~s_{0}(x)\neq s_{1}(x)
is an axiom of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
.

•

(
n
-induction). Let
\varphi
be a quantifier-free formula and
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},\vec{y}
be variables. Suppose that
\varphi
does not contain free variables other than
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
and
\vec{y}
. Then

\forall\vec{y}~\left(\bigwedge_{j\in[n]}\varphi(x_{j}/\varepsilon)\land\forall\vec{x}\left(\bigwedge_{\vec{\sigma}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}(\varphi\to\varphi_{\vec{\sigma}})\right)\to\forall\vec{x}~\varphi\right),

where
\varphi_{\vec{\sigma}}
denotes the formula
\varphi
with all free occurrences of
x_{i}
substituted by
s_{\sigma_{i}}(x_{i})
for each
i\in[n]
, is an axiom of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
.

We observe that
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
satisfies the following properties.

Proposition 2.8
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
admits a universal axiomatization.

Proof Sketch.

This is essentially the same as the proof that
\mathsf{PV}
admits a universal axiomatization (see
29
;
58
). Note that all axioms of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
are universal sentences except for the
n
-induction axiom scheme, which is a
\forall\exists
-sentence. In more detail, the
n
-induction axiom scheme is logically equivalent to the following sentence: For every
\vec{y}
and
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
satisfying that

•

\varphi(x_{j}/\varepsilon)
for every
j\in[n]
, and

•

\lnot\varphi
,

there exists an
\vec{x}^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})
and
\vec{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
\varphi(\vec{x}/\vec{x}^{\prime})
is true but
\varphi(\vec{x}/\vec{x}^{\prime}_{\sigma})
is false, where
\vec{x}^{\prime}_{\sigma}\triangleq(s_{\sigma_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,s_{\sigma_{n}}(x^{\prime}_{n}))
. Nevertheless, there is a straightforward polynomial-time algorithm that outputs such
\vec{x}^{\prime}
given
\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
by considering prefixes of
\vec{x}
, and the correctness of the algorithm can be proved in
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
. This can be used to show that the
n
-induction axiom scheme can be derived from other axiom schemes and
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
admits a universal axiomatization.
∎

Proposition 2.9
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
is conservative over
\mathsf{APX}
.

Proof Sketch.

The proof is essentially the same as the proof that
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
is conservative over
\mathsf{PV}
. We refer interested readers to
29
;
14
;
58
for more details.
∎

Similar to
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
(see
57
), we can show that a form of induction principle on quantifier-free formulas is provable in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. In order to state this result, we need to make some remarks about notation.

Recall that we assume a straightforward encoding of natural numbers, such as the dyadic encoding in
29
, and use
[x]_{\mathbb{N}}
to denote the natural number encoded by
x
. Let
\mathsf{Less}_{\mathbb{N}}(x,y)
be the
\mathsf{PV}
function that outputs
1
if (in the standard model)
[x]_{\mathbb{N}}<[y]_{\mathbb{N}}
and outputs
0
otherwise. We use
[x<y]
as a shorthand for
\mathsf{Less}_{\mathbb{N}}(x,y)
. We use
\forall x<y~\varphi(x)
as a shorthand for
\forall x~([x<y]=1\to\varphi(x))
, and
\exists x<y~\varphi(x)
as a shorthand for
\exists x~([x<y]=1\land\varphi(x))
.

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
\varphi(x,\vec{y})
be a quantifier-free formula. Then
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves

\forall\vec{y}~\forall b~\left(\varphi(0,\vec{y})\land\forall x<b~(\varphi(x,\vec{y})\to\varphi(x+1,\vec{y}))\to\varphi(b,\vec{y})\right),

where
0
is the
\mathsf{PV}
-term encoding
0\in\mathbb{N}
and
+
is the
\mathsf{PV}
-function for addition of natural numbers.

Proof Sketch.

The proof is essentially the same as the admissibility proof of such induction scheme in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
, following a binary search argument. We refer interested readers to
29
;
14
;
57
;
58
for more details.
∎

Models of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

Any model
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{P})
of
{\mathsf{APX}}
induces a model of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
with the same universe and interpretation for
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
terms. In particular, a model of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is said to be a
standard
model if it is derived from any standard model of
{\mathsf{APX}}
. A first-order sentence
\varphi
in the language of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is said to be a
true sentence
if it is true in
any
standard model. We provide two examples:

•

Let
C_{n,m}\equiv 0
be a constant circuit that takes
(x,y)\in\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{m}
. The sentence

\forall n\in\mathsf{Log}~\forall m\in\mathsf{Log}~\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~\forall\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}~{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C(x,\cdot))\leq 2\delta

in suitable formalization, is a true sentence as it holds when
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\cdot)
is interpreted as any valid approximate counting oracle with additive error
\delta
.

•

For the same circuit
C_{n,m}
, the sentence

\forall n\in\mathsf{Log}~\forall m\in\mathsf{Log}~\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~\forall\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}~{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C(x,\cdot))=0

is true in the exact standard model, but is not true in the standard model where
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C(x,\cdot))\triangleq\delta
and
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(D)\triangleq 0
when
D\neq C(x,\cdot)
. Therefore it is not a true sentence.

Proposition 2.11

(Soundness of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
)
.

Any provable sentence
\varphi
in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is a true sentence.

Proof.

This can be verified by induction on the proof.
∎

3
Probabilistic Reasoning in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}

In this section, we prove meta-theorems that exhibit the robustness of the approximate counting function in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
and develop basic concepts such as (approximate) expectation and variance for feasibly defined random variables.

3.1
Consistency of Approximate Counting

We now state a couple of meta-theorems indicating that the approximate counting functionality provided in
\mathsf{APX}
is consistent in a strong sense.

3.1.1
Global Consistency of Approximate Counting

Monotonicity.

Suppose that there are two circuits
C_{1},C_{2}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
satisfying that
C_{1}(x)\leq C_{2}(x)
for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. Then the acceptance probability of
C_{1}(x)
is at most that of
C_{2}(x)
. Therefore, if
{\mathsf{P}}
is a function for approximate counting, the acceptance probability of
C_{1}
reported by
{\mathsf{P}}
should be no larger than the reported acceptance probability of
C_{2}
plus twice the precision of counting. Formally:

Lemma 3.1
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves that

\displaystyle\forall n\in\mathsf{Log}~\forall C_{1},C_{2}\in B_{n}~\forall\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}~\forall\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}

\displaystyle\quad\big((\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C_{1}(x)\leq C_{2}(x)\to{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{1})\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{2})+2\cdot\delta+\beta\big).

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
\vec{y}
and
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and circuits
C_{1},C_{2}\in B_{n}
. Suppose that
\forall x~C_{1}(x)\leq C_{2}(x)
. We will prove that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{1})\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{2})+2\cdot\delta+\beta.

Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later, and
C_{1}^{k,x},C_{2}^{k,x}:\{0,1\}^{n-k}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuits obtained by fixing the rightmost
k
bits of
C_{1},C_{2}
by
x\in\{0,1\}^{k}
, respectively.

We will prove that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{1})\leq P_{\eta}(C_{2})+6\cdot n\cdot\eta
. This suffices as we can pick
\eta=1/(100\cdot n\cdot\beta)
and apply the
2.2
.

Towards a contradiction, assume that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{1})>P_{\eta}(C_{2})+6\cdot n\cdot\eta
. We will design a
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle algorithm that, for any such
C_{1},C_{2}
and
k\leq n
, outputs a string
x
of length
k
that satisfies the invariant

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{1}^{k,x})>{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{2}^{k,x})+6\cdot(n-k)\cdot\eta.

Moreover, the correctness of the algorithm can be proved in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. The algorithm is an iterative algorithm that considers
k=0,1,\dots,n
:

•

For
k=0
, the algorithm outputs
\varepsilon
. This is correct as
C_{1}^{0,\varepsilon}=C_{1}
,
C_{2}=C_{2}^{0,\varepsilon}
, and as a consequence the required statement follows from the assumption that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{1})>P_{\eta}(C_{2})+6\cdot n\cdot\eta
.

•

Now suppose the algorithm could output a string
x\in\{0,1\}^{k}
such that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{1}^{k,x})>{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{2}^{k,x})+6\cdot(n-k)\cdot\eta.

Our goal is to output a string
x^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{k+1}
such that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{1}^{k+1,x^{\prime}})>{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{2}^{k+1,x^{\prime}})+6\cdot(n-k-1)\cdot\eta.

Note that by the
2.2
13
13

13

Here, the parameter
\beta
in the Local Consistency Axiom is set to
\eta
.
, we know that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{1}^{k,x})\geq(1/2)\cdot({\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{1}^{k,x},0))+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{1}^{k,x},1)))-3\cdot\eta;

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{2}^{k,x})\leq(1/2)\cdot({\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{2}^{k,x},0))+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{2}^{k,x},1)))+3\cdot\eta.

Subsequently, there must be
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
such that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{1}^{k,x},\sigma))>{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{2}^{k,x},\sigma))+6\cdot(n-k-1)\cdot\eta
. The algorithm queries the
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle, finds such
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
, and outputs
x^{\prime}\triangleq\sigma\circ x
. This satisfies the invariant, as
\mathsf{Fix}(C_{1}^{k,x},\sigma)=C_{1}^{k,\sigma\circ x}
and
\mathsf{Fix}(C_{2}^{k,x},\sigma)=C_{2}^{k,\sigma\circ x}
.

It is clear that the correctness of the algorithm can be proved in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
using induction for open formulas.
14
14

14

Formally, we use
n
-induction for
n=1
, and rely on the fact that open formulas are expressive enough, i.e., the language of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
includes the function symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
and oracle polynomial-time functions with access to
{\mathsf{P}}
.
It follows that as we fix
k=n
, the algorithm provably outputs
x^{\star}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{1}^{n,x^{\star}})>{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{2}^{n,x^{\star}})
, under the assumption that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{1})>P_{\eta}(C_{2})+6\cdot n\cdot\eta
. Note that
C_{1}^{n,x}
and
C_{2}^{n,x}
are circuits that do not read their inputs and output
C_{1}(x^{\star})
and
C_{2}(x^{\star})
, respectively. This violates the
2.2
, since
C_{1}(x)\leq C_{2}(x)
for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.
∎

Global Consistency.

A corollary of monotonicity is that if two circuits are provably identical, their acceptance probabilities given by the oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
should not differ significantly. Formally:

Lemma 3.2
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves that

\displaystyle\forall n\in\mathsf{Log}~\forall C_{1},C_{2}\in B_{n}~\forall\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}~

\displaystyle\quad\big((\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C_{1}(x)=C_{2}(x))\to|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{1})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{2})|\leq 2\cdot\delta+\beta\big).

Proof.

We can prove by
Lemma

3.1
that if
C_{1}(x)=C_{2}(x)
for any
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, it follows that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{1})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{2})\leq 2\cdot\delta+\beta
and
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{2})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{1})\leq 2\cdot\delta+\beta
. Subsequently, we will have
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{1})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{2})|\leq 2\cdot\delta+\beta
as long as the absolute value function is properly defined.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

The standard way to formalize approximate counting for a polynomial-time decidable property in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
is to first translate it to a circuit
C
then query
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)
. The global consistency property shows that the approximate counting oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
is robust with respect to the translation of
\mathsf{PV}
functions into circuits, provided that we can prove in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
that the translation is functionally correct. The latter can be done already in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
(see, e.g.,
(
77
, Section 2.4)
).

3.1.2
Permutational Symmetry of Approximate Counting

Next we show that the approximate counting oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
is
permutational symmetric
, in the sense that a permutation of input variables does not change the acceptance probability given by
{\mathsf{P}}
significantly.

Local Symmetry.

As a first step, we show that swapping two adjacent input bits of a circuit does not change the acceptance probability significantly. Concretely:

Lemma 3.4
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves that

\forall n\in\mathsf{Log}~\forall i\in[n-1]~\forall C\in B_{n}~\forall\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}~|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\mathsf{Swap}(C,i))|\leq 2\cdot\delta+\beta,

where
\mathsf{Swap}(C,i)
is a
\mathsf{PV}
-function that outputs a circuit
C^{\prime}
obtained by swapping the
i
-th and the
(i+1)
-th input bits (from the rightmost bit) of
C
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
n\in\mathsf{Log}
,
i\in[n-1]
,
C\in B_{n}
,
\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, and
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later.

For
C\in B_{n}
and
|x|<n
, we define
\mathsf{Fix}(C,x)
as the
\mathsf{PV}
function that outputs a circuit obtained by fixing the last
|x|
input bits of
C
to
x
, i.e., it outputs
C_{x}\in B_{n-|x|}
such that
C_{x}(u)=C(u\circ x)
. Note that for properly defined
\mathsf{PV}
functions
\mathsf{Swap}
and
\mathsf{Fix}
, we can prove in
\mathsf{PV}
that if
|x|=i-1
,
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in\{0,1\}^{2}
,
C_{x,\sigma}^{12}\triangleq\mathsf{Fix}(C,\sigma_{1}\circ\sigma_{2}\circ x)
is functionally equivalent to
C_{x,\sigma}^{21}\triangleq\mathsf{Fix}(\mathsf{Swap}(C,i),\sigma_{2}\circ\sigma_{1}\circ x)
. Moreover, we may assume that it is provable in
\mathsf{PV}
that for any circuit
C\in B_{n}
,
x\in\{0,1\}^{k}
, and
z\in\{0,1\}^{n-k-1}
,
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{Fix}(C,x),s_{i}(z))=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{Fix}(C,s_{i}(x)),z)
, and that
\mathsf{Fix}(C,\varepsilon)=C
.

Therefore, by the
3.1.1
of approximate counting, we have that

\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{i-1}~\forall\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{2}~|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{x,\sigma}^{12})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{x,\sigma}^{21})|\leq 3\cdot\eta.

(3.1)

That is, for
x\in\{0,1\}^{i-1}
, if we arbitrarily fix the
rightmost
two bits of
\mathsf{Fix}(C,x)
and
\mathsf{Fix}(\mathsf{Swap}(C,i),x)
, their acceptance probabilities are close. By applying
2.2
twice and subsequently the
3.1.1
of approximate counting, we can prove that

\displaystyle\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{i-1}~\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C,x))-(1/4)\sum_{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{2}}{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C^{12}_{x,\sigma})\right|\leq 10\cdot\eta;

\displaystyle\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{i-1}~\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(\mathsf{Swap}(C,i),x))-(1/4)\sum_{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{2}}P_{\eta}(C^{21}_{x,\sigma})\right|\leq 10\cdot\hat{\eta}.

Subsequently, we know from
Equation

3.1
that

\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{i-1}~|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C,x))-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(\mathsf{Swap}(C,i),x))|\leq 20\cdot\eta.

(3.2)

This shows that the acceptance probabilities of
C
and
\mathsf{Swap}(C,i)
are close when the rightmost
i-1
bits of them are both fixed by
x\in\{0,1\}^{i-1}
.

We will now prove that

|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Swap}(C,i))|\leq 20\cdot(n+1)\cdot\eta.

(3.3)

This suffices as we can pick
\eta\triangleq\beta/(100\cdot(n+1))
and apply the
2.2
.

Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
Equation

3.3
does not hold. We design an iterative algorithm that given
C
,
i
, and
k\leq i-1
, outputs a string
x
of length
k
such that

|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C,x))-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(\mathsf{Swap}(C,i),x))|>20\cdot(n+1-k)\cdot\eta.

The algorithm is essentially the same as the algorithm in the proof of
Lemma

3.1
, i.e., it extends the string by one bit in each iteration by querying the approximate counting oracle. In particular, the base case
k=0
is satisfied, as
Equation

3.3
does not hold. Therefore, for
k=i-1
, the algorithm outputs a string
x\in\{0,1\}^{i-1}
such that

|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C,x))-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(\mathsf{Swap}(C,i),x))|>20\cdot(n+1-i)\cdot\eta\geq 20\cdot\eta.

This violates
Equation

3.2
and thus completes the proof.
∎

Permutational Symmetry.

We can then state and prove the
permutational symmetry
of approximate counting by decomposing a permutation into a sequence of transformations
C\mapsto\mathsf{Swap}(C,i)
.

We assume a straightforward encoding of permutations of
[n]
for
n\in\mathsf{Log}
, and write
\pi\in S_{n}
as an abbreviation of “
\pi
is a permutation of
[n]
” encoded by a straightforward
\mathsf{PV}
function. Let
C\in B_{n}
and
\pi\in S_{n}
be a permutation of
[n]
. We define
\mathsf{Permute}(C,\pi)
be the
\mathsf{PV}
function that outputs a circuit
C\circ\pi\in B_{n}
defined as
(C\circ\pi)(x)=C(x_{\pi_{n}}\circ\dots\circ x_{\pi_{1}})
. Then we have that:

Lemma 3.5
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}\vdash\forall n\in\mathsf{Log}~\forall\pi\in S_{n}~\forall C\in B_{n}~\forall\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}~|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C\circ\pi)|\leq 2\cdot\delta+\beta
.

Proof Sketch.

Under a straightforward encoding of permutations of
[n]
, we can prove in
\mathsf{PV}
that there is a list
\ell=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
for some
k\in\mathsf{Log}
such that
C\circ\pi
is functionally equivalent to
C_{k}
defined as

C_{0}\triangleq C,\quad C_{j}\triangleq\mathsf{Swap}(C_{j-1},i_{j})\quad(j\in[k]).

By induction on
j
, we can prove by applying
Lemma

3.4
that for any
\eta\in\mathsf{Log}
,
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{j})|\leq 3\cdot j\cdot\eta
. This, together with the
3.1.1
of approximate counting, implies that

|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C\circ\pi)|\leq 3\cdot(k+1)\cdot\eta.

We then prove the lemma by taking
\eta\triangleq\beta/(10\cdot(k+1))
and applying the
2.2
.
∎

3.1.3
Existence Lemma for Approximate Counting

An important counting principle is that if a mathematical object can be sampled with non-zero probability, then it must
exist
. This simple result is the bedrock of the celebrated probabilistic method in combinatorics (see, e.g.,
5
). The following lemma formalizes the principle in the context of approximate counting:

Lemma 3.6
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}\vdash\forall n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}~\forall C\in B_{n}~(\beta>0\land{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)>\delta+\beta\to\exists x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C(x)=1
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix any
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
n
-input circuit
C\in B_{n}
. Suppose that
\beta>0
and
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)>\delta+\beta
, and let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be determined later.

By the
2.2
, we can see that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)\geq\beta-\eta
. Suppose, towards a contradiction, that for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
C(x)=0
. In such case,
C(\vec{x})
is equivalent to the circuit
\mathsf{Null}_{n}
that always outputs
0
. By the
3.1.1
of approximate counting, we have that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Null}_{n})\geq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)-3\eta\geq\beta-4\eta.

Let
\eta=\beta/10
. We have that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Null}_{n})>3\eta
, which leads to a contradiction with the
2.2
.
∎

We note that a more general version of the principle will be proved in
Section

3.2.3
following a similar but more complicated argument, which will be later used to prove the
linearity of approximate expectation
.

3.1.4
Approximate Counting for Concrete Circuits

In this subsection, we consider the behavior of the approximate counting oracle on concrete circuits: the “less-than-
t
” circuit that parses its input as a number and outputs
1
if it is less than a fixed threshold, and circuits with a small number of inputs.

“Less-than-
t
” Circuits.

Let
t\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{n}\}
,
C_{<t}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that parses its input as the binary encoding of a number
x\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{n}-1\}
and accepts if and only if
x<t
. The following lemma shows in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
that the acceptance probability of
C_{<t}
is approximately
t/2^{n}
, as expected.

Lemma 3.7

(Less-than-
t
Circuits)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}\vdash\forall n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}~\forall t\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{n}\}~\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{<t})-t/2^{n}\right|\leq\delta+\beta
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1},t\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{n}\}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. Note that when
t=2^{n}
,
C_{<t}
is functionally equivalent to
\mathsf{True}_{n}
, and thus the lemma immediately follows from the
3.1.1
and
2.2
.
15
15

15

For this step to hold, we need for
C_{<t}
to be provably equivalent to
\mathsf{True}_{n}
. This will hold in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
for a natural implementation of the circuits
C_{<t}
.
In the rest of the proof, we assume
t<2^{n}
.

Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{\leq t})-t/2^{n}\right|>\delta+\beta
. By the
2.2
, we have that
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{<t})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{<t})|\leq\delta+2\eta
, and subsequently

\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{<t})-t/2^{n}\right|>\beta-2\eta.

We may assume that the rightmost bit of
t
is the most significant bit; this is without loss of generality by the
3.1.2
of approximate counting.

We will design an
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle iterative algorithm that, in the
i
-th iteration, outputs
t_{i}\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{n-i}-1\}
satisfying the following condition:

•

Let
C_{i}:\{0,1\}^{n-i}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that parses its input as a number
x\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{n-i}-1\}
and outputs
1
if
x<t_{i}
. Then
\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{i})-t_{i}/2^{n-i}\right|>\beta-20\eta\cdot(i+1)
.

The algorithm starts with
t_{0}\triangleq t
(and thus
C_{0}\triangleq C_{<t}
). In the
i
-th iteration, the algorithm considers the rightmost bit (i.e. the most significant bit) of
t_{i}
. Recall that
\mathsf{Fix}(C,b)
outputs the circuit obtained from
C
by fixing the rightmost input bit to be
b
.

If the rightmost bit of
t_{i}
is
0
, the algorithm outputs
t_{i+1}\triangleq t_{i}
. It is clear that
\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},1)
is functionally equivalent to the
\mathsf{Null}_{n-i-1}
, and thus by the
3.1.1
and
2.2
,
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},1))\leq 3\eta
. Let
C_{i+1}
be the circuit that parses its input as a number
x\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{n}-1\}
and outputs
1
if
x<t_{i+1}
. It follows that
\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},0)
is functionally equivalent to
C_{i+1}
, and thus by the
3.1.1
of approximate counting,
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},0))-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{i+1})|\leq 3\eta
. Subsequently:

\displaystyle~\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{i+1})-t_{i+1}/2^{n-i-1}\right|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},0))-t_{i+1}/2^{n-i-1}\right|-3\eta

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},0))+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},1))-t_{i+1}/2^{n-i-1}\right|-6\eta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~2\cdot\left|\frac{{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},0))+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},1))}{2}-\frac{t_{i+1}}{2^{n-i}}\right|-6\eta

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~2\cdot\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{i})-t_{i}/2^{n-i}\right|-12\eta

(
2.2
)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle~\beta-20\eta\cdot(i+1)-12\eta\geq\beta-20\eta\cdot(i+2).

If the rightmost bit of
t_{i}
is
1
, the algorithm outputs
t_{i+1}=t_{i}-2^{n-i-1}
. It is clear that
\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},0)
is functionally equivalent to
\mathsf{True}_{n-i+1}
, and thus by the
3.1.1
and
2.2
,
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},0))-1|\leq 3\eta
. Let
C_{i+1}
be the circuit that parses its input as a number
x\in\{0,1,\dots,2^{n}-1\}
and outputs
1
if
x<t_{i+1}
. It follows that
\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},1)
is functionally equivalent to
C_{i+1}
, and thus by the
3.1.1
of approximate counting,
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},1))-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{i+1})|\leq 3\eta
. Subsequently:

\displaystyle~\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{i+1})-t_{i+1}/2^{n-i-1}\right|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},1))-t_{i+1}/2^{n-i-1}\right|-3\eta

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},0))-1+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},1))-t_{i+1}/2^{n-i-1}\right|-6\eta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~2\cdot\left|\frac{{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},0))+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},1))}{2}-\frac{t_{i+1}+2^{n-i-1}}{2^{n-i}}\right|-6\eta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~2\cdot\left|\frac{{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},0))+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},1))}{2}-\frac{t_{i}}{2^{n-i}}\right|-6\eta

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~2\cdot\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{i})-t_{i}/2^{n-i}\right|-12\eta

(
2.2
)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle~\beta-20\eta\cdot(i+1)-12\eta\geq\beta-20\eta\cdot(i+2).

It is clear that the correctness of the algorithm follows from the induction principle for polynomial-time verifiable properties allowed by the
2.4
of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Therefore, after
n
iterations, the algorithm will output
t_{n}=0
such that the acceptance probability of the circuit
C_{n}\equiv\mathsf{Null}_{0}
is at least
\beta-20\eta\cdot(n+1)
. By setting
\eta\triangleq\beta/(40n+40)
, we can conclude a contradiction using the
2.2
.
∎

Circuits with Short Inputs.

For circuits
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
such that
n\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
it is feasible to enumerate all inputs of
C
. The following lemma shows that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)
is consistent with its acceptance probability computed via the brute-force algorithm.

Lemma 3.8

(Brute Force Counting Lemma)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. For every
n\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
, circuit
C\in B_{n}
, and
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, let
t
be the number of accepting inputs of
C
. Then
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)-t/2^{n}|\leq\delta+\beta
. In particular, if
\delta\leq 2^{-n-1}
,
t
is the nearest integer to
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)\cdot 2^{n}
.

The proof of the lemma employs the
2.2
and the
2.2
. We will formalize the argument using the following general tool: If there is a sequence of circuits serving as an approximating counting algorithm for a circuit
C
, in the sense that it satisfies the boundary condition and is locally consistent, then the acceptance probability estimated by the algorithm is necessarily close to
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)
. Formally:

Lemma 3.9

(Dueling Lemma)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C\in B_{n}
be a circuit, and
P_{0},P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
be circuits that output rational numbers such that
P_{i}
is of input length
i
. Let
\delta^{-1},\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. Suppose that for every
i<n
and every
x\in\{0,1\}^{i}
,

\left|P_{i}(x)-\frac{P_{i+1}(x\circ 0)+P_{i+1}(x\circ 1)}{2}\right|\leq\eta;

and that for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
P_{n}(x)=C(x)
. Then
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)-P_{0}|\leq\delta+4\eta\cdot(n+1)
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
n\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C\in B_{n}
, the circuits
P_{0},P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
, and
\delta^{-1},\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. Suppose that it satisfies the two conditions in the lemma. Assume for contradiction that
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)-P_{0}|>\delta+3\eta\cdot(n+1)
. By the
2.2
, we have that
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)-P_{0}|>4\eta\cdot n+3\eta
.

Let
C^{x}
be the circuit obtained by fixing the rightmost
|x|
bits of
C
to be
x
. Consider the following algorithm that, in the
i
-th iteration, outputs
x_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{i}
such that
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C^{x_{i}})-P_{i}(x_{i})|>4\eta\cdot(n-i)+3\eta
. The algorithm starts with
x_{0}\triangleq\varepsilon
. In the
i
-th iteration, we know by the
2.2
that

\displaystyle|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C^{x_{i}})-P_{i}(x_{i})|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C^{0\circ x_{i}})+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C^{1\circ x_{i}})}{2}-P_{i}(x_{i})\right|+3\eta

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C^{0\circ x_{i}})+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C^{1\circ x_{i}})}{2}-\frac{P_{i+1}(0\circ x_{i})+P_{i+1}(1\circ x_{i})}{2}\right|+4\eta

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\Big(|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C^{0\circ x_{i}})-P_{i+1}(0\circ x_{i})|+|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C^{1\circ x_{i}})-P_{i+1}(1\circ x_{i})|\Big)+4\eta.

This means that for some
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
,
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C^{\sigma\circ x_{i}})-P_{i+1}(\sigma\circ x_{i})|>4\eta\cdot(n-i-1)+3\eta
. The algorithm then proceeds by setting
x_{i+1}=\sigma\circ x_{i}
.

It is clear that the correctness of the algorithm can be proved by induction on a quantifier-free formula, which is available in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Therefore, in the
n
-th iteration, the algorithm outputs a string
x_{n}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C^{x_{n}})-P_{n}(x_{n})|>3\eta
. However, this violates the
2.2
as
P_{n}(x_{n})=C(x_{n})
, and the circuit
C^{x_{n}}
is a constant circuit that outputs
C(x_{n})
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.8
.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
n\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
and
C\in B_{n}
,
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, and let
t
be the number of accepting inputs of
C
. Let
P_{0},\dots,P_{n}
be the circuits such that
P_{i}(x)
takes an
i
-bit input
x
and outputs the acceptance probability of
C^{x}:\{0,1\}^{n-i}\to\{0,1\}
defined as
C^{x}(z)=C(z\circ x)
. In particular,
P_{0}=t/2^{n}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be determined later.

Since
n\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
, it is provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
that
P_{0},\dots,P_{n}
satisfy the conditions in the
3.1.4
. Then
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)-P_{0}|=|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)-t/2^{n}|\leq\delta+4\eta\cdot(n+1)
. The lemma follows by setting
\eta\triangleq\beta/(4n+4)
.
∎

3.2
Approximate Expectation and its Basic Theory

We now develop a theory of feasible random variables and their approximate expectation.

3.2.1
Definition of Random Variables and Approximate Expectation

We first define the approximate expectation of a discrete random variable taking values in
\mathbb{Q}
. Recall that explicit sets are sets encoded by an explicit list and, in particular, the size of explicit sets are always feasible. To have the expectation being feasibly computable (approximately), we restrict to the setting where the support of random variables are given as an
explicit set
.

Let
n\in\mathsf{Log}
,
V
be an explicit set of rational numbers, and
C
be a multi-output circuit such that
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves that

\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C(x)\in V.

We say that
(V,n,C)
defines a random variable
X\triangleq C(\mathcal{U}_{n})
, and define the expectation of
X
as

\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot\Pr_{x}[C(x)=v],

where the probability can be implemented by the approximate counting quantifier
{\mathsf{P}}
in
\mathsf{APX}
. This leads to the following formal definition of random variables and expectation.

Definition 3.10

(Random Variable)
.

Let
V
be an explicit set of rational numbers,
n\in\mathsf{Log}
, and
C
be a multi-output circuit. We say that
(V,n,C)
defines a
random variable

X
over
V
if
\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C(x)\in V
. The set
V
is called the
support
of
X
,
C
is called the
sampler
of
X
, and
n
is called the
seed length
of
X
.

Definition 3.11

(Approximate Expectation)
.

Let
(V,n,C)
be a tuple defining a random variable
X
over
V
, and
\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. We define the
approximate expectation
of
X
, denoted by
\Ex_{\delta}[X]
, as

\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{v}),

where
C_{v}
is the
n
-input circuit that given
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, output
1
(resp.
0
) if
C(x)=v
(resp.
C(x)\neq v
), “
\cdot
” denotes the multiplication of rational numbers, and
\sum
denotes the summation of rational numbers.

We note that there is a
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
function
{\mathsf{E}}(V,n,C,\Delta)
computing
\mathbb{E}_{|\Delta|^{-1}}[X]
for the random variable
X
defined from
(V,n,C)
; it enumerates over
v\in V
, constructs the circuit
C_{v}
, calls the oracle
p_{v}\leftarrow{\mathsf{P}}(C_{v},\Delta)
, and sums over
v\cdot p_{v}
for all
v\in V
. To see that this algorithm is feasible, notice that
V
is an explicit set of feasible size, and under the encoding specified in
Section

2.1
, the total length of all rational numbers in
V
is
\mathsf{PV}
-provably feasible.

For simplicity, we will use the notation
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
to denote that
C
is a multi-output circuit whose output is parsed as a rational number.

Moreover, one may think of the acceptance probability of a circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
as the expectation of the indicating random variable
I_{C}\triangleq(\{0,1\},n,C)
up to a small additive error, as shown by the following proposition. Therefore, the properties of expectation we will prove next also translate to properties of approximate counting.

Proposition 3.12
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a Boolean circuit. Let
I_{C}\triangleq(\{0,1\},n,C)
be the indicator random variable for
C(x)=1
. Then for any
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[I_{C}]|\leq 2\delta+\beta
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and a circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
. By the definition of approximate expectation, we know that
\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[I_{C}]=1\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}[C_{1}]
, where
C_{1}(x),C(x)
are functionally equivalent circuits. By the
3.1.1
of approximate counting, we have that

|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[I_{C}]|=|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}[C_{1}]|\leq 2\delta+\beta.\qed

3.2.2
Basic Properties of Approximate Expectation

Precision Consistency.

Similar to the
2.2
for approximate counting, the definition of approximate expectation is consistent with respect to different precisions as shown in the proposition below.

Proposition 3.13
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta_{1}^{-1},\delta_{2}^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
,
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
be an explicit set such that
\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C(x)\in V
. Let
X
be the random variable defined by
(V,n,C)
. Then for every
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta_{1}}[X]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta_{2}}[X]\right|\leq(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\beta)\cdot\|V\|,

where
\|V\|\triangleq\sum_{v\in V}|v|
is the
\ell_{1}
-norm of
V
and
|v|
denotes the absolute value of the rational
v
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Recall that
C_{v}
is the circuit that outputs
1
if
C(x)=v
, and outputs
0
otherwise. By the definition of approximate counting, we can see that

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta_{1}}[X]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta_{2}}[X]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta_{1}}(C_{v})-\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta_{2}}(C_{v})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot({\mathsf{P}}_{\delta_{1}}(C_{v})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta_{2}}(C_{v})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\beta)\right|\leq(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\beta)\cdot\|V\|,

where the second last inequality follows from the
2.2
.
∎

Local Consistency.

Similarly, we can prove that approximate expectation is locally consistent by fixing the rightmost bit of the random seed to be
0
or
1
randomly. Formally:

Proposition 3.14
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
,
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
be an explicit set such that
\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C(x)\in V
. Let
X
be the random variable defined by
(V,n,C)
. Then for every
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]-\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X|_{0}]+\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X|_{1}]}{2}\right|\leq(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|V\|,

where
\|V\|\triangleq\sum_{v\in V}|v|
is the
\ell_{1}
norm of
V
, and for
b\in\{0,1\}
,
X|_{b}
denotes the random variable defined by
(V,n-1,\mathsf{Fix}_{b}(C))
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be determined later. Recall that
\mathsf{Fix}_{b}(C)
outputs the circuit obtained by fixing the rightmost input bit of
C
to be
b
, where
b\in\{0,1\}
. By the
2.2
, we know that for every
v\in V
,

\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{v})-\frac{{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{v},0))+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{v},1))}{2}\right|\leq 3\eta.

By the definition of approximate counting, we can calculate that

\displaystyle~\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X]-\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X|_{0}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X|_{1}]}{2}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\left|\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{v})-\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}((\mathsf{Fix}_{0}(C))_{v})+\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}((\mathsf{Fix}_{1}(C))_{v})\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\left|\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{v})-\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{0}(C_{v}))+\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{1}(C_{v}))\right)\right|+3\eta\cdot\|V\|

(
3.1.1
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{v\in V}|v|\cdot\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{v})-\frac{{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{v},0))+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}(C_{v},1))}{2}\right|+3\eta\cdot\|V\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~6\eta\cdot\|V\|.

The result follows from the
3.2.2
by taking
\eta\triangleq\beta/10
.
∎

Consistency in Support Extension.

Suppose that
X
is a random variable defined by the tuple
(V,n,C)
. Consider an explicit set
V^{\prime}
such that
V\subseteq V^{\prime}
. We can define another random variable
X^{\prime}
that is essentially the same as
X
by considering the tuple
(V^{\prime},n,C)
. The following proposition shows that the expectation of
X^{\prime}
and
X
are nearly the same, i.e., a support extension does not affect the expectation of a random variable significantly.

Proposition 3.15
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
,
V,V^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
be explicit sets such that
\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C(x)\in V\subseteq V^{\prime}
. Let
X
be the random variable defined by
(V,n,C)
, and
X^{\prime}
be the random variable defined by
(V^{\prime},n,C)
. Then:

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X^{\prime}]\right|\leq(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|V^{\prime}\setminus V\|\leq(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|V^{\prime}\|.

where
\|V^{\prime}\setminus V\|=\sum_{v\in V^{\prime}\setminus V}|v|
is the
\ell_{1}
-norm of
V^{\prime}\setminus V
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. By the definition of approximate expectation, we know that

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X^{\prime}]\right|=\left|\sum_{v\in V^{\prime}\setminus V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{v})\right|\leq\sum_{v\in V^{\prime}\setminus V}|v|\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{v}),

(3.4)

where
C_{v}(x)
outputs
1
if
C(x)=v
, and
0
otherwise. Therefore it suffices to prove that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{v})\leq 2\delta+\beta
for
v\in V^{\prime}\setminus V
. Fix any
v\in V^{\prime}\setminus V
. Note that since
C(x)\in V
for
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, we know that
C(x)\neq v
and thus
C_{v}
is (provably) functionally equivalent to
\mathsf{Null}_{n}
. The desired bound then follows from the
3.1.1
of approximate counting using that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\mathsf{Null}_{n})=0
by the
2.2
.
∎

Permutational Symmetry.

Suppose that
X,X^{\prime}
are random variables defined by the tuples
(V,n,C)
and
(V,n,C\circ\pi)
, where
\pi\in S_{n}
denotes a permutation of the input bits. Similar to the
3.1.2
of approximate counting, we will show that
\mathbb{E}[X]\approx\mathbb{E}[X^{\prime}]
.

Proposition 3.16
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
,
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
be an explicit set such that
\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C(x)\in V
. Let
\pi\in S_{n}
be a permutation of the input bits. Let
X,X^{\prime}
be the random variables defined by
(V,n,C)
and
(V,n,C\circ\pi)
, respectively. Then:

|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X^{\prime}]|\leq(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|V\|,

where
\|V\|
is the
\ell_{1}
-norm of
V
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be determined later. We can calculate that

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X^{\prime}]|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{v\in V}|v|\cdot\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{v})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}((C\circ\pi)_{v})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{v\in V}|v|\cdot\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{v})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{v}\circ\pi)\right|+3\eta\cdot\|V\|

\displaystyle\leq 6\eta\cdot\|V\|.

Here, the second line follows from
3.1.1
of approximate counting, and the third line follows from the
3.1.2
of approximate counting. Subsequently, the proposition follows from the
3.2.2
by taking
\eta\triangleq\beta/10
.
∎

3.2.3
Averaging Argument for Expectation

We will prove a general version of the
averaging argument
that allows us to search for a suffix of the seed such that the given linear combination of expectations of random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
is approximately preserved after fixing part of the seed.

Suppose that
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are random variables supported on
V
defined by a sequence of circuits
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}
, each with seed length
n
, i.e., for each
i\in[m]
and every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
C_{i}(x)\in V
. Let
\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. Let
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in\mathbb{Q}
be coefficients, and consider the quantity

\mu_{n,m,\delta,\vec{\lambda}}\triangleq\lambda_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\lambda_{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{2}]+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}].

(3.5)

Let
z\in\{0,1\}^{k}
for
k\in[n]
. We can define the random variable
X_{i}|_{z}
for each
i\in[m]
from
(V,n-k,\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},z))
, where
\mathsf{Fix}(C_{i},z)
outputs the circuit obtained from
C_{i}
by fixing the rightmost
k
bits to
z
. That is,
X_{i}|_{z}
is the random variable obtained by fixing the last
k
input bits of
C_{i}
to be
z
.
16
16

16

This is without loss of generality by the
3.2.2
.
Let
\mu_{n,m,\delta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z}
be the quantity

\mu_{n,m,\delta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z}\triangleq\lambda_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}|_{z}]+\lambda_{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{2}|_{z}]+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}|_{z}].

(3.6)

Theorem 3.17

(Averaging Argument for Expectation)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,m,\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
be circuits,
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
be an explicit set such that
\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~\forall i\in[m]~C_{i}(x)\in V
, and
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m})
be a list of length
m
such that
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
for
i\in[m]
.

Then for every
k\in[n]
and
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, there is a
z\in\{0,1\}^{k}
such that

\mu_{n,m,\delta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z}\geq\mu_{n,m,\delta,\vec{\lambda}}-(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|V\|\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|,

(3.7)

where
\|V\|\triangleq\sum_{v\in V}|v|
and
\|\vec{\lambda}\|\triangleq\sum_{i\in[m]}|\lambda_{i}|
are the
\ell_{1}
-norm of
V
and
\lambda
, respectively, and
\mu_{n,m,\delta,\vec{\lambda}}
and
\mu_{n,m,\delta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z}
are defined by
Equation

3.5
and
Equation

3.6
, respectively.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
n,m,\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
, let
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
be an explicit set,
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m})\in\mathbb{Q}
,
k\in[n]
,
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. Recall that by definition, we have that for each
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z}=\lambda_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}|_{z}]+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}|_{z}];

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z\circ b}=\lambda_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}|_{z\circ b}]+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}|_{z\circ b}].

(3.9)

We will design a
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle polynomial-time algorithm
\mathsf{AvgSampler}(\pi)
that takes

\pi\triangleq(1^{n},1^{m},1^{\delta^{-1}},C_{1},\dots,C_{m},V,\vec{\lambda},1^{k},1^{\beta^{-1}})

as its input and outputs
z\in\{0,1\}^{k}
such that
Equation

3.7
holds.
Theorem

3.17
then follows if the correctness of
\mathsf{AvgSampler}(\pi)
can be proved in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
.

\mathsf{AvgSampler}(\pi)
is an iterative algorithm on
k
(i.e. the length of
z
). We will prove the invariant that for any
k\in\{0,1,\dots,n\}
, the algorithm
\mathsf{AvgSampler}(\pi)
outputs a string
z\in\{0,1\}^{k}
such that

\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z}\geq\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}-3k\cdot\eta\cdot\|V\|\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|

(3.10)

where
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. We note that if this is possible, we can set
\eta\triangleq\beta/(3n+3)
so that
Equation

3.7
follows from the
3.2.2
. Specifically, we can see that

\displaystyle\left|\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z}-\mu_{n,m,\delta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\lambda_{1}\cdot(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}|_{z}]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}|_{z}])+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{m}|_{z}]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}|_{z}])\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\lambda_{1}\cdot(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V\|+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V\|\right|\leq(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|\cdot\|V\|

and similarly

\left|\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}-\mu_{n,m,\delta,\vec{\lambda}}\right|\leq(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|\cdot\|V\|.

Equation

3.7
then follows from the triangle inequality.

For
k=0
,
\mathsf{AvgSampler}(\pi)
outputs
\varepsilon
, and
Equation

3.10
holds as
\mu_{n,m,\vec{y},\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z}=\mu_{n,m,\vec{y},\eta,\vec{\lambda}}
by definition. Suppose that it has already obtained a string
z\in\{0,1\}^{k}
such that
Equation

3.10
holds. Our goal is to choose a bit
b\in\{0,1\}
such that

\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{b\circ z}\geq\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}-3(k+1)\cdot\eta\cdot\|V\|\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|.

For each
i\in[m]
, we know by the
3.2.2
that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}|_{z}]-\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}|_{0\circ z}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}|_{1\circ z}]}{2}\right|\leq 3\eta\cdot\|V\|

It then follows that

\displaystyle\left|\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z}-\frac{\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{0\circ z}+\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{1\circ z}}{2}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i\in[m]}\lambda_{i}\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}|_{z}]-\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}|_{0\circ z}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}|_{1\circ z}]}{2}\right)\right|\leq 3\eta\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|\cdot\|V\|.

Therefore, for some
b\in\{0,1\}
, we will have that
\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{b\circ z}\geq\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z}-3\eta\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|\cdot\|V\|
, which subsequently implies that

\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{b\circ z}\geq\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}|_{z}-3\eta\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|\cdot\|V\|\geq\mu_{n,m,\eta,\vec{\lambda}}-3(k+1)\cdot\eta\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|\cdot\|V\|.

The algorithm
\mathsf{AvgSampler}
can use the
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle to determine
b
and output
b\circ z
. This completes the proof.
∎

3.2.4
Complementation

An easy corollary of the
3.17
is
complementary counting
. That is, if
X
is a random variable over
\{0,1\}
and
\overline{X}\triangleq 1-X
, then
\mathbb{E}[\overline{X}]=1-\mathbb{E}[X]
. Formally:

Corollary 3.18

(Complementary Counting)
.

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C_{1},C_{2}\in B_{n}
such that for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
C_{1}(x)\neq C_{2}(x)
. Then
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{1})+{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{2})-1|\leq 2\delta+\beta
. Moreover, let
X_{1},X_{2}
be the indicator random variables of
C_{1},C_{2}
over
\{0,1\}
, respectively. Then
|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{2}]-1|\leq 2\delta+\beta
.

Proof.

We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C_{1},C_{2}\in B_{n}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be determined later, and
X_{1},X_{2}
be the indicator random variables of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, respectively. It is clear that for any total assignment
\rho
to the seed,
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}|_{\rho}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}|_{\rho}]-1=0
. Therefore, by
3.17
with
k=n
,
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]-1\leq 6\eta
. Similarly, we can show that
-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]+1\leq 6\eta
. This implies that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]-1\right|\leq 6\eta.

(3.11)

By
Proposition

3.12
, we have

|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{1})+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{2})-1|\leq 12\eta.

Subsequently, by the
2.2
, we have
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{1})+{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C_{2})-1|\leq 2\delta+16\eta
. The desired bound then follows by setting
\eta\triangleq\beta/30
. The “Moreover” part follows from
Equation

3.11
by the
3.2.2
.
∎

3.2.5
Linearity of Expectation

We are now ready to prove the (approximate) linearity of expectation, one of the most useful results in probability theory. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m},Y
be random variables over an explicit set
V
. For a random seed
z
of the random variables, we use
X_{i}|_{z}
and
Y|_{z}
to denote the value that
X_{i}
and
Y
evaluate to, respectively. Suppose that for each random seed
z
, we have that

Y|_{z}=\gamma+\lambda_{1}\cdot X_{1}|_{z}+\lambda_{2}\cdot X_{2}|_{z}+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot X_{m}|_{z},

for some
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in\mathbb{Q}
. Then we should be able to obtain that
\mathbb{E}[Y]
is close to

\gamma+\lambda_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]+\lambda_{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[X_{2}]+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot\mathbb{E}[X_{m}].

Formally, we have that:

Theorem 3.19

(Linearity of Expectation)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following: Let
n,m,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
be a list of circuits,
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m})
be a list of length
m
such that
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
for
i\in[m]
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{Q}
, and
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
be an explicit set such that:

•

For any
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
i\in[m]
,
C_{i}(x)\in V
.

•

For any
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
\gamma+\lambda_{1}\cdot C_{1}(x)+\lambda_{2}\cdot C_{2}(x)+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot C_{m}(x)\in V
.

Let
X_{i}
be the random variable defined by
(V,n,C_{i})
for
i\in[m]
, and
Y
be the random variable defined by
(V,n,S)
, where
S:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
is a circuit such that

S(x)=\gamma+\lambda_{1}\cdot C_{1}(x)+\lambda_{2}\cdot C_{2}(x)+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot C_{m}(x).

Then:

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y]-(\gamma+\lambda_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}])\right|\leq(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|V\|\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|,

(3.12)

where
\|V\|
and
\|\vec{\lambda}\|
are the
\ell_{1}
-norm of
V
and
\lambda
, respectively.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. We first prove that
\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y]-(\gamma+\lambda_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}])\leq(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|V\|\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|
. Fix
n,m,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
,
\vec{\lambda}
,
\gamma
, and
V
. Let

\mu\triangleq 1\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y]+(-\lambda_{1})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\dots+(-\lambda_{m})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}],

and for each
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, we define
Y|_{z}
and
X_{i}|_{z}
to be random variable with seed length
0
as

Y|_{z}\triangleq S(z),\quad X_{i}|_{z}\triangleq C_{i}(z),\quad(i\in[m]),\\

(3.13)

and
\mu|_{z}
as

\mu|_{z}\triangleq 1\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y|_{z}]+(-\lambda_{1})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}|_{z}]+\dots+(-\lambda_{m})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}|_{z}].

(3.14)

By the
3.17
, there is a string
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
\mu|_{z}\geq\mu-(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|V\|\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|
, which implies that
\mu\leq\mu|_{z}+(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|V\|\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|
.

Notice that
\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y|_{z}]
is defined as

\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y|_{z}]=\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(S_{v})=\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot\mathsf{Bool}(S_{v})=S(z),

where
S_{v}
is the circuit with no input that outputs
1
if and only if
S(z)=1
. The second equality follows from the
2.2
. Similarly, we can prove that for each
i\in[m]
,
\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}|_{z}]=C_{i}(z)
. Subsequently,
\mu|_{z}=S(z)-(\lambda_{1}\cdot C_{1}(z)+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot C_{m}(z))=\gamma
, which further implies that
\mu\leq\gamma+(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|V\|\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|
, i.e.,

\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y]-(\gamma+\lambda_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}])\leq(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|V\|\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|.

Finally, we can apply the same argument to
\mu^{\prime}
and
\mu^{\prime}|_{z}
defined by

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}

\displaystyle\triangleq(-1)\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y]+\lambda_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}]

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}|_{z}

\displaystyle\triangleq(-1)\cdot\mathbb{E}[Y|_{z}]+\lambda_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}[X_{1}|_{z}]+\dots+\lambda_{m}\cdot\mathbb{E}[X_{m}|_{z}]

to conclude that
\mu=-\mu^{\prime}\geq\gamma-(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|V\|\cdot\|\vec{\lambda}\|
. This completes the proof.
∎

3.3
Probability Inequalities

We now develop several standard inequalities related to (approximate) probability and expectation, including the union bound, Markov’s inequality, and Chebyshev’s inequality.

3.3.1
Union Bound

Another application of the averaging argument for approximate expectation (see
Theorem

3.17
) is the union bound. Recall that the acceptance probability of a circuit
C
can be formalized as the expectation of its indicating random variable
I_{C}\in\{0,1\}
. Therefore the union bound can be derived from the following principle: Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m},Y
be Boolean-valued random variables such that for any random seed
z
,
Y|_{z}=X_{1}|_{z}\lor X_{2}|_{z}\lor\dots\lor X_{m}|_{z}
. Then
\mathbb{E}[Y]
should not be much larger than
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]+\mathbb{E}[X_{2}]+\dots+\mathbb{E}[X_{m}]
. Formally:

Theorem 3.20

(Union Bound)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,m,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}\in B_{n}
be single-output circuits,
V=\{0,1\}
. Suppose that
\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
i\in[m]
,
C_{i}(x)\in V
, and let
Y,X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be random variables defined as follows.

•

For each
i\in[m]
,
X_{i}
is defined by
(V,n,C_{i})
.

•

Y
is defined by
(V,n,S)
, where
S(x)\in\{0,1\}
is a circuit such that
S(x)\leq C_{1}(x)\lor\dots\lor C_{m}(x)
.

Then we have
\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\dots+\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}]+(2\delta+\beta)\cdot m
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
n,m,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}\in B_{n}
, and
V=\{0,1\}
. For each
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, we define
Y|_{z}
as the random variable with seed length
0
that outputs
S(z)
, and
X_{i}
as the random variable with seed length
0
that outputs
C_{i}(z)
for
i\in[m]
. Let
\mu
and
\mu|_{z}
be defined as

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\triangleq 1\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y]+(-1)\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\dots+(-1)\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}],

(3.15)

\displaystyle\mu|_{z}

\displaystyle\triangleq 1\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y|_{z}]+(-1)\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}|_{z}]+\dots+(-1)\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{m}|_{z}].

(3.16)

By the
3.17
, we can conclude that
\mu\leq\mu|_{z}+(2\delta+\beta)\cdot m
for some string
z
.

It then suffices to show that
\mu|_{z}\leq 0
. Similarly to the proof of
Theorem

3.19
, we can prove by the
2.2
that
\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y|_{z}]=S(z)
and
\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}|_{z}]=C_{i}(z)
for
i\in[m]
. Subsequently, we know by the definition of
\mu|_{z}
and the assumption on
S
that

\mu|_{z}=S(z)-(C_{1}(z)+\dots+C_{m}(z))\leq 0,

is provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. This completes the proof.
∎

3.3.2
Markov’s Inequality

Next, we consider Markov’s inequality. For a random variable
X
over an explicit set
V
, we should be able to prove that the probability that
X\geq k\cdot\mathbb{E}[X]
cannot be much larger than
1/k
. This can be naturally formalized as follows:

Theorem 3.21

(Markov’s Inequality)
.

The following statement is provable in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Let
X
be a random variable defined by
(V,n,C)
, where
V
is an explicit set of non-negative rational numbers,
n\in\mathsf{Log}
, and
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
is a circuit. Let
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
with
\mu\geq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]
and
\mu>0
,
k\in\mathbb{Q}
with
k>0
, and
T(x)
be the circuit that outputs
1
if
C(x)\geq k\cdot\mu
, and outputs
0
otherwise. Then

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)\leq\delta+k^{-1}\cdot(1+\delta\cdot\mu^{-1}\cdot\|V\|)+\beta\cdot(\mu^{-1}\cdot\|V\|+1),

where
\|V\|
is the
\ell_{1}
-norm of
V
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
,
\mu,k\in\mathbb{Q}
, and
V
. Let
T(x)
be the circuit as defined above, and
m=|V|
. We define the following random variables:

•

Y
is the indicator variable of
T(x)
, i.e., it is defined by
(\{0,1\},n,T)
.

•

For each
v\in V
,
X_{v}
is the indicator variable of
\mathsf{EQ}(C(x),v)
. Formally, let
C_{v}(x)
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
C(x)=v
, and outputs
0
otherwise,
X_{v}
is the random variable defined by
(\{0,1\},n,C_{v})
.

Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a precision parameter to be determined later, and for
b\in\{0,1\}
, let
C^{(b)}_{v}(x)\in\{0,1\}
be the circuit that outputs
1
if
C(x)=b
. By the definition of approximate expectation, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{v}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}v\cdot b\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{v}^{(b)})=\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{v}^{(1)})

By the definition of
C_{v}(x)
and
C^{(1)}_{v}(x)
, we know that
C^{(1)}_{v}(x)=C_{v}(x)
, and therefore by the
3.1.1
of approximate counting, we have that

|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C^{(1)}_{v})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{v})|\leq 3\cdot\eta.

Subsequently, we can see from the
3.2.2
that

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]\geq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X]-(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V\|

(3.17)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C_{v})-(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V\|

(3.18)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{v\in V}v\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{v}]-(\delta+5\eta)\cdot\|V\|

(3.19)

\displaystyle\geq k\mu\sum_{v\in V,v\geq k\mu}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{v}]-(\delta+5\eta)\cdot\|V\|,

(3.20)

where the last inequality uses that
k
,
\mu
, and
V
are all nonnegative.
This implies that

\sum_{v\in V,v\geq k\cdot\mu}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{v}]\leq k^{-1}\cdot(1+(\delta+5\eta)\cdot\mu^{-1}\cdot\|V\|).

(3.21)

Below we also rely on the following inequality, which follows from
Proposition

3.12
:

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T)|\leq 3\cdot\eta.

(3.22)

It is clear from the definition of
T(x)
and
C_{v}(x)
that

T(x)=\bigvee_{v\in V,v\geq k\cdot\mu}C_{v}(x).

Therefore, by the
3.3.1
, we can conclude that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]+3\cdot\eta

(
Equation

3.22
)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{v\in V,v\geq k\cdot\mu}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{v}]+3\cdot\eta\cdot(m+1)

(Union Bound)

\displaystyle\leq k^{-1}\cdot(1+(\delta+5\eta)\cdot\mu^{-1}\cdot\|V\|)+3\cdot\eta\cdot(|V|+1),

(
Equation

3.21
)

Finally, we take
\eta=\min\{\beta/(50(|V|+1)),\beta k/(50(|V|+1))\}
and apply the
2.2
, so

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)\leq\delta+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T)+2\eta\leq\delta+k^{-1}\cdot(1+\delta\cdot\mu^{-1}\cdot\|V\|)+\beta\cdot(\mu^{-1}\|V\|+1).

This completes the proof.
∎

3.3.3
Variance and Chebyshev’s Inequality

Next, we develop the basic theory of (approximate) variance and prove a form of Chebyshev’s Inequality.

Definition and Basic Properties.

Let
X
be a random variable defined by the tuple
(V,n,C)
, where
n\in\mathsf{Log}
,
V
is an explicit set, and
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
. We can define a random variable
X^{2}
by the tuple
(V^{2},n,C^{2})
, where
V^{2}\triangleq\{v^{2}\mid v\in V\}
and
C^{2}(x)\triangleq(C(x))^{2}
. Similarly, we can define a random variable
X-\mu
for any
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
by the tuple
(V-\mu,n,C_{-\mu})
, where
V-\mu\triangleq\{v-\mu\mid v\in V\}
and
C_{-\mu}(x)\triangleq C(x)-\mu
. We can then define:

Definition 3.22

(Approximate Variance)
.

Let
X
be a random variable defined by the tuple
(V,n,C)
,
\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. The approximate variance of
X
with precision parameter
\delta
, denoted by
\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X]
, is defined as

\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X]\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[(X-\mu)^{2}]

where
\mu\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]\in\mathbb{Q}
.

As an example, we prove an analogy of the equality
\mathsf{Var}[X]=\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[X]^{2}
for approximate variance by directly formalizing the standard proof in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
.

Proposition 3.23
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
X
be a random variable defined by the tuple
(V,n,C)
, where
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
is an explicit set,
n\in\mathsf{Log}
, and
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
satisfies
\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C(x)\in V
. Let
\mu\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]\in\mathbb{Q}
. Then for any
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,

|\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X]-(\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X^{2}]-\mu^{2})|\leq(2\delta+\beta)\cdot(1+|\mu|)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|,

where
\hat{V}=V\cup(V-\mu)^{2}\cup V^{2}\cup\{1\}
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
,
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, and
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
. Let
\hat{V}=V\cup(V-\mu)^{2}\cup V^{2}\cup\{1\}
be an explicit set, and
\mu\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]
. We define random variables
\hat{Y}
,
\hat{X}
,
\hat{X}^{2}
over
\hat{V}
as follows:

•

\hat{Y}
is the random variable that outputs
(X-\mu)^{2}
. Formally, let
Y(x)\triangleq(C(x)-\mu)^{2}
be a circuit. We can prove that
Y(x)\in\hat{V}
from the assumption
C(x)\in V
for any
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. We then define
\hat{Y}
by the tuple
(\hat{V},n,Y)
.

•

\hat{X}
be the random variable that outputs
X
. Formally, it is defined by the tuple
(\hat{V},n,C)
.

•

\hat{X}^{2}
be the random variable that outputs
X^{2}
. Formally, let
C^{2}(x)\triangleq(C(x))^{2}
be a circuit. We can prove that
C^{2}(x)\in\hat{V}
from the assumption
C(x)\in V
for any
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. We then define
\hat{X}^{2}
by the tuple
(\hat{V},n,C^{2})
.

Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be determined later. By the definition of the circuits
Y
,
C
,
C^{2}
, it is clear that

Y(x)=\mu^{2}+1\cdot C^{2}(x)+(-2\mu)\cdot C(x).

By the
3.2.5
, we can see that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}]-(\mu^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}^{2}]-2\mu\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}])\right|\leq 6\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|\cdot(|\mu|+1).

(3.23)

By the
3.2.2
, we can also conclude that
