\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[(X-\mu)^{2}]\right|,\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X^{2}]\right|,\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X]\right|\leq 3\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|.

(3.24)

By the triangle inequality and the
3.2.2
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X]-(\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X^{2}]-\mu^{2})|

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[(X-\mu)^{2}]-(\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X^{2}]-\mu^{2})|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[(X-\mu)^{2}]-(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X^{2}]-\mu^{2})|+2\cdot(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|

(
Proposition

3.13
)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}^{2}]+\mu^{2}|+(2\delta+10\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|

(
Equation

3.24
)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle|\mu^{2}-(2\mu\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}]-\mu^{2})|+(2\delta+10\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+6\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|\cdot(|\mu|+1)

(
Equation

3.23
)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle|\mu^{2}-(2\mu\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X]-\mu^{2})|+(2\delta+10\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+6\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|\cdot(|\mu|+1)+6\eta\cdot|\mu|\cdot\|\hat{V}\|

(
Equation

3.24

)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle|\mu^{2}-(2\mu\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]-\mu^{2})|+(2\delta+10\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+6\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|\cdot(|\mu|+1)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+6\eta\cdot|\mu|\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+2|\mu|\cdot(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|

(
Proposition

3.13
)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle(2\delta+10\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+6\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|\cdot(|\mu|+1)+6\eta\cdot|\mu|\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+2|\mu|\cdot(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|.

(
\mu\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]
)

The theorem then follows by taking
\eta=\beta/40
.
∎

Chebyshev’s Inequality.

We now prove a form of Chebyshev’s inequality that provides a tail bound for random variables with known (approximate) variance. Formally:

Theorem 3.24

(Chebyshev’s Inequality)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
X
be a random variable defined by the tuple
(V,n,C)
,
\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, where
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
is an explicit set,
n\in\mathsf{Log}
, and
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
is a circuit such that
\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C(x)\in V
. Let
\mu\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X]\in\mathbb{Q}
,
\sigma^{2}\triangleq\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X]
, and
T(x)
be the circuit that outputs
1
if
(C(x)-\mu)^{2}\geq k\cdot\sigma^{2}
, and outputs
0
otherwise.

Then for any
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
k\in\mathbb{Q}
, where
k>0
, we have that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)\leq\delta+k^{-1}\cdot(1+\delta\cdot\sigma^{-2}\cdot\|\hat{V}\|)+\beta\cdot(\sigma^{-2}\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+1),

where
\hat{V}=(V-\mu)^{2}=\{(v-\mu)^{2}\mid v\in V\}
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
V,n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1},C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q},\mu\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X],\sigma^{2}\triangleq\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X],\beta^{-1},k
. Let
Y
be the random variable defined by the tuple
(\hat{V},n,S)
, where
S(x)\triangleq(C(x)-\mu)^{2}
. By the definition of approximate variance, we know that

\sigma^{2}=\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X]=\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[(X-\mu)^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y].

By applying
3.3.2
to the random variable
Y
, we can see that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)\leq\delta+k^{-1}\cdot(1+\delta\cdot\sigma^{-2}\cdot\|\hat{V}\|)+\beta\cdot(\sigma^{-2}\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+1).

This completes the proof.
∎

3.3.4
Pairwise Independence and Variance

Now we develop the notion of (almost) pairwise independence, and prove a form of the equality
\mathsf{Var}[X_{1}+\dots+X_{m}]=\mathsf{Var}[X_{1}]+\dots+\mathsf{Var}[X_{m}]
for pairwise independent random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
. This combined with Chebyshev’s inequality serve as a standard technique to reduce the error probability of randomized algorithms.

Definition of (Almost) Independence.

We start with the definition of (almost) independence of random variables. Let
X_{1},X_{2}
be random variables over
V_{1},V_{2}
, respectively. Recall that the covariance of
X
and
Y
, denoted by
\mathsf{Cov}(X,Y)
, is defined as the quantity
\mathbb{E}[X\cdot Y]-\mathbb{E}[X]\cdot\mathbb{E}[Y]
, where
X\cdot Y
is a random variable over
V_{1}V_{2}\triangleq\{v_{1}\cdot v_{2}\mid v_{1}\in V_{1},v_{2}\in V_{2}\}
. Formally:

Definition 3.25

(Covariance)
.

Let
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
be the random variables defined by the tuples
(V_{1},n,C_{1})
and
(V_{2},n,C_{2})
, respectively, where
V_{1},V_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
are explicit sets,
n\in\mathsf{Log}
, and
C_{1},C_{2}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
are circuits. Let
\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
V_{1}V_{2}\triangleq\{v_{1}\cdot v_{2}\mid v_{1}\in V_{1},v_{2}\in V_{2}\}
,
Y(x)
be the circuit computing
C_{1}(x)\cdot C_{2}(x)
, and
Y
be the random variable defined by the tuple
(V_{1}V_{2},n,Y)
. The
\delta
-approximate covariance of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, denoted by
\mathsf{Cov}_{\delta}(X_{1},X_{2})
, is defined as

\mathsf{Cov}_{\delta}(X_{1},X_{2})\triangleq|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{2}]|.

Definition 3.26

(Almost Independence)
.

Let
\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
. Random variables
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are said to be
\varepsilon
-almost
\delta
-approximately independent if
\mathsf{Cov}_{\delta}(X_{1},X_{2})\leq\varepsilon
.

We can then define the pairwise independence of a sequence of random variables.

Definition 3.27

(Pairwise Independence)
.

Let
n,m\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
be circuits, and
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
be an explicit set such that
\forall i\in[m]~\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C_{i}(x)\in V
. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be random variables, where for each
i\in[m]
,
X_{i}
is defined by the tuple
(V,n,C_{i})
, and let
\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
. The sequence
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
of random variables is said to be
\varepsilon
-almost
\delta
-approximately pairwise independent if for every pair
(i,j)
with
i,j\in[m]
and
i\neq j
,
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
are
\varepsilon
-almost
\delta
-approximately independent.

We may drop the parameter
\varepsilon
and simply say
\delta
-approximately independent if
\varepsilon=0
. Note that since the approximate expectation of random variables may incur an error, random variables
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
may not be perfectly independent even if
\varepsilon=0
.

Sum of Pairwise Independent Variables.

Now we are ready to prove the following result: Suppose that
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are almost pairwise independent, and
Y=X_{1}+\dots+X_{m}
. Then the variance of
Y
is close to the sum of the variances of
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{m}
. Formally:

Theorem 3.28
.

The following statement is provable in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Let
n,m\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
be circuits, and
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
be an explicit set such that the following holds:

•

\forall i\in[m]~\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C_{i}(x)\in V
;

•

\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C_{1}(x)+C_{2}(x)+\dots+C_{m}(x)\in V
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be random variables, where for each
i\in[m]
,
X_{i}
is defined by the tuple
(V,n,C_{i})
. Let
Y
be the random variable defined by the tuple
(V,n,S)
, where
S(x)
is the circuit computing
C_{1}(x)+C_{2}(x)+\dots+C_{m}(x)
. Let
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
. Suppose that
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are
\varepsilon
-almost
\delta
-approximately pairwise independent. Then

\left|\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[Y]-(\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\dots+\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X_{m}])\right|\leq(\varepsilon+3\delta\cdot\|V\|^{2})\cdot m^{2}+\beta\cdot(\|V\|+1)^{3},

where
\|V\|=\sum_{v\in V}|v|
is the
\ell_{1}
-norm of
V
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
n,m
, circuits
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
,
V,\delta^{-1},\varepsilon^{-1},\beta^{-1}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a precision parameter to be determined later,
\mu\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]
, and
\mu_{i}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]
for
i\in[m]
.

Overview of the proof.

Recall that by
Proposition

3.23
, we have that

\left|\mathsf{Var}_{\eta}[Y]-(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y^{2}]-\mu^{2})\right|\leq 3\eta\cdot(1+|\mu|)\cdot\|\hat{V}_{Y}\|,

(3.25)

where
\hat{V}_{Y}=V\cup(V-\mu)^{2}\cup V^{2}\cup\{1\}
. Similarly, for each
i\in[m]
, we have that

\left|\mathsf{Var}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}^{2}]-\mu_{i}^{2})\right|\leq 3\eta\cdot(1+|\mu_{i}|)\cdot\|\hat{V}_{i}\|,

(3.26)

where
\hat{V}_{i}=V\cup(V-\mu_{i})^{2}\cup V^{2}\cup\{1\}
. Therefore, it suffices to bound

\Delta\triangleq|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y^{2}]-(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}^{2}]+\dots+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{m}^{2}])-\mu^{2}+(\mu_{1}^{2}+\dots\mu_{m}^{2})|

(3.27)

and combine it with
Equation

3.25
and (
3.26
). At a high level, our plan is to apply the
3.2.5
to prove that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y^{2}]
is close to

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}^{2}]+\sum_{i,j\in[m],i\neq j}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}X_{j}],

(3.28)

which is subsequently close to

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}^{2}]+\sum_{i,j\in[m],i\neq j}\mu_{i}\mu_{j}

by the almost pairwise independence of
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
. Finally, we can apply the
3.2.5
to show that

\mu_{1}^{2}+\dots+\mu_{m}^{2}+\sum_{i,j\in[m],i\neq j}\mu_{i}\mu_{j}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{i}\right)^{2}\approx\mu^{2}.

Putting the estimates together provides the upper bound for
\Delta
.

Step 1: Approximation of
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y^{2}]
.

We first show that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y^{2}]
is close to
Equation

3.28
. Recall that
Y^{2}
,
X^{2}
, and
X_{i}X_{j}
are the random variables over
V^{2}
defined as follows:

•

Y^{2}
is defined by the tuple
(V^{2},n,S^{2})
, where
S^{2}(x)\triangleq(S(x))^{2}
.

•

For
i\in[m]
,
X_{i}^{2}
is defined by the tuple
(V^{2},n,C_{i}^{2})
, where
C_{i}^{2}(x)\triangleq(C_{i}(x))^{2}
.

•

For
i,j\in[m]
such that
i\neq j
,
X_{i}X_{j}
is defined by the tuple
(V^{2},n,C_{ij})
, where
C_{ij}(x)\triangleq C_{i}(x)\cdot C_{j}(x)
.

It is clear from the definition of the terms that for any
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, we have

S^{2}(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}C_{i}^{2}(z)+\sum_{i,j\in[m],i\neq j}C_{ij}(z).

Thus by the
3.2.5
, we have

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y^{2}]-\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}^{2}]+\sum_{i,j\in[m],i\neq j}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}X_{j}]\right)\right|\leq 3\eta\cdot\|V^{2}\|\cdot(m^{2}+m+1)\leq 9\eta\cdot m^{2}\cdot\|V^{2}\|.

(3.29)

Step 2: Applying pairwise independence.

In this step we show that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}X_{j}]
is close to
\mu_{i}\mu_{j}
for any
i,j\in[m]
. Recall that
\mu_{i}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]
. By the
3.2.2
, we have

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}X_{j}]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}X_{j}]\right|\leq(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V^{2}\|

for any
i,j\in[m]
,
i\neq j
. Moreover, since
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are
\varepsilon
-almost
\delta
-approximately pairwise independent, we know that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}X_{j}]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{j}]\right|\leq\varepsilon.

For each
i\in[m]
, we have that
|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]|\leq(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V\|
, which implies that

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{j}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{j}]\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{j}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{j}]\right|+\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{j}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{j}]\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V\|\cdot(|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]|+|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{j}]|)\leq 2(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V\|^{2}.

Combining the equations above, we have that

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}X_{j}]-\mu_{i}\mu_{j}|\leq\varepsilon+(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V^{2}\|+2(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V\|^{2}\leq\varepsilon+3(\delta+2\eta)\|V\|^{2}.

(3.30)

Step 3: Applying linearity of expectation.

The last step is to prove that
\mu\approx\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{m}
. Recall that
\mu\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]
and
\mu_{i}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]
, where
Y
is defined by the tuple
(V,n,S)
and
X_{i}
is defined by the tuple
(V,n,C_{i})
. It is clear from the definition of
S
and
C_{i}
that for any
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

S(z)=C_{1}(z)+C_{2}(z)+\dots+C_{m}(z).

Therefore, by the
3.2.5
, we have that

|\mu-(\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{m})|=|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]-(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]+\dots+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{m}])|\leq 3\eta\cdot\|V\|\cdot(m+1).

(3.31)

Subsequently, we can see that

\displaystyle\left|\mu^{2}-(\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{m})^{2}\right|=~

\displaystyle|\mu+(\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{m})|\cdot|\mu-(\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{m})|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle(2|\mu|+3\eta\cdot\|V\|\cdot(m+1))\cdot 3\eta\cdot\|V\|\cdot(m+1)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 6|\mu|\cdot\eta\cdot 2m\cdot\|V\|+9\eta^{2}\cdot(2m)^{2}\cdot\|V\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 4\cdot(9\eta^{2}+6\eta)\cdot m^{2}\cdot\|V\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 60\cdot\eta\cdot(m\|V\|)^{2},

(3.32)

where the last inequality holds as we will take
\eta\leq 1
.

Wrapping things up.

Combining
Equation

3.29
, (
3.30
) and (
3.32
), we can see that

\displaystyle\Delta=~

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y^{2}]-(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}^{2}]+\dots+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{m}^{2}])-\mu^{2}+(\mu_{1}^{2}+\dots\mu_{m}^{2})|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i,j\in[m],i\neq j}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}X_{j}]-\mu^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{i}^{2}\right|+9\eta\cdot m^{2}\cdot\|V\|^{2}

(
Equation

3.29
)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i,j\in[m],i\neq j}\mu_{i}\mu_{j}-\mu^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{i}^{2}\right|+9\eta\cdot m^{2}\cdot\|V\|^{2}+m^{2}(\varepsilon+3(\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V\|^{2})

(
Equation

3.30
)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\left|-\mu^{2}+\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{i}\right)^{2}\right|+(\varepsilon+3\delta\cdot\|V\|^{2})\cdot m^{2}+20\cdot\eta\cdot(m\|V\|)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 60\cdot\eta\cdot(m\|V\|)^{2}+(\varepsilon+3\delta\cdot\|V\|^{2})\cdot m^{2}+20\cdot\eta\cdot(m\|V\|)^{2}

(
Equation

3.32
)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle(\varepsilon+3\delta\cdot\|V\|^{2})\cdot m^{2}+80\cdot\eta\cdot(m\|V\|)^{2}.

Finally, we combine this with
Equation

3.25
and (
3.26
), which gives

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[Y]-(\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\dots+\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X_{m}])\right|\leq\Delta+3\eta\cdot(1+|\mu|)\cdot\|\hat{V}_{Y}\|+3\eta\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}(1+|\mu_{i}|)\cdot\|\hat{V}_{i}\|.

(3.33)

Note that

\displaystyle|\mu|\leq\|\hat{V}_{Y}\|\leq~

\displaystyle\|V\|+\|(V-\mu)^{2}\|+\|V^{2}\|+1

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\|V\|+\|V\|^{2}+1+\sum_{v\in V}(v-\mu)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\|V\|+\|V\|^{2}+1+\|V\|^{2}+|V|\cdot\mu^{2}+2\mu\cdot\|V\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2\cdot\|V\|^{3}+(|V|+2)\cdot\|V\|^{2}+\|V\|+1=8\cdot|V|\cdot(\|V\|+1)^{3}.

Similarly, we have that
|\mu_{i}|\leq\|\hat{V}_{i}\|\leq 8\cdot|V|\cdot(\|V\|+1)^{3}
.

Let
\eta\triangleq\min\{\beta/(2000\cdot m^{2}\cdot|V|),1/10\}\leq 1
. By combining
Equation

3.33
and the upper bound above, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[Y]-(\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X_{1}]+\dots+\mathsf{Var}_{\delta}[X_{m}])\right|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle(\varepsilon+3\delta\cdot\|V\|^{2})\cdot m^{2}+80\cdot\eta\cdot(m\|V\|)^{2}+1000\cdot m\cdot\eta\cdot|V|\cdot(\|V\|+1)^{3}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle(\varepsilon+3\delta\cdot\|V\|^{2})\cdot m^{2}+\beta\cdot(\|V\|+1)^{3}.

This completes the proof.
∎

3.4
Independence, Error Reduction, and Concentration Bounds

We now consider the provability in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
of concentration bounds for independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) random variables, which are important tools in combinatorics, probability, and the analysis of randomized algorithms.

3.4.1
Explicit Independence

Before stating and proving the concentration bounds, we formally define the way we formulate independent and identically distributed random variables, and prove a form of the multiplication principle for approximate counting.

Formalization of i.i.d. RVs.

We will only consider random variables that are “explicitly” i.i.d., in the sense that they are defined by the same sampling algorithm using disjoint parts of the random seed. This suffices for our applications and greatly simplifies the calculation of parameters for approximate counting. We first formally define explicit independence of random variables as follows:

Definition 3.29

(Explicit Independence)
.

Let
X
and
X^{\prime}
be random variables defined by
(V,n,C)
and
(V,n,C^{\prime})
, respectively. We say that
X
and
X^{\prime}
are
explicitly independent
if
C
and
C^{\prime}
read disjoint bits of the
n
-bit seed; that is, there is a partition
\pi_{1}\cup\pi_{2}
of
[n]
such that for any seed
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
C
only reads
x_{\pi_{1}}
and
C^{\prime}
only reads
x_{\pi_{2}}
, where
x_{\pi}
denotes the bits of
x
with indices in
\pi
.

Similarly, we define explicitly i.i.d. random variables as follows:

Definition 3.30

(Explicitly i.i.d. RVs)
.

Let
n,m\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
be a circuit,
V\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
be an explicit set such that
\forall x\in\{0,1\}^{n}~C(x)\in V
. The explicitly i.i.d. random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
defined by the tuple
(V,n,C)
are obtained as follows.

Let
C_{i}(\cdot):\{0,1\}^{nm}\to V
be the circuit such that for any
\overline{x}=x_{1}\circ x_{2}\circ\dots\circ x_{m}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, where
x_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
for each
i\in[m]
,
C_{i}(\overline{x})\triangleq C(x_{i})
. For each
i\in[m]
, the random variable
X_{i}
is defined by the tuple
(V,nm,C_{i})
.

3.4.2
Multiplication Principle

We will need a form of multiplication principle: For any explicitly independent random variables
X
and
Y
, we have
\Ex[XY]\approx\Ex[X]\cdot\Ex[Y]
, or equivalently,
\mathsf{Cov}(X,Y)
is small. In other words, explicitly independent random variables are approximately independent. Formally:

Theorem 3.31

(Multiplication Principle)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
V_{1},V_{2}
be explicit sets,
C_{1},C_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}
be circuits. Suppose that the random variables
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, defined by the tuples
(V_{1},n,C_{1})
and
(V_{2},n,C_{2})
, respectively, are explicitly independent. Then for any
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,

\mathsf{Cov}_{\delta}(X,Y)=|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}X_{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{2}]|\leq(2\delta+\beta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+(4\delta+\beta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|^{2},

where
\hat{V}\triangleq V_{1}\cup V_{2}\cup V_{1}V_{2}
, and
V_{1}V_{2}\triangleq\{v_{1}v_{2}\mid v_{1}\in V_{1},v_{2}\in V_{2}\}
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
V_{1},V_{2},C_{1},C_{2}
, and
\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be determined later and
\hat{V}\triangleq V_{1}\cup V_{2}\cup V_{1}V_{2}
be an explicit set.

Overview of the proof.

At a high level, the proof goes as follows. Let
\pi_{1}\cup\pi_{2}
be a partition of
[n]
such that for any seed
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
X_{1}
only reads
x_{\pi_{1}}
and
X_{2}
only reads
x_{\pi_{2}}
. Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
|\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X_{2}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[X_{2}]|
is large. By the
3.17
, we may find an assignment
\rho_{1}
to the part
x_{\pi_{1}}
of the seed such that

\left|\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[X_{2}]\right|

is large. More formally, we are applying
3.17
by treating
X_{1}X_{2}
and
X_{1}
as random variables with coefficients
1
and
-\mathbb{E}[X_{2}]
, respectively. Note that since
X_{2}
does not read the part
x_{\pi_{1}}
of the seed, we know that
\mathbb{E}[X_{2}]
is close to
\mathbb{E}[X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}]
, and subsequently

\left|\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}]\right|

is also large.

Next, we apply the
3.17
again by treating
X_{1}X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}
and
X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}
as random variables with coefficients
1
and
-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]
, respectively, using the seed
x_{\pi_{2}}
. This gives an assignment
\rho_{2}
to
x_{\pi_{2}}
such that

\left|\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]\right|.

is large. Note that, again, since
X_{1}
does not read the part
x_{\pi_{2}}
of the seed, we know that
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]
is close to
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]
, and subsequently

\left|\mathbb{E}[X_{1}X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]\right|.

is also large. However, this is impossible as
X_{1}X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}
,
X_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}
, and
X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}
are random variables with seed length
0
and are supposed to satisfy
X_{1}X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}=X_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}\cdot X_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}
by definition.

We now prove the theorem in detail. Note that we will implement the proof idea above in backward direction for simplicity of calculation.

Step 1: Averaging argument after fixing
\rho_{1}
.

Let
\hat{Y},\hat{X}_{1},\hat{X}_{2}
be the random variables over
\hat{V}\triangleq V_{1}\cup V_{2}\cup V_{1}V_{2}
obtained from
X_{1}X_{2}
,
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
via support extension. Let
\pi_{1}\cup\pi_{2}
be a partition of
[n]
such that for any seed
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
C_{1}
only reads
x_{\pi_{1}}
and
C_{2}
only reads
x_{\pi_{2}}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. It is clear that for any fixed assignments
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
to
x_{\pi_{1}},x_{\pi_{2}}
, respectively,

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]=\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]

(3.34)

by the definition of the random variables.

Note that
\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}
is a random variable that does not read the its seed
x_{\rho_{2}}
. Therefore, for any fixed
\rho_{2},\rho_{2}^{\prime}
,
|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}^{\prime}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]|=0
. By the
3.17
, we have that for any
\rho_{2}
,

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]|\leq 3\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|,

(3.35)

and subsequently by
Equation

3.34
,

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]|\leq 3\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]\leq 3\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|^{2}.

(3.36)

Therefore, by the
3.17
, we have that for any fixed assignment
\rho_{1}
to
x_{\pi_{1}}
,

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}|_{\rho_{1}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}]|\leq 3\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|\cdot(1+|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]|)\leq 3\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|\cdot(1+\|\hat{V}\|).

(3.37)

Note that here we treat
\hat{Y}|_{\rho_{1}}
,
\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}
as random variables, and
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]
as a coefficient.

Step 2: Averaging argument again.

Similarly to
Equation

3.35
, we will first show that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}]
is close to
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}]
for any
\rho_{1}
. We can see that for any assignments
\rho_{1},\rho_{1}^{\prime}
to
x_{\pi_{1}}
and
\rho_{2}
to
x_{\pi_{2}}
,
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}|_{\rho_{2}}]=\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}^{\prime}}|_{\rho_{2}}]
as
\hat{X}_{2}
does not read
x_{\pi_{1}}
. Therefore, by the
3.17
, for every
\rho_{1},\rho_{1}^{\prime}
,

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}^{\prime}}]|\leq 6\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|\cdot

Again, by the
3.17
applied to
\hat{X}_{2}
, we get
\rho_{1}^{\prime}
such that

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}]|\leq 3\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}^{\prime}}]|\leq 9\eta\cdot\|V\|.

Combining this with
Equation

3.37
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}|_{\rho_{1}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}]|\leq|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}|_{\rho_{1}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}|_{\rho_{1}}]|+9\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 12\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|^{2}+3\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|.

(3.38)

By applying the
3.17
on
Equation

3.38
with random variables
\hat{Y}
and
\hat{X}_{1}
, we have that

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}]|\leq|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}|_{\rho_{1}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}|_{\rho_{1}}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}]|+3\eta\cdot\|V\|\cdot(1+\|V\|)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 15\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|^{2}+6\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|.

(3.39)

Wrapping things up.

Note that by the
3.2.2
, we have that

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}X_{2}]|,|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]|,|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]|\leq 3\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|.

By the
3.2.2
, we have

|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}X_{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}X_{2}]|,|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]|,|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]|\leq(2\delta+\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|.

We can therefore deduce from
Equation

3.39
that

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}X_{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{2}]|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}X_{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]|+(2\delta+\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+(4\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}]|+(2\delta+\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+(4\delta+2\eta)\cdot\|V\|^{2}+3\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+6\eta\cdot\|\hat{V}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{X}_{2}]|+(2\delta+4\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+(4\delta+8\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}^{2}\|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle(2\delta+10\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|+(4\delta+23\eta)\cdot\|\hat{V}\|^{2}.

This completes the proof by setting
\eta=\beta/100
.
∎

Subsequently, we can obtain the following more convenient form of the multiplication principle for explicitly independent Bernoulli random variables:

Corollary 3.32

(Multiplication Principle for Bernoulli RVs)
.

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,m,\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be explicitly independent random variables over
\{0,1\}
with seed length
n
. Then

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{m}]-\prod_{j\in[i]}\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]\right|\leq 8\delta\cdot m,

where the random variable
X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{m}
is defined in the natural way.

Proof.

We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Fix
n,m,\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. We prove by induction on
i
such that for every
i\in[m]
, we have

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{i}]-\prod_{j\in[i]}\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]\right|\leq 8\delta\cdot i.

(3.40)

Note that this employs induction on a quantifier-free formula, which is available in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. The base case
i=1
is straightforward. Suppose that
Equation

3.40
holds. By the
3.4.2
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{i}X_{i+1}]-\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{i}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i+1}]\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq 3\delta\cdot\|V\|+5\delta\cdot\|V\|^{2}\leq 8\delta,

(3.41)

and subsequently

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{i}X_{i+1}]-\prod_{j\in[i+1]}\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]\right|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{i}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i+1}]-\prod_{j\in[i+1]}\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]\right|+8\delta

(
Equation

3.41
)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{i}]-\prod_{j\in[i]}\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]\right|\cdot|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i+1}]|+8\delta

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 8\delta\cdot i\cdot\|V\|+8\delta\leq 8\delta\cdot(i+1).

(Induction Hypothesis)

This completes the proof.
∎

3.4.3
Error Reduction for One-Sided Error Statements

The multiplication principle allows us to prove the correctness of error reduction via repetition for one-sided error algorithms. Specifically, for any circuit
C
that accepts a
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction of its inputs, the circuit
C^{\lor k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=\bigvee_{i\in[k]}C(x_{i})
accepts all but an
\varepsilon^{k}
-fraction its inputs.

This is formalized as the following theorem:

Theorem 3.33

(One-sided error reduction lemma)
.

Let
C^{\lor k}:\{0,1\}^{nk}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit defined as
C^{\lor k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\triangleq\bigvee_{i\in[k]}C(x_{i})
for any circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
. The following statement is provable in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. For any
n,k,\delta^{-1},\gamma^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
, if
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\lnot C)\leq\varepsilon
and
\gamma\geq(\delta+\beta+\varepsilon)^{k}+\delta+\beta
, then
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\lnot C^{\lor k})\leq\gamma
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\gamma^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and a circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
. Let
V\triangleq\{0,1\}
, and let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. Let
X_{i}
be the random variable that takes an
nk
-bit seed
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in(\{0,1\}^{n})^{k}
and outputs
\lnot C(x_{i})
for
i\in[k]
. It is clear that
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are explicit independent random variables. Let
Y
be the random variable that takes an
nk
-bit seed
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in(\{0,1\}^{n})^{k}
and outputs
\lnot C^{\lor k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
.

RVs and approximate counting.

It is clear that
Y
is the indicator random variable for the circuit
\lnot C^{\lor k}
, and thus by
Proposition

3.12
,
|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\lnot C^{\lor k})|\leq 3\eta
.

We will prove that for every
i\in[k]
,
|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\lnot C)|\leq 6\eta
. Fix any
i\in[k]
and let
\pi_{i}\cup\overline{\pi_{i}}=[nk]
, where
\pi_{i}
denotes the set of indices corresponding to the
i
-th
n
-bit block that
X_{i}
reads, and
\overline{\pi_{i}}
denotes the other
(k-1)n
indices. For every assignment
\rho
to
\overline{\pi_{i}}
, we can see that
X_{i}|_{\rho}
is the indicator random variable for
C
, and thus by
Proposition

3.12
,

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}|_{\rho}]-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\lnot C)|\leq 3\eta.

Subsequently, we can apply the
3.17
to prove that

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\lnot C)|\leq 6\eta.

(3.42)

Note that by the assumption, we have that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\lnot C)\leq\varepsilon
. By the
2.2
for approximate counting, we have
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)\leq\delta+2\eta+\varepsilon
, and thus

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)+6\eta\leq\delta+8\eta+\varepsilon

(3.43)

for every
i\in[k]
.

Wrapping things up.

We first prove that
|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{k}]|\leq 6\eta
. Note that
Y
and
X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{k}
are both random variables taking
nk
-bit seeds, and for every assignment
\rho
to the seeds, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y|_{\rho}]=\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{k}|_{\rho}]
. By the
3.17
, we have

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{k}]|\leq 3\eta\cdot\|V\|\cdot 2\leq 6\eta.

(3.44)

Additionally, by the
2.2
of approximate counting, we have

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\lnot C^{\lor k})\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\lnot C^{\lor k})+\delta+2\eta.

Let
\eta\triangleq\beta/(20k)
. Recall that it suffices to prove
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]\leq\gamma
. It follows that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(\lnot C^{\lor k})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\lnot C^{\lor k})+\delta+2\eta

(
2.2
)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]+\delta+5\eta

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}X_{2}\dots X_{k}]+\delta+12\eta

(
Equation

3.44
)

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]+\delta+8\eta k+12\eta

(
3.4.2
)

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i\in[k]}(\delta+8\eta+\varepsilon)+\delta+8\eta k+12\eta

(
Equation

3.43
)

\displaystyle\leq(\delta+\beta+\varepsilon)^{k}+\delta+\beta\leq\gamma,

which completes the proof.
∎

3.4.4
Chernoff Bound for
O(\log n)
Random Variables

We consider a form of the Chernoff bound where the number of random variables
m\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
. In a nutshell, we formalize a combinatorial proof using Binomial coefficients due to Chvátal (see, e.g.,
(
70
, Section 3.2)
).

Definition 3.34

(Binomial coefficient, in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
)
.

Let
n,m\in\mathsf{Log}
and
m\leq n
. The binomial coefficient
\binom{n}{m}
is defined recursively as:

\binom{n}{0}\triangleq 1,\;\binom{n}{m}\triangleq\binom{n-1}{m-1}+\binom{n-1}{m}.

(3.45)

For
m>n
, we let
\binom{n}{m}\triangleq 0
.
Note that the function computing
(1^{n},1^{m})\mapsto\binom{n}{m}
can be defined by a
\mathsf{PV}
function that runs in
{\mathsf{poly}}(n)
time, such that
Equation

3.45
is provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
.

Lemma 3.35

(Binomial theorem)
.

The following statement is provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. For every
n\in\mathsf{Log}
and
x,y\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0\}
,

(x+y)^{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{n}{i}x^{i}y^{n-i}.

Proof Sketch.

Fix
n\in\mathsf{Log}
and
x,y\in\mathbb{Q}
. We prove by induction on
n\in\mathsf{Log}
. In the base case, the equation trivially holds for
n=0
as both sides are
1
. The induction step follows from
Equation

3.45
.
∎

Now we are ready to prove the Chernoff bound when
n\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
.

Theorem 3.36

(Chernoff bound I,
\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
form)
.

The following sentence is provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Let
V=\{0,1\}
,
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, and
m\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be a sequence of explicit i.i.d. random variables over
V
defined by a tuple
(V,n,C)
and taking an
nm
-bit seed
(z_{1},\dots,z_{m})\in(\{0,1\}^{n})^{m}
. Let
p\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]\in\mathbb{Q}
for any
i\in[m]
, and
Y_{\geq k}
be the indicator variable of
X_{1}+\dots+X_{m}\geq k
that takes an
nm
-bit seed. Then for
t\in\mathbb{Q}
,
0\leq t\leq 1
, and
k\geq(1+t)pm
,

\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y_{\geq k}]\leq\left(e^{-t^{2}p/4}+(4\delta+\beta)\cdot 2^{-pt(1+t)}\right)^{m}+\delta+\beta.

Proof.

We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Fix
V=\{0,1\}
,
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
m\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
, and
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
. Let
p\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]
,
t,k
, and
Y_{\geq k}
be defined as above. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. By the
3.2.2
,

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-p|=|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]|\leq\delta+2\eta.

(3.46)

Probability of each subset.

Let
\alpha\subseteq[m]
encode a subset of variables. We define the random variable
Y_{\alpha}
over
\{0,1\}
as follows: Given any seed
(z_{1},\dots,z_{m})\in(\{0,1\}^{n})^{m}
,
Y_{\alpha}=1
if and only if for every
i\in\alpha
,
X_{i}=1
. In the first step, we show that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{\alpha}]\leq(p+\delta+O(\eta m))^{|\alpha|}

(3.47)

for each
\alpha\subseteq[m]
. Note that as
m\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
, we can define all random variables
Y_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\subseteq[m]
by an explicit list of circuits. This will be useful later in the proof.

Fix any
\alpha=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{t}\}\subseteq[m]
. As
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are explicitly independent random variables, by the
3.4.2
, we have that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}\left[\prod_{j\in[t]}X_{i_{j}}\right]-\prod_{j\in[t]}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i_{j}}]\right|\leq 8\eta m.

Subsequently, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}\left[\prod_{j\in[t]}X_{i_{j}}\right]\leq 8\eta m+(p+\delta+2\eta)^{|\alpha|}.

Note that for every assignment
\rho
to the random seed,
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{\alpha}|_{\rho}]=\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\prod_{j\in[t]}X_{i_{j}}|_{\rho}]
. By the
3.17
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{\alpha}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}\left[\prod_{j\in[t]}X_{i_{j}}\right]+6\eta\leq 14\eta m+(p+\delta+2\eta)^{|\alpha|}.

(3.48)

Let
\overline{Y}_{\alpha}
be the random variable defined over
\{0,1\}
as follows: Given any seed
(z_{1},\dots,z_{m})\in(\{0,1\}^{n})^{m}
,
Y_{\alpha}=1
if and only if for every
i\in[m]\setminus\alpha
,
X_{i}=0
. Similar to the proof above, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}\left[\overline{Y}_{\alpha}\right]\leq(1-p+\delta+10\eta m)^{m-|\alpha|}+20\eta m.

(3.49)

Combining all subsets.

Let
\hat{Y}_{\geq k}
be the following random variable over
\{0,1\}
:

\hat{Y}_{\geq k}\triangleq\sum_{\alpha\subseteq[m],|\alpha|\geq k}Y_{\alpha}\overline{Y}_{\alpha}.

By applying the
3.4.2
and
3.2.5
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\quad\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}_{\geq k}]

\displaystyle\leq 3\eta\cdot 2^{m}+\sum_{\alpha\subseteq[m],|\alpha|\geq k}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{\alpha}\overline{Y}_{\alpha}]

(
3.3.1
)

\displaystyle\leq 3\eta\cdot 2^{m}+8\eta\cdot 2^{m}+\sum_{\alpha\subseteq[m],|\alpha|\geq k}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{\alpha}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[\overline{Y}_{\alpha}]

(
3.4.2
)

\displaystyle\leq 11\eta\cdot 2^{m}+\sum_{\alpha\subseteq[m],|\alpha|\geq k}\left((p+\delta+2\eta m)^{|\alpha|}+14\eta m\right)\left((1-p+\delta+10\eta m)^{m-|\alpha|}+20\eta m\right)

(
Equations

3.48
and
3.49
)

\displaystyle\leq 90\eta m\cdot 2^{m}+\sum_{j\geq k}\binom{m}{j}(p+\delta+2\eta m)^{j}(1-p+\delta+10\eta m)^{m-j}.

Note that the binomial number in the last line is efficiently computable (even using a brute-force counting algorithm) as
m,j\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
.

Recall that
Y_{\geq k}
is the random variable indicating that
X_{1}+\dots+X_{m}\geq k
. Note that for every assignment
\rho
to the random seed, we have that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{\geq k}|_{\rho}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}_{\geq k}|_{\rho}]
. Therefore, by the
3.17
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{\geq k}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\hat{Y}_{\geq k}]+6\eta\leq\sum_{j\geq k}\binom{m}{j}(p+\delta+2\eta m)^{j}(1-p+\delta+10\eta m)^{m-j}+100\eta m\cdot 2^{m}.

(3.50)

Binomial coefficient inequalities.

It remains to prove an upper bound for
Equation

3.50
. Note that as
m\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
, we can easy formalize the standard proof in
(
70
, Section 3.2)
, where all equalities about binomial coefficients can be proved in
\mathsf{PV}
.

Let
\tau=e^{\lambda}\geq 1
be a parameter to be determined later. In more detail, we can perform the following calculation for any
\varepsilon^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq k}\binom{m}{j}(p+\varepsilon)^{j}(1-p+\varepsilon)^{m-j}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq k}\binom{m}{j}(p+\varepsilon)^{j}(1-p+\varepsilon)^{m-j}\tau^{j-k}+\sum_{0\leq j<k}(p+\varepsilon)^{j}(1-p+\varepsilon)^{m-j}\tau^{j-k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau^{-k}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\binom{m}{j}\left((p+\varepsilon)\tau\right)^{j}(1-p+\varepsilon)^{m-j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau^{-k}\big(1+p(\tau-1)+\varepsilon(\tau+1)\big)^{m}

(
3.34
)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1+p(e^{\lambda}-1)+\varepsilon(e^{\lambda}+1)}{e^{\lambda p(1+t)}}\right)^{m}.

Note that for
\lambda\in(0,1)
, we have
1+\lambda\leq e^{\lambda}\leq 1+\lambda+3\lambda^{2}/4
, and this is provable in
\mathsf{PV}
. Then

\displaystyle\ln\left(1+p(e^{\lambda}-1)\right)\leq p(e^{\lambda}-1)\leq p\lambda+3p\lambda^{2}/4.

This implies that
1+p(e^{\lambda}-1)\leq\exp(p\lambda+3p\lambda^{2}/4)
. We set
\varepsilon\triangleq\delta+30\eta m
,
\eta\leq\beta/(120m)
, then
\varepsilon\leq\delta+\beta/4
, and
\lambda\triangleq t
. Then we have

\left(\frac{1+p(e^{\lambda}-1)+\varepsilon(e^{\lambda}+1)}{e^{\lambda p(1+t)}}\right)^{m}\leq\left(e^{-t^{2}p/4}+4(\delta+\beta/4)\cdot e^{-pt(1+t)}\right)^{m}.

Finally, we can obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y_{\geq k}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{\geq k}]+\delta+2\eta

(
3.2.2
)

\displaystyle\leq\left(e^{-t^{2}p/4}+4(\delta+\beta/4)\cdot e^{-pt(1+t)}\right)^{m}+200\eta m\cdot 2^{m}+\delta+2\eta

\displaystyle\leq\left(e^{-t^{2}p/4}+(4\delta+\beta)\cdot e^{-pt(1+t)}\right)^{m}+\delta+\beta,

where the last inequality follows if we set
\eta\triangleq\beta/(1000m\cdot 2^{m})
.
∎

Using essentially the same proof with
\tau=e^{-\lambda}<1
, we can obtain a Chernoff bound for the other side of the tail probability:

Theorem 3.37

(Chernoff bound II,
\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
form)
.

The following sentence is provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Let
V=\{0,1\}
,
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, and
m\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be a sequence of explicit i.i.d. random variables over
V
that takes an
nm
-bit seed
(z_{1},\dots,z_{m})\in(\{0,1\}^{n})^{m}
. Let
p\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]\in\mathbb{Q}
, and
Y_{\leq k}
be the indicator variable of
X_{1}+\dots+X_{m}\leq k
that takes an
nm
-bit seed. Then for
t\in\mathbb{Q}
,
0\leq t\leq 1
, and
k\leq(1-t)pm
,

\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y_{\leq k}]\leq\left(e^{-t^{2}p/4}+(4\delta+\beta)\cdot 2^{-pt(1-t)}\right)^{m}+\delta+\beta.

4
Theoretical Computer Science in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}

In this section, we formalize in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
several fundamental results from algorithms, complexity theory, and related areas.

4.1
Yao’s Distinguisher-to-Predictor Transformation

Theorem 4.1

(Yao’s transformation)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,m,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
G:\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
be a multi-output circuit, and
C\in B_{n}
be a circuit such that

\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C\circ G)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(C)\right|\geq 2\delta+\varepsilon

(4.1)

for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)\cap\mathbb{Q}
, where
C\circ G
is the
m
-input circuit defined as
(C\circ G)(u)\triangleq C(G(u))
.

Then there is an index
i\in[n]
and a circuit
P:\{0,1\}^{i-1}\to\{0,1\}
such that the following holds: Let
T(u)\in B_{m}
be the circuit such that
T(u)=1
if
P(G(u)_{<i})=G(u)_{i}
(i.e.,
P
successfully predicts the
i
-th bit of
G(u)
), then

\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)-\frac{1}{2}\right|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4n}-(\delta+\beta).

Proof.

We formalize the standard proof of Yao’s lemma in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
(see, e.g.,
(
6
, Chapter 9)
). Fix
n,m,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
G:\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
, and circuit
C\in B_{n}
. For every index
i\in\{0,1,\dots,n\}
, we define the circuit
C_{i}:\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
as follows:

•

C_{i}
parses its input as
(u,x)\in\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
.

•

Let
z\triangleq G(u)_{\leq i}\circ x_{>i}
, i.e., the string where the first
i
bits agree with the first
i
bits of
G(u)
, and the remaining bits agree with the last
n-i
bits of
x
. The circuit
C_{i}(u,x)
then outputs
C(z)
.

Let
X_{0},X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be the random variables over
\{0,1\}
with seed length
m+n
, where
X_{i}
is the indicator variable of
C_{i}(j,x)=1
. That is,
X_{i}
is defined by the tuple
(\{0,1\},m+n,C_{i})
.

Step 1: Gap between
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{0}]
and
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{n}]
.

We first argue that
|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{0}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{n}]|
is large. Note that by
Equation

4.1
and the
2.2
, we have

\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C\circ G)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)\right|\geq\varepsilon-4\eta.

(4.2)

Note that
X_{0}
is the random variable over
\{0,1\}
with seed length
m+n
that, on the seed
(u,x)\in\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
, ignores the first part of the seed and outputs
C(x)
. For any assignment
u\in\{0,1\}^{m}
to the first part of the seed, it can be verified that
|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{0}|_{u}]-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)|\leq 3\eta
, and thus by the
3.17
, we know that

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{0}]-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)|\leq 6\eta.

Similarly, we can prove that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{n}]-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C\circ G)\right|\leq 6\eta.

(4.3)

This is because
X_{n}
is the random variable that, on the seed
(u,x)\in\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
, ignores the second part of the seed and outputs
C\circ G(u)
.

By combining
Equation

4.2
and
Equation

4.3
, we have

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{0}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{n}]|\geq\varepsilon-16\eta

(4.4)

Step 2: Gap between
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]
and
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X_{i+1}}]
.

As
|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{0}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{n}]|\geq\beta-16\eta+\varepsilon
, we know that for some
1\leq i\leq n
,

|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{n}-\frac{16\eta}{n}.

(4.5)

In more detail, suppose towards a contradiction that this is not true. We can prove by induction on
j
that if
j\leq n
,
|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{0}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{j}]|<(j/n)\cdot(\varepsilon-16\eta)
.

We will produce a predictor
P
for this index
i
. Recall that both
X_{i-1}
and
X_{i}
are random variables that parse their seeds as
(u,x)\in\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
, and

•

X_{i-1}
outputs
C(G(u)_{<i}\circ x_{i}\circ x_{>i})
;

•

X_{i}
outputs
C(G(u)_{<i}\circ G(u)_{i}\circ x_{>i})
.

Let
\overline{X_{i}}
be the random variable that parses its input as
(u,x)\in\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
and outputs
C(G(u)_{<i}\circ\overline{G(u)_{i}}\circ x_{>i})
. We will prove

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X_{i}}]\right|>\frac{\varepsilon}{2n}-100\eta

(4.6)

by rewriting both
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}]
and
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]
in
Equation

4.5
.

Let
X_{i-1}^{0},X_{i-1}^{1}
be the random variables over
\{0,1\}
that parse their seeds as
(u,x)\in\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
satisfying that:

•

X_{i-1}^{0}
outputs
1
if and only if
C(G(u)_{<i}\circ x_{i}\circ x_{>i})=1
and
G(u)_{i}=x_{i}
.

•

X_{i-1}^{1}
outputs
1
if and only if
C(G(u)_{<i}\circ\overline{G(u)_{i}}\circ x_{>i})=1
and
G(u)_{i}\neq x_{i}
.

One can observe that for any assignment
\rho
to their seeds,
X_{i-1}|_{\rho}=X_{i-1}^{0}|_{\rho}+X_{i-1}^{1}|_{\rho}
, and thus by the
3.2.5
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}]-\left(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}^{0}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}^{1}]\right)\right|\leq 6\eta.

(4.7)

Similarly, let
X_{i}^{0},X_{i}^{1}
be the random variables over
\{0,1\}
that parse their seeds as
(u,x)\in\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
satisfying that:

•

X_{i}^{0}
outputs
1
if and only if
C(G(u)_{<i}\circ x_{i}\circ x_{>i})=1
and
G(u)_{i}=x_{i}
.

•

X_{i}^{1}
outputs
1
if and only if
C(G(u)_{<i}\circ G(u)_{i}\circ x_{>i})=1
and
G(u)_{i}\neq x_{i}
.

For any assignment
\rho
to their seeds,
X_{i}|_{\rho}=X_{i}^{0}|_{\rho}+X_{i}^{1}|_{\rho}
, and thus by the
3.17
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-\left(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}^{0}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}^{1}]\right)\right|\leq 6\eta.

(4.8)

One can observe that
X_{i}^{0}
and
X_{i-1}^{0}
are exactly the same random variable. Moreover, we argue that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-2\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}^{1}]\right|\leq 20\eta.

(4.9)

(As a sanity check,
\mathbb{E}[X_{i}]=2\cdot\mathbb{E}[X^{1}_{i}]
in exact expectation.) Assume for contradiction that this does not hold. By the
3.17
, there is an assignment
\rho
to all but
x_{i}
such that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}|_{\rho}]-2\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}^{1}|_{\rho}]\right|>10\eta.

Note that both
X_{i}|_{\rho}
and
X_{i}^{1}|_{\rho}
have seed length
1
, and we know that
\mathbb{E}[X_{i}|_{\rho}]=2\cdot\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{1}|_{\rho}]
. This leads to a contradiction by the
3.1.4
. Similarly, we can prove that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}]-2\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}^{1}]\right|\leq 10\eta.

(4.10)

By
Equations

4.5
,
4.7
,
4.8
,
4.9
and
4.10
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X_{i}}]\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\left(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}^{0}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}^{1}]\right)-\left(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}^{0}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}^{1}]\right)\right|-12\eta

(
Equations

4.7
and
4.8
)

\displaystyle\geq\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}^{1}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}^{1}]\right|-12\eta

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]\right|}{2}-60\eta

(
Equations

4.9
and
4.10
)

\displaystyle>\frac{\varepsilon}{2n}-100\eta.

(
Equation

4.5
)

Step 3: Producing the Predictor.

We now prove that
Equation

4.6
suffices to produce the predictor. Let
P:\{0,1\}^{i-1}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
and
T:\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be the following circuits:
P(v,x)\triangleq C(v\circ x_{i}\circ x_{>i})\oplus x_{i}
, and
T(u,x)
outputs
1
if and only if
P(G(u)_{<i},x)=G(u)_{i}
.

We also define two circuits
T_{0},T_{1}:\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
such that

•

T_{0}(u,x)
outputs
1
if
C(G(u)_{<i}\circ G(u)_{i}\circ x_{>i})=0
and
x_{i}=G(u)_{i}
.

•

T_{1}(u,x)
outputs
1
if
C(G(u)_{<i}\circ\overline{G(u)_{i}}\circ x_{>i})=1
and
x_{i}\neq G(u)_{i}
.

One can prove (in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
) that
T_{0}(u,x)=1
if and only if
T(u,x)=1
and
x_{i}=G(u)_{i}
, while
T_{1}(u,x)=1
if and only if
T(u,x)=1
and
x_{i}\neq G(u)_{i}
. Therefore for every
(u,x)\in\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
,
T(u,x)=T_{0}(u,x)+T_{1}(u,x)
. By considering their indicator variables and applying the
3.2.5
, one can prove that

\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T)-({\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{0})+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{1}))\right|\leq 50\eta.

(4.11)

Next, we argue that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-\left(1-2\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{0})\right)\right|\leq 20\eta.

(4.12)

Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
Equation

4.12
does not hold. Let
I_{T_{0}}
be the indicator random variable of
T_{0}
, and we have
|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{T_{0}}]-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{0})|\leq 3\eta
(see
Proposition

3.12
). Both
X_{i}
and
I_{T_{0}}
have seed
(u,x)\in\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
. By the
3.17
, there is an assignment
\rho
to all variables but
x_{i}
(i.e. the
i
-th bit of
x
) such that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}|_{\rho}]-\left(1-2\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{T_{0}}|_{\rho}]\right)\right|>10\eta.

Note that both
X_{i}|_{\rho}
and
I_{T_{0}}|_{\rho}
have seed length
1
, and we know by the definition that
17
17

17

Indeed, since after fixing
\rho
the only randomness is
x_{i}
and
X_{i}\!\restriction_{\rho}
is independent of
x_{i}
, we have
I_{T_{0}}\!\restriction_{\rho}=\mathbf{1}\{x_{i}=G(u)_{i}\}\,\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}\!\restriction_{\rho}=0\}=\mathbf{1}\{x_{i}=G(u)_{i}\}\,\bigl(1-X_{i}\!\restriction_{\rho}\bigr)
. Consequently,

\mathbb{E}\!\bigl[I_{T_{0}}\!\restriction_{\rho}\bigr]=\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\bigl(1-X_{i}\!\restriction_{\rho}\bigr)=\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\Bigl(1-\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{i}\!\restriction_{\rho}\bigr]\Bigr)
, which implies that
\mathbb{E}\bigl[X_{i}\!\restriction_{\rho}\bigr]=1-2\cdot\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[I_{T_{0}}\!\restriction_{\rho}\bigr].

\left|\mathbb{E}[X_{i}|_{\rho}]-\left(1-2\cdot\mathbb{E}[I_{T_{0}}|_{\rho}]\right)\right|=0.

By the
3.1.4
, we conclude a contradiction that thus proves
Equation

4.12
. Similarly, we can prove that

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X_{i}}]-2\cdot{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{1})\right|\leq 20\eta.

(4.13)

By combining
Equations

4.6
,
4.11
,
4.12
and
4.13
, we have that

\displaystyle\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T)-\frac{1}{2}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{0})+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{1})-\frac{1}{2}\right|-50\eta

(
Equation

4.11
)

\displaystyle\geq\left|\frac{1-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]}{2}+\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X_{i}}]}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\right|-80\eta

(
Equations

4.12
and
4.13
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X_{i}}]\right|-80\eta

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4n}-180\eta.

(
Equation

4.6
)

Recall that
T
takes an input
(u,x)\in\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
. Let
T_{x}
be the circuit obtained by fixing the second part of its input to be
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. By considering its indicator variable (see
Proposition

3.12
) and applying the
3.17
, there exists a string
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that

\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}[T_{x}]-\frac{1}{2}\right|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4n}-200\eta.

By the definition, one can see that
T_{x}
evaluating to
1
means the predictor
P_{x}(v)\triangleq C(v\circ x\circ x_{>i})\oplus x_{i}
correctly predicts the
i
-th bit of
G(u)
. This concludes the proof by applying the
3.1.1
of approximate counting, the
2.2
, and setting
\eta\triangleq\beta/400
.
∎

4.2
Schwartz-Zippel Lemma

Before stating the Schwartz-Zippel lemma, we need to clarify the formalization of finite fields and polynomials. A finite field
\mathbb{F}
is said to be feasible if
|\mathbb{F}|\in\mathsf{Log}
, e.g.,
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{F}_{p}
for some prime number
p\in\mathsf{Log}
. It is verified in
(
44
, Section 4.3.3)
that for any feasible field, the field elements can be encoded such that (1) the field operations can be implemented by
\mathsf{PV}
function symbols, and (2) field axioms can be proved in
\mathsf{PV}
. For simplicity, we identify an element in
\mathbb{F}
and its encoding as a string.

Fix a feasible field
\mathbb{F}
. A degree-
d
univariate polynomial
p\in\mathbb{F}[x]
can be defined by a list of coefficients
c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{d}\in\mathbb{F}
such that
p(x)\triangleq c_{0}+c_{1}x+\dots+c_{d}x^{d}
. A polynomial is said to be nonzero if any of
c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{d}
is nonzero.

Proposition 4.2

(Implicit in
(
44
, Lemma 4.3.6)
)
.

It is provable in
\mathsf{PV}
that any nonzero degree-
d
polynomial
p\in\mathbb{F}[x]
has at most
d
roots.

Proposition 4.3

(Implicit in
44
)
.

It is provable in
\mathsf{PV}
that for any
d<|\mathbb{F}|
, any distinct
x_{1},\dots,x_{d}\in\mathbb{F}
, and
y_{1},\dots,y_{d}\in\mathbb{F}
, there is a degree-
d
polynomial
p\in\mathbb{F}[x]
such that
p(x_{i})=y_{i}
for every
i\in[d]
.

Let
m,d\in\mathsf{Log}
. We say that a circuit
C

computes an
m
-variate function in
\mathbb{F}
, denoted by
C:\mathbb{F}^{m}\to\mathbb{F}
, if for every
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in\mathbb{F}
,
C(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{F}
. Depending on the encoding of field elements, some circuit may not compute an
m
-variate function in
\mathbb{F}
as it outputs a string that does not encode any field element. We say that
C:\mathbb{F}^{m}\to\mathbb{F}
has
individual degree at most
d
if for every
i\in[m]
and any assignment
\rho
to all but the
i
-th variable, there is a polynomial
p_{i,\rho}\in\mathbb{F}[x]
of degree at most
d
such that
p_{i,\rho}(x)=C|_{\rho}(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{F}
.

Theorem 4.4

(Schwartz-Zippel Lemma)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
\mathbb{F}
be a feasible field such that each field element is encoded by a string of length
b\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
. Let
m,d\in\mathsf{Log}
,
d<|\mathbb{F}|
, and
C:\mathbb{F}^{m}\to\mathbb{F}
be a circuit of individual degree at most
d
. Let
T_{C}:(\{0,1\}^{b})^{m}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that given
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\{0,1\}^{b}
, it accepts if
x_{i}\in\mathbb{F}
for each
i\in[m]
, and
C(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=0
.

Suppose that for some
\vec{z}\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
,
C(\vec{z})\neq 0
. Then for every
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C})\leq md/|\mathbb{F}|+\delta+\beta
.

Proof.

The key idea is to formalize the proof of Atserias and Tzameret
9
in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix any feasible field
\mathbb{F}
,
b\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
,
m,d\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C:\mathbb{F}^{m}\to\mathbb{F}
,
\vec{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{m})\in\mathbb{F}_{m}
, and
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, as in the statement.

Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. For every
i\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
, we define
T_{C}^{i}:(\{0,1\}^{b})^{m}\to\{0,1\}
as follows: Given
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\{0,1\}^{b}
, it accepts if
x_{i}\in\mathbb{F}
for each
i\in[m]
and
C(x_{1},\dots,x_{i},z_{i+1},\dots,z_{m})=0
. It is clear that
T^{0}_{C}\equiv\mathsf{Null}
, and
T^{m}_{C}\equiv T_{C}
.

Let
X_{0},X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be the indicator random variables for
T_{C}^{0},T_{C}^{1},\dots,T_{C}^{m}
. We will prove that for every
i\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}]\leq d/|\mathbb{F}|+10\eta.

(4.14)

Assume for contradiction that it is not the case. By
3.17
, there is an assignment
\rho
to all but the
i
-th part
x_{i}
of the seed such that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}|_{\rho}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}|_{\rho}]>d/|\mathbb{F}|+10\eta-6\eta>0.

(4.15)

Note that
X_{i-1}|_{\rho}
is the indicator random variable of
T_{C}^{i-1}|_{\rho}
, and by the definition,
T_{C}^{i-1}|_{\rho}
is a constant circuit that does not read the seed. Therefore it falls into one of the two cases:

•

Suppose that
T_{C}^{i-1}|_{\rho}\equiv\mathsf{True}
. We also know that the circuit
\mathsf{EQ}(T_{C}^{i-1}|_{\rho},1)
that given
x\in\{0,1\}^{b}
, outputs
1
if and only if
T_{C}^{i-1}(x)=1
is also functionally equivalent to
\mathsf{True}
. One can prove by the definition that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i-1}|_{\rho}]={\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{EQ}(T^{i-1}_{C}|_{\rho},1))=1,

where the second inequality follows from the
2.2
. This leads to a contradiction to
Equation

4.15
.

•

Otherwise,
T_{C}^{i-1}|_{\rho}\equiv\mathsf{Null}
. Let
\rho=(y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1},*,y_{i+1},\dots,y_{m})
. Recall that as the individual degree of
C
is at most
d
, there is a polynomial
p\in\mathbb{F}[x]
such that
p\equiv C(y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1},\cdot,z_{i+1},\dots,z_{m})
. It follows from
T_{C}^{i-1}|_{\rho}\equiv\mathsf{Null}
and
Equation

4.15
that

p(z_{i})=C(y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1},z_{i},z_{i+1},\dots,z_{m})\neq 0,

and thus
p
is a nonzero polynomial. By
Proposition

4.2
, it has at most
d
roots. Moreover, as the input length of
T_{C}^{i-1}|_{\rho}
is
b\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
, we can prove by
Proposition

3.12
and
3.1.4
that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}|_{\rho}]\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C}^{i})+2\eta\leq d/|\mathbb{F}|+4\eta.

This leads to a contradiction to
Equation

4.15
.

Finally, as
T^{0}_{C}\equiv\mathsf{Null}
, we know that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{0}]\leq 10\eta
. By induction on
i
(using the
2.4
) and
Equation

4.14
, we can prove that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{m}]\leq md/|\mathbb{F}|+10\eta\cdot(m+1).

(4.16)

Subsequently, we have

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C})+\delta+2\eta

(
2.2
)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C}^{m})+\delta+5\eta

(
3.1.1
)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}(X_{m})+\delta+8\eta

(
Proposition

3.12
)

\displaystyle\leq md/|\mathbb{F}|+\delta+10\eta\cdot(m+1)+8\eta

\displaystyle\leq md/|\mathbb{F}|+\delta+\beta,

where the last inequality follows by taking
\eta\triangleq\beta/(10m+20)
.
∎

4.3
Linear Hashing

The linear hash function
x\mapsto Ax\bmod 2
is one of the simplest constructions of hash functions. The following theorem formalizes that linear hashing is an (almost) universal hash function.

Theorem 4.5

(Universality of linear hashing)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,m,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. For every
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
T_{x,y}:\{0,1\}^{nm}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that parses its input as a Boolean matrix
A\in\{0,1\}^{m\times n}
and outputs
1
if and only if
Ax\equiv Ay\pmod{2}
. Then for all distinct
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{x,y})\leq\delta+\beta+(1/2+\beta)^{m}
.

Proof.

We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Fix
n,m,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and let
T_{x,y}
be the circuit defined above. Suppose that
x\neq y
. We will prove that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{x,y})\leq\delta+\beta+(1/2+\beta)^{m}
.

We first upper bound the probability when
m=1
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later, and
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that given
c\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, it outputs
\langle c,x-y\rangle\bmod 2
. We will prove that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\lnot C)\leq 1/2+6\eta
.

Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\lnot C)>1/2+6\eta
. As
x\neq y
, there is an index
i\in[n]
such that
x_{i}\neq y_{i}
. Fix the index
i
. Let
X
be the indicator random variable of
\lnot C
; by
Proposition

3.12
, we know that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X]>1/2+6\eta-3\eta=1/2+3\eta
. Subsequently, by
3.17
, there is an assignment
\rho
to all but the
i
-th bit of the random seed such that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X|_{\rho}]>1/2
. However, as the seed length of
X|_{\rho}
is
1
and the probability is
1/2
, this violates the
3.1.4
.

Let
C^{\lor m}:\{0,1\}^{nm}\to\{0,1\}
denote the circuit
C^{\lor m}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\triangleq\bigvee_{i\in[m]}C(x_{i})
. By
3.4.3
, we have

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\lnot C^{\lor m})\leq(1/2+8\eta)^{m}+2\eta.

It can be observed that
\lnot C^{\lor m}
is functionally equivalent to
T_{x,y}
, and thus by the
3.1.1
of approximate counting,

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{x,y})\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\lnot C^{\lor m})+3\eta\leq(1/2+8\eta)^{m}+5\eta.

By the
2.2
, we have

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{x,y})\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{x,y})+\delta+2\eta\leq(1/2+8\eta)^{m}+\delta+7\eta.

This completes the proof when we take
\eta\triangleq\beta/20
.
∎

4.4
Lower Bounds for Parity Against
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
Circuits

The first result of this section is a formalization in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
of an average-case lower bound for the Parity function
\oplus_{n}
against
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
. Our proof is based on a technique due to Furst, Saxe, and Sipser
31
. Previous formalizations of the lower bound
18
18

18

Both results consider only worst-case lower bounds, but it can be verified that an average-case lower bound follows from a similar argument when formalized appropriately (in the style of
Jeřábek

44
;
46
).
due to Müller and Pich
69
(following
31
) and
Krajíček

(
58
, Theorem 15.2.3)
(following Razborov’s proof of the switching lemma
81
) require the theory
\mathsf{APC}_{1}=\mathsf{PV}_{1}+\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
.
19
19

19

Note that both results automatically give a formalization of the worst-case lower bound in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}+\mathsf{rWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
, as
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
is
\forall\Sigma_{1}^{b}
-conservative over
\mathsf{PV}_{1}+\mathsf{rWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})

43
;
46
and the worst-case lower bound can be formalized as a
\forall\Sigma_{1}^{b}
-sentence (see, e.g.,
(
69
, Theorem 1.1)
).

See
1.6

The main technical challenge is to avoid “encoding-based counting argument” that rely on
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
, which is not available in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. The encoding-based counting argument is used in both
69
and Razborov’s
81
proof of the switching lemma. This was partially addressed by Agrawal et al.
2
(see also
3
)
20
20

20

As mentioned in
2
, the result was known to Ajtai and Wigderson (unpublished).
, which presented a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm that outputs a suitable restriction given by the switching lemma. As one of our contributions, we show that the correctness of the algorithm in Agrawal et al.
2
can be established in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. This, together with tools developed in
Section

3
), allow us to formalize the
average-case
lower bound in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

Interestingly, using the same technique, we further show that the
worst-case
lower bound
\oplus_{n}\notin{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
can be formalized in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. This resolves an open problem from
69
.

See
1.7

Notation.

We let
n
denote the number of input variables. A
k
-CNF is a propositional formula of the form
C_{1}\land C_{2}\land\cdots\land C_{m}
, where each clause
C_{i}
is a disjunction of at most
k
literals, i.e., variables or their negations. Similarly, a
k
-DNF is a disjunction of terms, where each term is a conjunction of at most
k
literals. We say that a formula is a
k
-NF if it is either a
k
-CNF or a
k
-DNF. A clause
21
21

21

In the context of
k
-NFs, we use “clause” to refer to a subformula, regardless of the connective.

C
of a
k
-NF can be described by its type (i.e.
\land
or
\lor
) and two subsets
S_{C}^{+},S_{C}^{-}\subseteq[n]
of size
|S_{C}^{+}|+|S_{C}^{-}|\leq k
, where
S_{C}^{+}
denotes the ID of variables in the clause, and
S_{C}^{-}
denotes the ID of negations of variables in the clause. Let
S_{C}\triangleq S_{C}^{+}\cup S_{C}^{-}
.

Note that we may assume without loss of generality that
S_{C}^{+}\cap S_{C}^{-}=\varnothing
, as otherwise the clause will be either always
0
or always
1
.

For simplicity, we assume without loss of generality that
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
circuits satisfy the following properties:

•

The circuit is
layered
, i.e., gates in each layer are fed by gates only in previous layer.

•

All negation gates are pushed to be directly above input variables. Equivalently, there is no negation gate inside the circuit, i.e., gates in the first layer can be fed by literals, i.e., input variables or their negations.

An
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
circuit satisfying these properties is called a
well-formed
circuit. Note that an arbitrary
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
circuit can be transformed into a well-formed circuit with only a polynomial size overhead, and the correctness of the transformation can be proved in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. We start directly from well-formed circuits to simplify calculations.

4.4.1
Deterministic Selection of Subset for Restriction

We start by stating two lemmas that formalize the core combinatorial property used in the proof of Furst, Saxe, and Sipser
31
. Similar functions are used implicitly in
2
. Here we use potential functions in order to implement a derandomization via the method of conditional expectations. The approach provides both a feasible algorithm and a feasible proof.

Lemma 4.6

(Potential Function for Small Sets)
.

The following sentence is provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
for every constant
c\geq 1
. Let
n,t\in\mathsf{Log}
with
t\leq n
,
s,m\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
,
s\leq m
,
p\triangleq t/n\in\mathbb{Q}
, and
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}\subseteq[n]
be disjoint sets of size at most
c
such that
pc<1
. There is a circuit
\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}:\{*,\circ\}^{\leq n}\to\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
such that the following holds.

•

(Initial Condition)
.
\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}(\varepsilon)\leq m^{s}(pc)^{m-s}
.

•

(Recursion Condition)
. For every
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{i}
,
i<n
, we have

\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}(x)=p\cdot\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}(x*)+(1-p)\cdot\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}(x\circ).

•

(Termination Condition)
. For every
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{n}
,
\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}(x)\in\{0,1\}
. Moreover, let
T_{x}\triangleq\{i\in[n]\mid x_{i}=\circ\}
. Then
\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}(x)=0
if and only if there are at least
s
subsets
S\in\{S_{1},\dots,S_{m}\}
such that
S\subseteq T_{x}
.

Proof Sketch.

We argue in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. Let
n,t,s\in\mathsf{Log}
,
m\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
,
p\triangleq t/n
, and
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}\subseteq[n]
. The circuit
\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}
is defined as follows: Given
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{i}
for some
0\leq i\leq n
, let
T_{x}\triangleq\{j\leq i\mid x_{j}=\circ\}
. It outputs

\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}(x)\triangleq\sum_{\alpha\subseteq[m],|\alpha|<s}\phi(x,\alpha),

where

\displaystyle\phi(x,\alpha)\triangleq\begin{cases}\displaystyle 0&\exists j\in\alpha~\exists k\in[i]~(k\in S_{j}\land x_{k}=*)\\
\displaystyle\prod_{j\in[\alpha]}(1-p)^{|S_{j}\setminus T_{x}|}\prod_{j^{\prime}\in[m]\setminus\alpha}\psi(x,j^{\prime})&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

\displaystyle\psi(x,j^{\prime})\triangleq\begin{cases}1&\exists k\in[i]~(k\in S_{j^{\prime}}\land x_{k}=*)\\
1-(1-p)^{|S_{j^{\prime}}\setminus T_{x}|}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

For instructive purposes, we mention the combinatorial interpretation of the functions (which is not a part of the
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
proof): Given any
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{i}
, we randomly assign
x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n}
independently to
*
with probability
p
and to
\circ
with probability
1-p
. Let
T_{x}^{\prime}\triangleq\{i\in[n]\mid x_{i}=\circ\}
. Then

•

\psi(x,j^{\prime})
is the probability that
S_{j^{\prime}}\nsubseteq T_{x}^{\prime}
.

•

\phi(x,\alpha)
is the probability that
S_{j}\subseteq T_{x}^{\prime}
if and only if
j\in\alpha
.

•

\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}(x)
is the probability that at most
s-1
subsets
S\in\{S_{1},\dots,S_{m}\}
satisfy
S\subseteq T_{x}
.

We come back to the
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
proof. The recursion condition and termination condition can be verified by a tedious but straightforward calculation, which we omit here. To prove the initial condition, notice that

\displaystyle\psi(\varepsilon,j^{\prime})=1-(1-p)^{|S_{j}^{\prime}|}\leq 1-(1-p)^{c}\leq pc

\displaystyle\phi(\varepsilon,\alpha)\leq\prod_{j^{\prime}\in[m]\setminus\alpha}\psi(\varepsilon,j^{\prime})\leq(pc)^{m-|\alpha|}

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}(\varepsilon)\leq\sum_{0\leq j<s}\binom{m}{j}(pc)^{m-j}\leq m^{s}(pc)^{m-s}

The argument can be implemented in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
using that
s,m\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
and
c
is constant. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.7

(Potential for General Set Systems)
.

For every choice of constants
k,c\geq 1
, there are constants
b,n_{0}\geq 1
such that the following sentence is provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. Let
n,t,m\in\mathsf{Log}
,
n>n_{0}
,
p\triangleq t/n\in\mathbb{Q}
, and
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{m}\subseteq[n]
be nonempty subsets of size at most
c
. Then there is a circuit
\Phi:\{*,\circ\}^{\leq n}\to\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
such that the following holds.

•

(Initial Condition)
. If
t\leq\sqrt{n}
, then
\Phi(\varepsilon)\leq n^{-k}
.

•

(Recursion Condition)
. For every
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{i}
,
i<n
, we have

\Phi(x)=p\cdot\Phi(x*)+(1-p)\cdot\Phi(x\circ).

•

(Termination Condition)
. For every
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{n}
,
\Phi(x)\in\{0,1\}
. Moreover, for
T_{x}\triangleq\{i\in[n]\mid x_{i}=\circ\}
, if
\Phi(x)=0
, then one of the following conditions holds:

1.

|S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup\dots\cup S_{m}\setminus T_{x}|\leq b
.

2.

There are disjoint nonempty sets
V_{1},\dots,V_{\ell}\subseteq[n]
that are subsets of
\ell
distinct sets among
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}
, such that
V_{i}\subseteq T_{x}
for every
i\in[\ell]
, where
\ell\geq k\ln n
.

Moreover, the disjoint sets
V_{1},\dots,V_{\ell}
can be obtained by a
\mathsf{PV}
function given
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}
and
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{n}
.

Proof.

Fix any constants
k,c\geq 1
and let
b,n_{0}\geq 1
be constants to be determined later. We argue in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. Fix
n,t,m\in\mathsf{Log}
,
p\triangleq t/n
, and
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}\subseteq[n]
.

Disjoint Set Decomposition.

Consider the iterative algorithm: Let
\mathcal{U}_{0}\leftarrow\{S_{1},\dots,S_{m}\}
. In the
i
-th step, where
i\geq 1
, we choose a maximal set
\mathcal{V}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{i-1}
such that the sets in
\mathcal{V}_{i}
are disjoint, and compute
\mathcal{U}_{i}
as follows:

•

Let
\mathcal{U}_{i}\leftarrow\varnothing
. For every
S\in\mathcal{U}_{i-1}
, we include
S\setminus(\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{V}_{i}}S)
in
\mathcal{U}_{i}
if this set is nonempty.

Note that each set remaining in
\mathcal{U}_{i}
must be a subset of some
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}
. Moreover, it must be of size at most
c-i
, as the maximal set
\mathcal{V}_{i}
intersects with each set in
\mathcal{U}_{i-1}
(otherwise it is not maximal). The algorithm terminates if no set is added to
\mathcal{U}_{i}
during an iteration.

Let
d\leq c
and
\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{d}
be the sets obtained by the algorithm. One can prove that the union of the sets in
\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{d}
covers
S_{1}\cup\dots\cup S_{m}
. Furthermore, it can be verified that for each
i\in[d]
and
V_{1},\dots,V_{\ell}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
, there are distinct
i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}\in[m]
such that
V_{1}\subseteq S_{i_{1}},\dots,V_{\ell}\subseteq S_{i_{\ell}}
.

Wide Case.

We first consider the case that for some
i\in[d]
,
\mathcal{V}_{i}
contains at least
\ell\triangleq 2k\ln n
sets. Fix
i
to be the smallest number satisfying this. Let
V_{1},\dots,V_{\ell}
be the first
\ell
sets in
\mathcal{V}_{i}
. Let
\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}(x)
be the potential function in
Lemma

4.6
for
V_{1},\dots,V_{\ell}
and
s\triangleq\ell/2
(note that
\ell\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
). We define
\Phi(x)\triangleq\Phi_{\mathsf{s}}(x)
.

It then suffices to verify that the three required properties hold.

•

(
Initial Condition
). By
Lemma

4.6
, we know that

\Phi(\varepsilon)\leq\ell^{\ell/2}\cdot(pc)^{\ell/2}\leq\left(\frac{bkc\ln n}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{\ell/2}\leq n^{-k},

where we assume a choice of
b\geq 2
and
a sufficiently large
n
, which can be ensured by setting
n_{0}
as a large constant.

•

(
Recursion Condition
). It follows from the recursion condition in
Lemma

4.6
.

•

(
Termination Condition
). For
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{n},\Phi(x)\in\{0,1\}
by
Lemma

4.6
. Let
T_{x}\triangleq\{i\in[n]\mid x_{i}=\circ\}
. Suppose that
\Phi(x)=0
. We know that there are at least
\ell/2\geq k\ln n
sets
V
among
V_{1},\dots,V_{\ell}
such that
V\subseteq T_{x}
. This satisfies the second termination condition. Moreover, the
k\ln n
sets can be obtained by a
\mathsf{PV}
function given
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}
and
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{n}
, using the algorithm that constructs
\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{d}
.

Narrow Case.

Now we consider the case that
\mathcal{V}_{i}\leq 2k\ln n
sets for every
i\in[d]
. Note that the union of the sets in
\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{d}
covers
S_{1}\cup\dots\cup S_{m}
. We know that

|S_{1}\cup\dots\cup S_{m}|=\left|\bigcup_{i\in[d]}\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}}V\right|\leq d\cdot(2k\ln n)\cdot c\leq 2kc^{2}\ln n.

Recall that
b\geq 1
is a constant to be determined. Let
S\triangleq S_{1}\cup\dots\cup S_{m}
. We define the potential function
\Phi(x)
as follows. Given
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{i}
for
i<n
, let
q^{*}\triangleq|\{j\in[i]\cap S\mid x_{j}=*\}|
,
q^{\circ}\triangleq|\{j\in[i]\cap S\mid x_{j}=\circ\}|
, and
q^{\diamond}\triangleq|S|-q^{*}-q^{\circ}
. Then

\Phi(x)\triangleq\sum_{j=\max\{b+1,q^{*}\}}^{|S|-q^{\circ}}\binom{q^{\diamond}}{j-q^{*}}p^{j-q^{*}}(1-p)^{q^{\diamond}-j+q^{*}}.

(4.17)

For instructive purposes, we mention that the combinatorial interpretation of the function is as follows. We randomly assign
x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n}
independently to
*
with probability
p
and to
\circ
with probability
1-p
. Then
\Phi(x)
is the probability that the number of indices
j\in[n]\cap S
such that
x_{j}=*
is at least
b+1
. The combinatorial interpretation is
not
a part of the
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
proof.

Note that
\Phi(x)\geq 0
as each term is non-negative, and
\Phi(x)\leq 1
by the
3.34
. It suffices to verify the three required properties.

•

(
Initial Condition
). Note that

\Phi(\varepsilon)\leq\sum_{j=b+1}^{|S|}\binom{|S|}{j}p^{j}\leq\sum_{j=b+1}^{|S|}|S|^{j}p^{j}\leq\sum_{j=b+1}^{|S|}\left(\frac{2kc^{2}\ln n}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{j}\leq n^{-k},

where the last inequality holds if we set
b\geq 10k
and
n_{0}
to be sufficiently large.

•

(
Recursion Condition
). Fix any
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{i}
for
i<n
, we consider whether
i+1\in S
. If not, we have that
\Phi(x)=\Phi(x*)=\Phi(x\circ)
and the equation holds. Otherwise, let
q^{*}\triangleq|\{j\in[i]\cap S\mid x_{j}=*\}
,
q^{\circ}\triangleq\{j\in[i]\cap S\mid x_{j}=\circ\}
, and
q^{\diamond}\triangleq|S|-q^{*}-q^{\circ}
. We can see that

\displaystyle\Phi(x*)\triangleq\sum_{j=\max\{b+1,q^{*}+1\}}^{|S|-q^{\circ}}\binom{q^{\diamond}-1}{j-q^{*}-1}p^{j-q^{*}-1}(1-p)^{q^{\diamond}-j+q^{*}};

\displaystyle\Phi(x\circ)\triangleq\sum_{j=\max\{b+1,q^{*}\}}^{|S|-q^{\circ}-1}\binom{q^{\diamond}-1}{j-q^{*}}p^{j-q^{*}}(1-p)^{q^{\diamond}-1-j+q^{*}}.

It follows from
Equation

3.45
that
\Phi(x)=p\cdot\Phi(x*)+(1-p)\cdot\Phi(x\circ)
.

•

(
Termination Condition
). For
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{n}
, let
q^{*},q^{\circ},q^{\diamond}
be defined as above. We have
q^{\diamond}=0
and
q^{*}+q^{\circ}=|S|
. In that case, one can observe that if
q^{*}>b
, then
\Phi(x)=1
, and otherwise
\Phi(x)=0
. Moreover, we know by the definition of
q^{*}
that if
\Phi(x)=0
, for
T_{x}\triangleq\{i\in[n]\mid x_{i}=\circ\}
, we have
|S\setminus T_{x}|=q^{*}\leq b
. This satisfies the first termination condition.

This completes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to state the
Subset Selection Lemma
.

Lemma 4.8

(Subset Selection Lemma)
.

For every
k,c\geq 1
, there are constants
b,n_{0}\geq 1
such that the following sentence is provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. Let
n,t,\ell\in\mathsf{Log}
,
n>n_{0}
,
\ell\leq n^{k}
, and
F_{1},F_{2},\dots,F_{\ell}
be
c
-NFs over
n
input variables. If
t\leq\sqrt{n}
, there exists a subset
T\subseteq[n]
of size at most
n-t
such that for every
i\in[\ell]
, at least one of the following conditions hold.

•

If we fix the
j
-th variable for every
j\in T
,
F_{i}
is fed by at most
b
literals.

•

There are
m^{\prime}\geq k\ln n
disjoint non-empty clauses
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}
such that (1) each
C^{\prime}_{j}
is a sub-clause of a different clause in
F_{i}
; (2)
S_{C_{j}^{\prime}}\subseteq T
.

Moreover, the subset
T
and the clauses
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C_{m}^{\prime}
for each
i
are computed by a
\mathsf{PV}
function given
F_{1},\dots,F_{\ell}
and
1^{n},1^{t},1^{\ell}
.

Proof.

Fix any
k,c\geq 1
and let
b,n_{0}\geq 1
be determined later. We argue in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. Fix
n,t\in\mathsf{Log}
,
\ell\leq n^{k}
,
p\triangleq t/n
, and
c
-NFs
F_{1},\dots,F_{\ell}
over
n
variables.

Fix any
i\in[\ell]
. Suppose that
F_{i}
has
m_{i}
clauses, and let
C_{ij}
be the
j
-th clause of
F_{i}
. Let
S_{ij}\triangleq S_{C_{ij}}
be the subset of variables which or whose negation feeds
C_{ij}
. For each
i\in[\ell]
, consider the sets
S_{i1},\dots,S_{im_{i}}
; by
Lemma

4.7
(using
3k
instead of
k
), there are constants
b^{\prime},n_{0}^{\prime}
and a potential function
\Phi_{i}:\{*,\circ\}^{\leq n}\to\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
such that the following conditions hold.

•

(
Initial Condition
).
\Phi_{i}(\varepsilon)\leq n^{-3k}
;

•

(
Recursion Condition
).
\Phi_{i}(x)=p\cdot\Phi_{i}(x*)+(1-p)\cdot\Phi_{i}(x\circ)
.

•

(
Termination Condition
). For
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{n}
,
\Phi_{i}(x)\in\{0,1\}
. Moreover, let
T_{x}\triangleq\{i\in[n]\mid x_{i}=\circ\}
. If
\Phi_{i}(x)=0
, then one of the following two conditions holds:

1.

|S_{i1}\cup\dots\cup S_{im_{i}}\setminus T_{x}|\leq b
. This effectively means that if we fix
j
-th variable for every
j\in T_{x}
, then
F_{i}
is fed by at most
b
literals.

2.

There are disjoint nonempty sets
V_{1},\dots,V_{\ell_{i}}\subseteq[n]
that are subsets of
\ell_{i}
distinct sets among
S_{i1},\dots,S_{im_{i}}
, such that
V_{i}\subseteq T_{x}
for every
i\in[\ell]
, where
\ell_{i}\geq 3k\ln n\geq k\ln n
. This means that there are
m^{\prime}=\ell_{i}\geq k\ln n
disjoint clauses
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}
, each of which is a sub-clause of a clause in
F_{i}
, such that
S_{C_{j}^{\prime}}\subseteq T
.

Therefore, if
\Phi_{i}(x)=0
, then the clause satisfies the required property if we choose
T\triangleq T_{x}
.

Consider the potential function
\Phi(x)
as follows. Given any
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{i}
,
i\leq n
, let
q^{\circ}
be the number of
\circ
’s in
x
. We define

\Phi(x)\triangleq q^{\circ}+(1-p)(n-i)+n\cdot\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\Phi_{i}(x).

We can show that
\Phi(x)
satisfies the following conditions:

•

(
Initial Condition
).
\Phi(\varepsilon)\leq(1-p)n+n\cdot\ell\cdot n^{-3k}<(1-p)n+1
.

•

(
Recursion Condition
).
\Phi(x)=p\cdot\Phi(x*)+(1-p)\cdot\Phi(x\circ)
.

•

(
Termination Condition
). For
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{n}
,
\Phi(x)
is an integer. Moreover,
\Phi(x)
is at most the sum of (1) the number of
*
’s in
x
and (2)
n
times the number of
c
-NFs
F_{i}
that violates the required properties if we choose
T=T_{x}=\{i\in[n]\mid x_{i}=\circ\}
. In particular, if
\Phi(x)\leq(1-p)n
,
T=T_{x}
is a desired subset.

It remains to construct
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{n}
such that
\Phi(x)\leq(1-p)n
. Indeed, it can be obtained by the greedy algorithm that, starting from
x\leftarrow\varepsilon
, appends either
*
or
\circ
to
x
to minimize
\Phi(x)
. By induction on
i
, we can prove that the string
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{\leq i}
after the
i
-th round of the algorithm satisfies that
\Phi(x)\leq\Phi(\varepsilon)<(1-p)n+1
. This is available in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
as the property can be verified by a straightforward polynomial-time algorithm. Finally, the string
x\in\{*,\circ\}^{n}
obtained after
n
rounds satisfies that
\Phi(x)\leq(1-p)n
, as it must be an integer smaller than
(1-p)n+1
. This completes the proof.
∎

4.4.2
Average-Case Lower Bound in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}

We say that a partial assignment
\rho

trivializes
an NF
F
if
F
is a constant function after applying
\rho
. Note that for every NF
F
