that contains a non-constant clause
C
, there is an assignment to variables in
C
that trivializes
F
.

Lemma 4.9

(Random Restriction Lemma)
.

For every
k,c,b\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an
n_{0}\geq 1
such that the following is provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Let
n,t,\ell\in\mathsf{Log}
,
n>n_{0}
. Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{\ell}
be
c
-NFs over
n
input variables, and
T\subseteq[n]
be a subset of size
n-t
such that for every
i\in[\ell]
, at least one of the following conditions hold.

•

(Narrow)
. If we simultaneously fix all variables whose indices are in
T
, the
c
-NF
F_{i}
will be fed by at most
b
literals.

•

(Wide)
. There are
m^{\prime}_{i}\geq k\ln n
(explicitly given) non-empty disjoint clauses
C_{i,1}^{\prime},\dots,C^{\prime}_{i,m^{\prime}_{i}}
such that (1) each
C_{i,j}^{\prime}
is a sub-clause of a different clause in
F_{i}
; (2)
S_{C^{\prime}_{i,j}}\subseteq T
for every
j\in[m^{\prime}_{i}]
.

Let
Y
be the random variable over
\{0,1\}
that takes a seed
x
of length
n
, parses it as an assignment
\rho
to variables in
T
(i.e. it fixes the
i
-th variable to
x_{i}
for every
i\in T
), and outputs
1
if and only if at least one of
F_{1},\dots,F_{\ell}
is neither trivialized nor depends on at most
b
literals after applying
\rho
. Then for every
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}

\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y]\leq\ell\cdot(1-2^{-c}+\beta)^{k\ln n}+\delta+\beta.

Proof.

We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Fix
k,c,b\in\mathbb{N}
and let
n_{0}\geq 1
be a constant to be determined. Fix
n,t,\ell\in\mathsf{Log}
,
F_{1},\dots,F_{\ell}
, and
T\subseteq[n]
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later.

For simplicity, we assume that
F_{1},\dots,F_{\ell^{\prime}}
are the gates that satisfy the second condition, and let
m^{\prime}_{i}\geq k\cdot\ln n
be the number of disjoint clauses
C^{\prime}_{i,1},\dots,C^{\prime}_{i,m^{\prime}_{i}}
. We will define random variables over
\{0,1\}
with
n
bit seeds as follows.

•

For every
i\in[\ell^{\prime}]
and
j\in[m_{i}^{\prime}]
,
X_{ij}
is defined as the following random variable: Let
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
be the seed. Then
X_{ij}=1
if and only if fixing the
r
-th variable to
x_{r}
for every
r\in T
does not trivialize
C^{\prime}_{i,j}
.

•

For every
i\in[\ell^{\prime}]
, let
X_{i}=\prod_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}_{i}}X_{ij}
.

•

Notice that
Y\leq\bigvee_{i\in[\ell^{\prime}]}X_{i}
.

Note that each
X_{ij}
reads at most
c\in\mathbb{N}
bits of its seed. Thus by the
3.1.4
, we know that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{ij}]\leq 1-2^{-c}+2\eta
. By the
3.4.2
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i1}X_{i2}\dots X_{im^{\prime}_{i}}]\leq(1-2^{-c}+2\eta)^{m^{\prime}_{i}}+8\eta\cdot m^{\prime}_{i}\leq(1-2^{-c}+2\eta)^{k\cdot\ln n}+8\eta\cdot|F_{i}|\leq(1-2^{-c}+\beta)^{k\ln n}+8\eta\cdot|F_{i}|,

where the last inequality holds when
\eta\leq\beta/2
. By the
3.3.1
,

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\ell^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]+3\eta\cdot\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell\cdot(1-2^{-c}+\beta)^{k\ln n}+8\eta\cdot\ell\cdot(2^{c}n^{c})+3\eta\cdot\ell.

The lemma then follows from the
3.2.2
by setting
\eta\triangleq\beta/(20\cdot\ell\cdot 2^{c}n^{c})
.
∎

Now we are ready to prove the average-case lower bound for
\oplus_{n}
against
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
.

See
1.6

Proof.

We prove by induction on
d
in the meta-theory that the statement holds for every
k
. The constant
n_{0}^{k,d}\geq 1
will be determined later in the proof. In both the base case and induction case, we argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and the circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later.

Base Case.

Suppose that
d=1
. Towards a contradiction, assume that
Equation

1.1
does not hold. Then by the
2.2
, we have

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C})>\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{n^{k}}+(\beta-2\eta).

(4.18)

Note that
\mathsf{Fix}_{b}(T_{C})
is the circuit
T_{\mathsf{Fix}_{b}(C)\oplus b}
.

We first show that for
D:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
, if
D
depends on at most
n-1
of its input bits, then
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{D})\leq 1/2+O(\eta)
. Suppose that
D
does not depend on the
i
-th input bit. Let
X_{D}
be the indicator random variable of
T_{D}
and
\rho
be any assignment to all but the
i
-th input bit of
D
. By the
3.1.4
, we have that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{D}|_{\rho}]\leq 1/2+2\eta
. Subsequently, by the
3.17
, we can conclude that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{D}]\leq\frac{1}{2}+2\eta+3\eta\leq\frac{1}{2}+5\eta.

(4.19)

This implies that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{D})\leq 1/2+8\eta
by
Proposition

3.12
.

Now we assume that
C
depends on all of its input bits and is of depth at most
d=1
. Consider the following iterative
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle algorithm. Let
C_{0}\triangleq C
and
s_{0}\triangleq 0
. The algorithm maintains the invariant that after the
i
-th round,
C_{i}
depends on all of its input bits. Given that the invariant holds after the
(i-1)
-th round, there exists
b_{i}\in\{0,1\}
such that

•

\mathsf{Fix}_{b_{i}}(C_{i-1})
is a constant circuit;

•

\mathsf{Fix}_{1-b_{i}}(C_{i-1})
depends on all of its input bits.

Fix that
b_{i}\in\{0,1\}
. The algorithm then defines
C_{i}\triangleq\mathsf{Fix}_{1-b_{i}}(C_{i-1})\oplus(1-b_{i})
and
s_{i}\triangleq s_{i-1}\oplus(1-b_{i})
in the
i
-th round, and the invariant is maintained.

We will prove by induction on
i\leq n
that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C_{i}\oplus s_{i}})>\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2^{i}}{n^{k}}+(\beta-8(i+1)\cdot\eta).

(Note that
n\in\mathsf{Log}
and
i\leq n
, so the induction hypothesis can be expressed by an open formula in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.)
The base case follows from
Equation

4.18
. Suppose that the inequality holds for
i<n
. Notice that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C_{i}\oplus s_{i}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{b_{i}}(T_{C_{i}})\oplus s_{i})+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{1-b_{i}}(T_{C_{i}})\oplus s_{i})}{2}

(
2.2
)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{\mathsf{Fix}_{b_{i}}(C_{i})\oplus s_{i}\oplus b_{i}})+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{\mathsf{Fix}_{1-b_{i}}(C_{i})\oplus s_{i}\oplus(1-b_{i})})}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{\mathsf{Fix}_{b_{i}}(C_{i})\oplus s_{i}\oplus b_{i}})+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C_{i+1}\oplus s_{i+1}})}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}+8\eta+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C_{i+1}\oplus s_{i+1}})\right).

(The last inequality follows as
\mathsf{Fix}_{b_{i}}(C_{i})
is a constant circuit and thus does not depend on all of its input bits.) It then follows by the induction hypothesis that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C_{i+1}\oplus s_{i+1}})>\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2^{i+1}}{n^{k}}+(\beta-8(i+2)\cdot\eta).

We set
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
to be sufficiently large such that
2^{n-10}/n^{k}\geq 1
for every
n>n_{0}
. Therefore, we have that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C_{n-10}})>3/2+(\beta-8(n+1)\cdot\eta)
, where
C_{n-10}
has input length exactly
10
and is an
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}
circuit of depth
1
. This is provably impossible if we set
\eta\triangleq\beta/(20n)
by the
3.1.4
.

Induction Case.

Suppose that the theorem holds for
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Our goal is to prove the theorem for
d+1
. Let
n_{0}^{k,d}
be the constant
n_{0}\geq 1
corresponding to the theorem for
d
and
k
. Fix any
k\geq 1
and let
n_{0}^{k,d+1}
be a constant to be determined. Towards a contradiction, assume that
Equation

1.1
does not hold. Then by the
2.2
, we have

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C})>\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{n^{k}}+(\beta-2\eta).

(4.20)

At a high level, we will apply random restrictions twice to convert
C
to an
{\mathsf{AC}}^{0}_{d}
circuit that computes the parity function w.h.p. on a smaller input length; after that, we can apply the induction hypothesis to conclude the proof.

Restriction 1.

Let
G_{1},G_{2},\dots,G_{\ell}
be the gates in the first layer, i.e., directly fed by literals. We may view them as
1
-NFs, as each literal can be viewed as a clause with one literal. Let
t\triangleq\sqrt{n}
. By the
4.4.1
, there exists a subset
T_{1}\subseteq[n]
of size at most
n-t
such that for every gate
G_{i}
, one of the conditions hold.

•

If we fix all variables in
T_{1}
,
G_{i}
will be fed by at most
b_{1}
variables, where
b_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
is a constant.

•

At least
100k\cdot\ln n
literals of
G_{i}
are using variables in
T_{1}
.

Fix the subset
T_{1}\subseteq[n]
. We assume that
|T_{1}|=n-t
; if not, we add
n-t-|T_{1}|
arbitrary elements to it.

We define random variables over
\{0,1\}
with
n
bit seeds as follows.

•

Let
Y
be the random variable in the
4.4.2
. That is, given
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, it parses
x
as a partial assignment
\rho
to variables in
T_{1}
, and outputs
1
if and only if at least one of the gates is neither trivialized nor depends on at most
b
literals after applying
\rho
.

•

Let
Y_{T}
be the indicator random variable of
T_{C}
.

By the
4.4.2
and using
c=1
, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]\leq\ell\cdot(1/2+\eta)^{100k\ln n}+2\eta\leq n^{-8k},

where the last inequality holds when
\eta
is sufficiently small and
n
is sufficiently large (by setting
n_{0}^{k,d+1}\in\mathbb{N}
). By the
3.17
, there exists an assignment
\rho_{1}
to variables in
T_{1}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{T}|_{\rho_{1}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y|_{\rho_{1}}]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{n^{k}}+(\beta-30\eta)-\frac{1}{n^{8k}}>5\eta,

(4.21)

where the last inequality holds if
\eta
is sufficiently small.

Fix the assignment
\rho_{1}
. Note that
Y|_{\rho_{1}}\in\{0,1\}
as
Y
only reads its input variables in
T_{1}
. Therefore, we must have
Y|_{\rho_{1}}=0
. In this case, all gates are either trivialized or fed by at most
b_{1}
variables after applying
\rho_{1}
, and thus can be replaced by a gate of fan-in at most
b_{1}
.

Let
\sigma_{1}\triangleq\oplus_{n-t}(\rho_{1})
,
n_{1}\triangleq t
, and
C_{1}:\{0,1\}^{n_{1}}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit obtained from
C
by applying the assignment
\rho_{1}
, replacing each gate in the first layer with an equivalent gate of fan-in at most
b_{1}
, and XORing the output of the circuit with the bit
\sigma_{1}
. Note that
C|_{\rho_{1}}(x)=C_{1}(x)\oplus\sigma_{1}
for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n_{1}}
. Moreover,
C_{1}
is of size at most
n^{k}\leq n_{1}^{2k}
.

Let
T_{C_{1}}:\{0,1\}^{n_{1}}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that, given
x
, it outputs
1
if and only if
C_{1}(x)=\oplus_{n_{1}}(x)
. It turns out that
Y_{T}|_{\rho_{1}}
is the indicator random variable of
T_{C_{1}}
; to see this, notice that for every assignment
x
to all variables but
T_{1}
,
C(x\cup\rho_{1})=\oplus_{n}(x\cup\rho_{1})
if and only if
C|_{\rho_{1}}(x)=(\oplus_{n_{1}}(x))\oplus\sigma_{1}
, where
C|_{\rho_{1}}(x)\oplus\sigma_{1}=C_{1}(x)
. Therefore, by
Proposition

3.12
, we have that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C_{1}})\geq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{T}|_{\rho}]-3\eta\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{n^{k}}+(\beta-33\eta)-\frac{1}{n^{8k}}\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{n_{1}^{4k}}+(\beta-33\eta),

where the last inequality holds if
n
is sufficiently large (by setting
n_{0}^{k,d+1}\in\mathbb{N}
).

Restriction 2.

As mentioned above, each gate in the first layer of
C_{1}
has fan-in at most
b_{1}
, and thus the gates in the second layer of
C_{1}
computes
b_{1}
-NFs. Let
F_{1},F_{2},\dots,F_{\ell_{1}}
be the
b_{1}
-NFs in the second layer of
C_{1}
. Let
t_{1}\triangleq\sqrt{n}_{1}
. By the
4.4.1
with appropriate choice of parameters, there exists a subset
T_{2}\subseteq[n]
of size at most
n_{1}-t_{1}
such that for every
i\in[\ell_{1}]
, one of the conditions hold.

•

If we fix all variables in
T_{2}
,
F_{i}
will depend on at most
b_{2}
variables, where
b_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
is a constant.

•

There are
m_{1}^{\prime}\geq 100k\cdot 4^{b_{1}}\cdot\ln n
disjoint sub-clauses of
F_{i}
that only use literals from variables in
T_{2}
.

Let
n_{2}\triangleq t_{1}
and fix the set
T_{2}\subseteq[n]
. We assume that
|T_{2}|=n_{1}-t_{1}
; if not, we add
n_{1}-t_{1}-|T_{2}|
arbitrary elements to it. We define random variables over
\{0,1\}
with
n
bit seeds as follows.

•

Let
Y^{\prime}
be the random variable in the
4.4.2
. That is, given
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, it parses
x
as a partial assignment
\rho
to variables in
T_{2}
, and outputs
1
if and only if each of the
b
-NFs is either trivialized or depends on at most
b_{2}
literals after applying
\rho
.

•

Let
Y_{T}^{\prime}
be the indicator random variable of
T_{C_{1}}
.

By the
4.4.2
, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y^{\prime}]\leq\ell_{1}\cdot(1-2^{-b_{1}}+\eta)^{100k\cdot 4^{b_{1}}\cdot\ln n}+2\eta\leq n_{1}^{-8k},

where the last inequality holds when
\eta
is sufficiently small and
n
is sufficiently large (by setting
n_{0}^{k,d+1}\in\mathbb{N}
). By the
3.17
, there exists an assignment
\rho_{2}
to variables in
T_{2}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y^{\prime}_{T}|_{\rho_{2}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y^{\prime}|_{\rho_{2}}]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{n_{1}^{4k}}+(\beta-36\eta)-\frac{1}{n_{1}^{8k}}>5\eta,

(4.22)

where the last inequality holds if
\eta
is sufficiently small.

Fix the assignment
\rho_{2}
. Note that
Y^{\prime}|_{\rho_{2}}\in\{0,1\}
as it only reads its input variables in
T_{2}
. Therefore, we must have
Y_{\rho_{2}}^{\prime}=0
. In such case, all
b_{1}
-NFs (i.e. gates in the second layer of
C_{1}
) are either trivialized or fed by at most
b_{2}
variables. In such case, we can transform
C_{1}
into an equivalent circuit of depth at most
d
as follows. Suppose that
d\geq 2
(the case for
d=1
is left as an exercise). For each gate
G
in the second layer, if it is not trivialized, we remove
G
and consider each gate
G^{\prime}
in the third layer originally fed by
G
:

•

If
G^{\prime}
is an AND gate, we rewrite
G
as an equivalent CNF of size at most
b_{2}\cdot 2^{b_{2}}
and connect all clauses of it to
G^{\prime}
.

•

If
G^{\prime}
is an OR gate, we rewrite
G
as an equivalent DNF of size at most
b_{2}\cdot 2^{b_{2}}
and connect all clauses of it to
G^{\prime}
.

In either case, the circuit remains functionally equivalent.

Let
\sigma_{2}\triangleq\oplus_{n_{1}-t_{1}}(\rho_{2})
and
C_{2}:\{0,1\}^{n_{2}}\to\{0,1\}
be the depth-
d
circuit that computes
C_{1}|_{\rho_{2}}(x)\oplus\sigma_{2}
. The size of
C_{2}
blows up by a linear factor, which is at most
O(n^{k})\leq n_{2}^{6k}
, when
n
is sufficiently large (by setting
n_{0}^{k,d+1}\in\mathbb{N}
). Let
T_{C_{2}}:\{0,1\}^{n_{2}}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that, given
x
, it outputs
1
if and only if
C_{2}(x)=\oplus_{n_{2}}(x_{2})
. As before,
Y_{T}^{\prime}|_{\rho}
is the indicator random variable of
T_{C_{2}}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C_{2}})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{T}^{\prime}|_{\rho}]-3\eta

(
Proposition

3.12
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{n_{1}^{4k}}+(\beta-39\eta)-\frac{1}{n_{1}^{8k}}

(
Equation

4.22
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{n_{2}^{6k}}+(\beta-39\eta)

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{n_{2}^{6k}}+2\eta,

(4.23)

where the last two lines hold when
n
is sufficiently large (by setting
n_{0}^{k,d+1}\in\mathbb{N}
) and
\eta
is sufficiently small.

Now we arrive at a contradiction:
C_{2}:\{0,1\}^{n_{2}}\to\{0,1\}
is a depth-
d
circuit of size at most
n_{2}^{6k}
, and it computes parity with advantage
1/n_{2}^{6k}
. This violates
Equation

1.1
. The theorem then follows from the induction hypothesis for depth
d
and size
n_{2}^{6k}
if we set
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
n_{0}^{k,d+1}\in\mathbb{N}
appropriately based on
b_{1},b_{2},n_{0}^{6k,d}
and the requirements of inequalities used in the proofs.
∎

4.4.3
Worst-Case Lower Bound in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}

First, we derandomize the
4.4.2
via an explicit implementation of the method of conditional expectations in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
.

Lemma 4.10

(Derandomized Restriction Lemma)
.

For every
k,c,b\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an
n_{0}\geq 1
such that the following is provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. Let
n,t,\ell\in\mathsf{Log}
,
n>n_{0}
. Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{\ell}
be
c
-NFs over
n
input variables, and
T\subseteq[n]
be a subset of size
n-t
such that for every
i\in[\ell]
, at least one of the following conditions hold.

•

(Narrow)
. If we fix the
j
-th variable for every
j\in T
,
F_{i}
is fed by at most
b
literals.

•

(Wide)
. There are
m^{\prime}_{i}\geq k\ln n
(explicitly given) disjoint clauses
C_{i,1}^{\prime},\dots,C^{\prime}_{i,m^{\prime}_{i}}
such that (1) each
C^{\prime}_{i,j}
is a sub-clause of a different clause in
F_{i}
; (2)
S_{C^{\prime}_{i,j}}\subseteq T
for every
j\in[m^{\prime}_{i}]
.

Suppose that
\ell\cdot(1-2^{-c})^{k\ln n}<1
. Then there exists an assignment
\rho
to the variables in
T
such that each of
F_{1},\dots,F_{\ell}
is either trivialized or depends on at most
b
literals after applying
\rho
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. Let
k,c,b\in\mathbb{N}
and
n_{0}\geq 1
be a constant to be determined later. Fix
n,t,\ell\in\mathsf{Log}
,
c
-NFs
F_{1},\dots,F_{\ell}
, and
T\subseteq[n]
. We say that a
c
-NF is good after applying a restriction
\rho
to the variables in
T
if it is either trivialized or depends on at most
b
literals. As the
c
-NFs satisfying the first bullet are good regardless of the assignment
\rho
, we assume without loss of generality that all
c
-NFs satisfy the second bullet. We will construct an assignment
\rho
such that all such
c
-NFs are trivialized.

For simplicity of presentation, we assume that
T=\{1,2,\dots,n-t\}
. Fix any
i\in[\ell]
, and let
C^{\prime}_{i,1},\dots,C^{\prime}_{i,m^{\prime}_{i}}
be the disjoint sub-clauses such that
S_{C^{\prime}_{i,j}}\subseteq T
. For a partial assignment
x\in\{0,1\}^{\leq n-t}
to the first
|x|
variables, we say that:

•

C^{\prime}_{i,j}
is positively determined if it is an AND gate and all literals of it are fixed to
1
, or it is an OR gate and all literals of it are fixed to
0
.

•

C^{\prime}_{i,j}
is negatively determined if it is an AND gate and one of its literals is fixed to
0
, or it is an OR gate and one of its literals is fixed to
1
.

•

C^{\prime}_{i,j}
is
d
-far from positively determined if it is not negatively determined, and there are exactly
d
of its literals that remain unfixed.

We define
\phi_{ij},\Phi_{i},\Phi:\{0,1\}^{\leq n-t}\to\{0,1\}
as follows. Given any
x\in\{0,1\}^{\leq n-t}
parsed as a partial assignment to the first
|x|
literals,

\displaystyle\Phi(x)\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\Phi_{i}(x),\quad\Phi_{i}(x)\triangleq\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}^{\prime}}(1-\phi_{ij}(x))

(4.24)

\displaystyle\phi_{ij}(x)\triangleq\begin{cases}0&C^{\prime}_{ij}\text{ is negatively determined}\\
2^{-d}&C^{\prime}_{ij}\text{ is $d$-far from positively determined}\end{cases}

(4.25)

For instructive purposes, we mention that the combinatorial interpretation of
\Phi(x)
is the expected number of
c
-NFs that are not trivialized if we extend
x
to an assignment to variables in
T
by fixing each unfixed bit uniformly at random. Note that this is not a part of the
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
proof. Instead, we prove that:

•

(
Initial Condition
). Note that
\phi_{ij}(\varepsilon)\geq 2^{-c}
and thus

\Phi_{i}(\varepsilon)\leq(1-2^{-c})^{m_{i}^{\prime}}\leq(1-2^{-c})^{k\ln n}<\frac{1}{\ell},\quad\Phi(\varepsilon)<1.

•

(
Recursion Condition
). For every
x\in\{0,1\}^{<n-t}
, we can prove that
\Phi_{i}(x)=(\Phi_{i}(x0)+\Phi_{i}(x1))/2
. To see this, notice that:

–

When the
(|x|+1)
-th variable does not appear in
C_{i,1},\dots,C_{i,m^{\prime}_{i}}
,
\Phi_{i}(x)=\Phi(x0)=\Phi(x1)
.

–

Otherwise, it appears in exactly one of
C_{i,1},\dots,C_{i,m^{\prime}_{i}}
as the clauses are disjoint. Assume for simplicity that it appears in
C_{i,1}
and it is an OR gate. Then

\displaystyle\Phi_{i}(x)=(1-\phi_{i1}(x))\cdot\prod_{j=2}^{m_{i}^{\prime}}(1-\phi_{ij}(x)),

\displaystyle\Phi_{i}(x0)=(1-2\cdot\phi_{i1}(x))\prod_{j=2}^{m_{i}^{\prime}}(1-\phi_{ij}(x)),\quad\Phi_{i}(x1)=\prod_{j=2}^{m_{i}^{\prime}}(1-\phi_{ij}(x)).

Thus
\Phi_{i}(x)=(\Phi_{i}(x0)+\Phi_{i}(x1))/2
. Subsequently,
\Phi(x)=(\Phi(x0)+\Phi(x1))/2
.

•

(
Termination Condition
).
\Phi_{i}(x)\in\{0,1\}
for
x\in\{0,1\}^{n-t}
. Moreover, if
\Phi(x)=0
, the partial assignment
x
will trivialize all
c
-NFs.

The lemma then follows from a greedy algorithm as in the
4.4.1
.
∎

See
1.7

Proof Sketch.

The proof closely follows the proof of
1.2.3
, so we will only sketch the argument. We prove it by induction on
d
in the meta-theory, and the constant
n_{0}=n_{0}^{k,d}
depends on both
k
and
d
. The case when
d=1
is easy and left as an exercise.

For
d\geq 2
, we assume towards a contradiction that
C
computes
\oplus_{n}
. We first apply the
4.4.1
to find a subset
T
of size
n-\sqrt{n}
by viewing the gates in the first layer as
1
-NFs, and then apply the
4.4.3
to find an assignment
\rho
to variables in
T
such that the gates in the first layer are either trivialized or of fan-in at most
b=O(1)
after applying
\rho
. Let
n_{1}=\sqrt{n}
. We can construct (from
C
and
\rho
) a circuit
C_{1}
that computes
\oplus_{n_{1}}
on the unfixed bits such that all gates in the first layer are of fan-in
b
.

We then apply the
4.4.1
again to find a subset
T_{1}
of size
n_{1}-\sqrt{n_{1}}
by viewing the gates in the second layer as
b
-NFs, and then apply the
4.4.3
to find an assignment
\rho_{1}
to variables in
T_{1}
such that the gates in the second layer are either trivialized or of fan-in at most
b_{1}=O(1)
after applying
\rho_{1}
. Let
n_{2}=\sqrt{n}_{1}
. We can then construct (from
C_{1}
and
\rho_{1}
) a circuit
C_{2}
of depth at most
d-1
that computes
\oplus_{n_{2}}
on the unfixed bits. The size of the circuit is at most
n^{k+1}\leq n_{2}^{6k}
. This leads to a contradiction to the induction hypothesis by setting
n_{0}^{k,d}
to be sufficiently large based on
n_{0}^{6k,d-1}
.
∎

4.5
Blum-Luby-Rubinfeld Linearity Testing

We now formalize the linearity testing algorithm due to Blum, Luby, and Rubinfeld
11
. Recall that a function
g:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
is said to be linear if
g(x\oplus y)=g(x)\oplus g(y)
, where
\oplus
denotes bit-wise XOR; equivalently,
g(x)=\langle x,z\rangle\bmod 2
for some
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. Let
g:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a function. Blum, Luby, and Rubinfeld
11
proved that for any sufficiently small constant
\varepsilon>0
:

•

(
Linearity Testing
): If
g
is
\varepsilon
-far from any linear function, then the BLR linearity testing algorithm fails with probability at least
\Omega(\varepsilon)
. Conversely, if
g
is
\varepsilon
-close to a linear function, the BLR linearity testing algorithm fails with probability at most
O(\varepsilon)
.

•

(
Self Correction
): The key idea behind linearity testing is a random self correctness algorithm: If
g
is
\varepsilon
-close to a linear function
\hat{g}
, then the function
f(x,r)\triangleq g(x\oplus r)\oplus g(r)
is a
randomized
algorithm that computes
\hat{g}
with error
O(\varepsilon)
, where
x
is the input and
r
is the random seed.

Linearity testing is the key component of the exponential length PCP theorem
{\mathsf{NP}}\subseteq{\mathsf{PCP}}[{\mathsf{poly}},1]
, which is further used to reduce the number of queries in the proof of the PCP theorem
{\mathsf{NP}}={\mathsf{PCP}}[\log n,1]
(see, e.g.,
36
).

We first state the main theorems, namely the completeness and soundness of the BLR linearity testing. The completeness states that a function that is close to a linear function is likely to be accepted. Formally:

Theorem 4.11

(Completeness of BLR linearity testing)
.

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves the following. Let
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a circuit, and
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
be a string. Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
\varepsilon<1/2
. Define the following circuits:

•

Let
T_{C}(x)
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
C(x)\neq\langle x,z\rangle\bmod 2
.

•

Let
T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}(x,y):\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that outputs
1
when
C(x)\oplus C(y)\neq C(x\oplus y)
.

Suppose that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C})\leq\varepsilon
. Then
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}})\leq 3\varepsilon+4\delta+\beta
.

The soundness states that if a function is likely to be accepted by the BLR linearity testing algorithm, then it is close to a linear function.

Theorem 4.12

(Soundness of BLR linearity testing)
.

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a circuit. Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
. Assume that
\varepsilon,\delta,\beta<0.01
. We define the following circuits:

•

For every
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
T_{C,z}(x)
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
C(x)\neq\langle x,z\rangle\bmod 2
.

•

Let
T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}(x,y)
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
C(x)\oplus C(y)\neq C(x\oplus y)
.

Suppose that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}})\leq\varepsilon
. Then there exists a string
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C,z})\leq 5\varepsilon+6\delta+\beta
.

We formalize the combinatorial proof
11
via majority correction (see
(
10
)
for an alternate proof). Note that the same proof is also formalized by Pich
78
in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
to prove the exponential PCP theorem
{\mathsf{NP}}\subseteq{\mathsf{PCP}}[{\mathsf{poly}},1]
, and our main contribution is to show that it can be formalized in the (possibly weaker) theory
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.
22
22

22

Our formalization is slightly different: We formalize linear functions
x\mapsto\langle x,z\rangle\bmod 2
by explicitly giving
z
, while Pich
77
formalizes linear functions
f
using the sentence that for every
x,y
,
f(x\oplus y)=f(x)\oplus f(y)
; nevertheless, the difference in formalization does not matter in most cases.

4.5.1
Two Useful Lemmas

Before formalizing the BLR linearity testing algorithm, we prove two useful lemmas. The first lemma shows that the acceptance probability of a circuit does not change significantly if the input is XORed with a
fixed
string. Formally:

Lemma 4.13

(Re-randomization)
.

\mathsf{APX}_{1}
proves the following statement. For every
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, circuit
T:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
, and
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
T_{x}^{\oplus}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit defined as
T_{x}^{\oplus}(r)\triangleq T(x\oplus r)
. Then
\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{x}^{\oplus})\right|\leq 2\delta+\beta
.

Proof.

We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, the circuit
T
and
T_{x}^{\oplus}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
|{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{x}^{\oplus})|>2\delta+\beta
. Then by the
2.2
, we have that

|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T)-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{x}^{\oplus})|>\beta-4\eta.

Recall that for a circuit
C
,
C^{k,z}
denotes the circuit obtained by fixing the last
k
input bits of
C
to be
z
. We will design an
n
-round iterative
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
algorithm that, in the
i
-th round, outputs a string
z_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{i}
such that

|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{i,z_{i}})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{\oplus,i,z_{i}^{\prime}}_{x})|>\beta-10\cdot(i+1)\cdot\eta.

(4.26)

where
z_{i}^{\prime}\triangleq z_{i}\oplus x_{>n-i}
. The algorithm initializes by setting
z_{0}\triangleq\varepsilon
. In the
i
-th round, it works as follows:

•

Recall that by the invariant that
Equation

4.26
holds in the
(i-1)
-th round, we have

|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{i-1,z_{i-1}})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{\oplus,i-1,z_{i-1}^{\prime}}_{x})|>\beta-10\cdot i\cdot\eta.

•

By the
2.2
, we know that

\displaystyle|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{i-1,z_{i-1}})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{\oplus,i-1,z_{i-1}^{\prime}}_{x})|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{b}(T^{i-1,z_{i-1}}))-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{b\oplus x_{n-i+1}}(T^{\oplus,i-1,z_{i-1}^{\prime}}_{x}))\right|+3\eta.

Subsequently, there is a constant
b\in\{0,1\}
such that

\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{b}(T^{i-1,z_{i-1}}))-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{b\oplus x_{n-i+1}}(T^{\oplus,i-1,z_{i-1}^{\prime}}_{x}))\right|>\beta-10\cdot i\cdot\eta-3\eta.

(4.27)

The algorithm finds such
b\in\{0,1\}
by querying the
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle, and outputs
z_{i}\triangleq b\circ z_{i-1}
.

To see that the algorithm is correct, notice that the circuit
\mathsf{Fix}_{b}(T^{i-1,z_{i-1}})
is functionally equivalent to
T^{i,z_{i}}
, and
\mathsf{Fix}_{b\oplus x_{n-i+1}}(T^{\oplus i-1,z_{i-1}^{\prime}}_{x})
is functionally equivalent to
T^{\oplus,i,z_{i}}_{x}
. Therefore, by
Equation

4.27
and the
3.1.1
, we have

|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{i,z_{i}})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{\oplus,i,z_{i}^{\prime}}_{x})|\geq\left|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{b}(T^{i-1,z_{i-1}}))-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(\mathsf{Fix}_{b\oplus x_{n-i+1}}(T^{\oplus,i-1,z_{i-1}^{\prime}}_{x}))\right|-6\eta\geq\beta-10\cdot(i+1)\cdot\eta.

The correctness of the algorithm can thus be proved by induction on a
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
term, which is available by
Theorem

2.10
.

Finally, in the
n
-th round, the algorithm outputs a string
z_{n}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that

|{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{n,z_{n}})-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{\oplus,n,z_{n}^{\prime}}_{x})|>\beta-10\cdot(n+1)\cdot\eta,

where
z_{n}^{\prime}\triangleq z_{n}\oplus x
. Note that both circuits above have input length
0
and, by the definition, must output the same value. This violates the
2.2
by setting
\eta\triangleq\beta/(100(n+1))
.
∎

The second lemma is as follows. Let
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
be two explicitly i.i.d. RVs over
\{0,1\}
. If
\Pr[X_{1}=X_{2}]
is larger than
1/2
, then
\mathbb{E}[X_{i}]
must be biased. Formally:

Lemma 4.14
.

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
V=\{0,1\}
, and
X_{1},X_{2}
be explicitly i.i.d. RVs over
V
defined by the circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
. Let
Y_{c}
be the indicator random variable of
X_{1}=X_{2}
, where
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
takes disjoint random seeds. Then for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]-\frac{1}{2}\right|\geq\sqrt{\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\delta}(Y_{c})}{2}-\frac{1}{4}-5\delta-\beta}.

Proof.

We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, circuits
C,T
, and random variables
X_{1},X_{2},Y_{c}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. Note that as
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are both the indicator random variable of
C
, we can prove by
Proposition

3.12
that
\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)\right|,\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]-{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(C)\right|\leq 3\eta
,
and subsequently

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]\right|\leq 6\eta.

(4.28)

Let
Y_{0},Y_{1}
be random variables over
\{0,1\}
such that
Y_{i}
takes
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
and output
1
if and only if
C(x_{1})=C(x_{2})=i
. It is easy to see that for every assignment
\rho=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\{0,1\}^{2n}
to the seed,
Y_{c}|_{\rho}=Y_{0}|_{\rho}+Y_{1}|_{\rho}
. Therefore, by the
3.17
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{c}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{0}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{1}]\right|\leq 6\eta.

(4.29)

Let
\overline{X}_{1},\overline{X}_{2}
be the random variables defined by
1-X_{1}
and
1-X_{2}
, respectively. Using
3.2.4
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X}_{i}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-1\right|\leq 6\eta

(4.30)

for
i\in\{1,2\}
. We can further observe that for every assignment
\rho
to the random seed,
Y_{0}|_{\rho}=\overline{X}_{1}\overline{X}_{2}|_{\rho}
and
Y_{1}|_{\rho}=X_{1}X_{2}|_{\rho}
, and subsequently by the
3.17
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{0}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X}_{1}\overline{X}_{2}]\right|\leq 6\eta,\quad\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{1}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}X_{2}]\right|\leq 6\eta.

Subsequently, by the
3.4.2
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{0}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X}_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X}_{2}]\right|\leq 14\eta,\quad\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{1}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]\right|\leq 14\eta.

(4.31)

Fix any
i\in\{1,2\}
and let
p\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[X_{i}]
,
q\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y_{C}]
. For simplicity, we assume that
0<\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]<1
and
0<\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]<1
. We can perform the following calculation:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\delta}[Y_{C}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{C}]+(\delta+2\eta)

(
3.2.2
)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{0}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{1}]+(\delta+8\eta)

(
Equation

4.29
)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X}_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X}_{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]+(\delta+36\eta)

(
Equation

4.31
)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]+6\eta)(1-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]+6\eta)+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]+(\delta+36\eta)

(
Equation

4.30
)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]+6\eta)(1+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]+12\eta)+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]\cdot(\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]-6\eta)+(\delta+36\eta)

(
Equation

4.28
)

\displaystyle\leq(1-p+\delta+14\eta)^{2}+(p+\delta+14\eta)^{2}+(\delta+36\eta)

(
3.2.2
)

\displaystyle\leq(1-p)^{2}+(\delta+14\eta)^{2}+2(1-p)(\delta+14\eta)+p^{2}+(\delta+14\eta)^{2}+2p(\delta+14\eta)+(\delta+36\eta)

\displaystyle\leq(1-p)^{2}+p^{2}+5\delta+78\eta

\displaystyle\leq 1-2(p-p^{2})+5\delta+\beta,

where the last inequality holds if we set
\eta\triangleq\beta/100
. Thus we have
p-p^{2}\leq(1-q)/2+5\delta+\beta
, and subsequently

\left|\frac{1}{2}-p\right|=\sqrt{\left(\frac{1}{2}-p\right)^{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}-(p-p^{2})}\geq\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}-\left(\frac{1-q}{2}+5\delta+\beta\right)}\geq\sqrt{\frac{q}{2}-\frac{1}{4}-5\delta-\beta}.

This completes the proof.
∎

4.5.2
Completeness of BLR Linearity Testing

We first formalize the completeness of the linearity testing algorithm. That is, if a circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
computes a function that is indeed close to a linear function
x\mapsto\langle z,x\rangle\bmod 2
, then the self-correction algorithm works. Formally:

Lemma 4.15

(Completeness of BLR self-correction)
.

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a circuit, and
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
be a string. Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
\varepsilon<1/2
. Define the following circuits:

•

Let
T_{C}(x)
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
C(x)\neq\langle x,z\rangle\bmod 2
.

•

Let
D(x,r):\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that outputs
C(x\oplus r)\oplus C(r)
.

•

For
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
T_{D,x}(r)
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
D(x,r)\neq\langle x,z\rangle\bmod 2
.

Suppose that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C})\leq\varepsilon
. Then for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{D,x})\leq 2(\delta+\varepsilon)+\beta
.

Proof.

We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, the circuit
C
,
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
. Let
T_{C},D,T_{D,x}
be the circuit as defined above, and
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. Suppose that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C})\leq\varepsilon
, we know by the
2.2
that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C})\leq\varepsilon+\delta+2\eta.

(4.32)

Fix any
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. Let
X_{1},X_{2}
be random variables over
\{0,1\}
that takes a seed
r\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, where
X_{1}=1
if
C(r)\neq\langle r,z\rangle\bmod 2
, and
X_{2}=1
if
C(x\oplus r)\neq\langle x\oplus r,z\rangle\bmod 2
. It is clear that
X_{1}
is the indicator random variable of
T_{C}
, and thus by
Equation

4.32
and
Proposition

3.12
,
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]\leq\varepsilon+\delta+5\eta
. By
Proposition

3.12
and the
4.5.1
, we can further show that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C})+6\eta\leq\varepsilon+\delta+8\eta.

Let
T_{\lor}(r)
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
C(r)\neq\langle r,z\rangle\bmod 2
or
C(x\oplus r)\neq\langle x\oplus r,z\rangle\bmod 2
, and
Y
be the indicator random variable of
T_{\lor}
. By
Proposition

3.12
and the
3.3.1
, we have

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{\lor})\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]+3\eta\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]+6\eta\leq 2\varepsilon+2\delta+19\eta.

(4.33)

Finally, we observe that if
T_{D,x}(r)=1
, then
T_{\lor}(r)=1
. To see this, assume that
T_{\lor}(r)=0
, we have

D(x,r)=C(x\oplus r)\oplus C(r)=\langle x\oplus r,z\rangle+\langle r,z\rangle\bmod 2=\langle x,z\rangle\bmod 2,

which implies that
T_{D,x}(r)=0
. Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{D,x})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{D,x})+\delta+2\eta

(
2.2
)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{\lor})+\delta+5\eta

(
3.1.1
)

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon+2\delta+24\eta.

(
Equation

4.33
)

This completes the proof by setting
\eta\triangleq\beta/30
.
∎

It can be observed that this immediately gives the completeness of the BLR identity testing algorithm. Namely, if
C
is close to a linear function, then it passes the linearity testing with high probability.

See
4.11

Proof.

We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
,
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
, and
T_{C}
,
T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}
be the circuits as described above. Let
T^{\prime}_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that given
(x,y)
, outputs
1
when
C(x\oplus y)\oplus C(y)\neq\langle x,z\rangle\bmod 2
. Let
I_{C},I_{C,\mathsf{BLR}},I_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}^{\prime}
be the indicator random variables of
T_{C},T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}},T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}^{\prime}
, respectively.

Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. Note that by the
4.5.2
and
Proposition

3.12
, we can prove that for any assignment
\rho
to be first part of the seed of
T^{\prime}_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}|_{\rho}]\leq 2(\delta+\varepsilon)+4\eta
. Subsequently, by the
3.17
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}]\leq 2(\delta+\varepsilon)+4\eta+6\eta\leq 2(\delta+\varepsilon)+10\eta.

(4.34)

By
Proposition

3.12
and
2.2
, we also have
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{C}]\leq\varepsilon+\delta+5\eta
.

It can be observed that if
T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}(x,y)=1
, then either
T_{C}(x,y)=1
or
T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}^{\prime}(x,y)=1
. Therefore, by the
3.3.1
, we can prove that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{C}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}^{\prime}]+6\eta\leq 3(\delta+\varepsilon)+15\eta.

Subsequently, by
Proposition

3.12
,
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}})\leq 3(\delta+\varepsilon)+\delta+18\eta
. The result then follows from the
2.2
by setting
\eta\triangleq\beta/30
.
∎

4.5.3
Correctness of Majority Correction

We move on to prove the soundness of the BLR linearity testing. As a first step, we prove that if
C
passes the linearity testing, then the BLR self correction algorithm is
single-valued
. Formally:

Lemma 4.16

(Single-valuedness of BLR correction)
.

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a circuit. Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
. Assume that
\varepsilon,\delta,\beta\leq 0.01
. Define the following circuits:

•

Let
T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}(x,y)
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
C(x)\oplus C(y)\neq C(x\oplus y)
.

•

Let
D_{x,b}(r):\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
C(x\oplus r)\oplus C(r)=b
.

Suppose that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}})\leq\varepsilon
. For every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(D_{x,b})\geq 1-4\varepsilon-(4\delta+\beta)
for some
b\in\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, the circuit
C
, and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
. Let
T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}
and
D_{x,b}
be the circuits as defined above, and
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. Suppose that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}})\leq\varepsilon
, we know by the
2.2
that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}})\leq\varepsilon+\delta+2\eta.

(4.35)

Fix any
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. Let
D_{x}^{\prime}:\{0,1\}^{2n}\to\{0,1\}
be circuit that takes
(r_{1},r_{2})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
are the input, and outputs
1
if
C(x\oplus r_{1})\oplus C(r_{1})=C(x\oplus r_{2})\oplus C(r_{2})
. Let
Y
be the indicator random variable of
D_{x}^{\prime}
, and
\overline{Y}
be the indicator random variable of
1-D_{x}^{\prime}
. It follows from
3.17
that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]\geq 1-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{Y}]-6\eta
.

Consider the following two circuits
T,T^{\prime}:\{0,1\}^{2n}\to\{0,1\}
:

•

T(r_{1},r_{2})\triangleq 1
if and only if
C(r_{1})\oplus C(r_{2})\neq C(r_{1}\oplus r_{2})
.

•

T^{\prime}(r_{1},r_{2})\triangleq 1
if and only if
C(x\oplus r_{1})\oplus C(x\oplus r_{2})\neq C((x\oplus r_{1})\oplus(x\oplus r_{2}))
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}
be the indicator random variable of
T
and
T^{\prime}
, respectively. It is clear that
T
is exactly
T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}
, and thus by
Equation

4.35
,
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T)\leq\varepsilon+\delta+2\eta
. Similarly, as
T_{i}^{\prime}
is obtained
T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}
by taking bitwise-XOR to the input string with the fixed string
(x,x)
, by
Equation

4.35
and the
4.5.1
,
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{\prime})\leq\eta+\delta+5\eta
.

Moreover, one can observe that
D_{x}^{\prime}(x,y)=0
implies that either
T(x,y)
or
T^{\prime}(x,y)
outputs
1
: This is because if
T(x,y)=T^{\prime}(x,y)=0
, we can conclude that

C(x\oplus r_{1})\oplus C(x\oplus r_{2})\oplus C(r_{1})\oplus C(r_{2})=C(r_{1}\oplus r_{2})\oplus C((x\oplus r_{1})\oplus(x\oplus r_{2}))=0,

which implies that
D_{x}^{\prime}(x,y)=1
. Subsequently, by the
3.3.1
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{Y}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}^{\prime}]+3\eta\leq 2(\varepsilon+\delta)+10\eta.

and thus
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]\geq 1-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{Y}]-6\eta\geq 1-2(\varepsilon+\delta)-16\eta
.

Let
I_{x},I_{x}^{\prime}
be explicitly i.i.d. RVs over
\{0,1\}
defined by the circuit
D_{x}(r)\triangleq C(x\oplus r)\oplus C(r)
, and
Y_{c}
is the indicator random variable of
I_{x}=I_{x}^{\prime}
. By definitions, we can see that
Y_{c}|_{\rho}=Y|_{\rho}
for any assignment
\rho
, and thus by the
3.17
,

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y_{c}]\geq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]-6\eta\geq 1-2(\varepsilon+\delta)-22\eta.

Subsequently, by
Lemma

4.14
, we have

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{x}]-\frac{1}{2}\right|\geq\sqrt{\frac{1-2(\varepsilon+\delta)-22\eta}{2}-\frac{1}{4}-6\eta}\geq\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}-(\varepsilon+\delta+17\eta)}\geq\frac{1}{2}-4(\varepsilon+\delta+17\eta).

(4.36)

Recall that
I_{x}
is the random variable that takes
(r_{1},r_{2})\in\{0,1\}^{2n}
as random seed and outputs
C(x\oplus r_{1})\oplus C(r_{1})
. Suppose that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{x}]\geq 1-4(\varepsilon+\delta+17\eta)
. By
3.17
, there is an assignment
\rho
of the second part
r_{2}
of the seed (which was for
I_{x}^{\prime}
) such that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{x}|_{\rho}]\geq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{x}]-6\eta\geq 1-4\eta-(4\delta+74\eta).

As
I_{x}|_{\rho}
is the indicator random variable of
D_{x,1}
, it follows from
Proposition

3.12
that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(D_{x,1})\geq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{x}]-3\eta\geq 1-4\varepsilon-(4\delta+74\eta)
. It suffices if we set
\eta\leq\beta/100
. The other case
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[I_{x}]\leq 4(\varepsilon+\delta+17\eta)
can be resolved by considering
\overline{I_{x}}\triangleq 1-I_{x}
.
∎

Lemma

4.16
shows that the BLR self correction algorithm is single-valued assuming that the circuit
C
passes the linearity testing. Second, we show that the “corrected” function
g(\cdot)
satisfies that
g(x)\oplus g(y)=g(x\oplus y)
for
every

x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. Formally:

Lemma 4.17

(Linearity of BLR correction)
.

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves the following statement. Let
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a circuit. Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
. Assume that
\varepsilon,\delta,\beta\leq 0.01
. Let
D_{x,b}
be the circuit in
Lemma

4.16
, and
g(x)
be the
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle circuit that works as follows: Given
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, it outputs
b\in\{0,1\}
if
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(D_{x,b})\geq 1-4\varepsilon-(4\delta+\beta)
, and
\bot
otherwise.

Let
T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}(x,y)
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
C(x)\oplus C(y)\neq C(x\oplus y)
. Suppose that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}})\leq\varepsilon
. Then for every
x_{1},x_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
g(x_{1})\oplus g(x_{2})=g(x_{1}\oplus x_{2})
.

Proof.

We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, the circuit
C
, and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
. Assume that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}})\leq\varepsilon
. Note that by
Lemma

4.16
and
\varepsilon,\delta,\beta<0.01
,
g(x)\in\{0,1\}
for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. Fix
x_{1},x_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and let
b_{j}\triangleq g(x_{j})
for
j\in\{1,2\}
, and
b_{\oplus}\triangleq g(x_{1}\oplus x_{2})
.

Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later, and
X_{1},X_{2},X_{\oplus}
be the random variables over
\{0,1\}
with seed
r\in\{0,1\}^{n}
defined as follows:

•

X_{1}
outputs
1
if and only if
b_{1}=C(x_{1}\oplus r)\oplus C(r)
.

•

X_{2}
outputs
1
if and only if
b_{2}=C(x_{2}\oplus r)\oplus C(r)
.

•

X_{\oplus}
outputs
1
if and only if
b_{\oplus}=C(x_{1}\oplus x_{2}\oplus(x_{2}\oplus r))\oplus C(x_{2}\oplus r)
.

Note that as
b_{j}\triangleq g(x_{j})
, we know by the definition of
g
that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(D_{x_{j},b_{j}})\geq 1-4\varepsilon-(4\delta+\beta)
. It can be observed that
X_{1},X_{2}
are the indicator random variables of
D_{x_{1},b_{1}},D_{x_{2},b_{2}}
, thus by
Proposition

3.12
and the
3.2.2
,

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}],\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}]\geq 1-4\varepsilon-(5\delta+\beta-2\eta).

Moreover,
X_{\oplus}
is the indicator variable of the circuit that outputs
1
if
b_{\oplus}=C(x_{1}\oplus x_{2}\oplus(x_{2}\oplus r))\oplus C(x_{2}\oplus r)
, and the circuit is obtained from
D_{x_{1}\oplus x_{2},b_{\oplus}}
by taking XOR to the input with a fixed string
x_{2}
. Therefore, by
Proposition

3.12
and the
4.5.1
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{0.01}[X_{\oplus}]\geq 1-4\varepsilon-(5\delta+\beta-2\eta)
.

Let
\overline{X}_{1}\triangleq 1-X_{1}
,
\overline{X}_{2}\triangleq 1-X_{2}
, and
\overline{X}_{\oplus}\triangleq 1-X_{\oplus}
. Let
Y\triangleq\overline{X}_{1}\lor\overline{X}_{2}\lor\overline{X}_{\oplus}
and
\overline{Y}\triangleq 1-Y
. Then using
3.2.4
and
3.3.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X}_{1}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X}_{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{X}_{\oplus}]+9\eta

(
3.3.1
)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}])+(1-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{2}])+(1-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{\oplus}])+18\eta

(
3.2.4
)

\displaystyle\leq 12\varepsilon+15\delta+\beta+24\eta.

Again, using
3.2.4
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{Y}]\geq 1-(12\varepsilon+15\delta+\beta+24\eta)
. By setting
\eta\triangleq\beta/100
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[\overline{Y}]>3\eta
.

By
3.17
, there exists an assignment
\rho
such that
\mathbb{E}_{0.01}[\overline{Y}|_{\rho}]\geq 0.05
, or in other words,
\overline{Y}|_{\rho}=1
as its seed length is
0
after applying the restriction
\rho
. By the definition of the random variables, this indicates that

\displaystyle b_{1}

\displaystyle=C(x_{1}\oplus r)\oplus C(r),

\displaystyle b_{2}

\displaystyle=C(x_{2}\oplus r)\oplus C(r),

\displaystyle b_{\oplus}

\displaystyle=C(x_{1}\oplus x_{2}\oplus(x_{2}\oplus r))\oplus C(x_{2}\oplus r)=C(x_{1}\oplus r)\oplus C(x_{2}\oplus r).

It immediately follows that
b_{\oplus}=b_{1}\oplus b_{2}
.
∎

4.5.4
Soundness of BLR Linearity Testing

Now we are ready to prove the soundness of the BLR linearity testing. At a high level, we will recover the string
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
that defines the linear function using the oracle circuit
g(\cdot)
. It is worth noting that the correctness proof of the string
z
is quite non-trivial: It crucially builds on the tools for random variables developed in
Section

3
, especially
3.17
.

See
4.12

Proof.

We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Fix
n,\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, the circuit
C
, and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
. Let
T_{C,z}
and
T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}
be the circuits as defined above, and
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. Suppose that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}})\leq\varepsilon
, we know by the
2.2
that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}})\leq\varepsilon+\delta+2\eta.

(4.37)

Let
D_{x,b}(r)\triangleq C(x\oplus r)\oplus C(r)\oplus(1-b)
be the circuit in
Lemma

4.16
, and
g(x)
be the oracle circuit in
Lemma

4.17
. By
Lemma

4.16
, we have that for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, there is a
b\in\{0,1\}
such that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(D_{x,b})\geq 1-4(\varepsilon+\delta+2\eta)-5\eta\geq 1-4(\varepsilon+\delta)-13\eta.

(4.38)

Note that we will choose
\eta
such that
13\eta<0.01
. Therefore, by
Lemma

4.17
that for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, the bit
b
satisfying
Equation

4.38
is given by
g(x)
. Moreover, for every
x_{1},x_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, we have that
g(x_{1})\oplus g(x_{2})=g(x_{1}\oplus x_{2})
.

Let
e_{i}
be the string that is
0
on all but the
i
-th bit, and
z\triangleq g(e_{1})\circ g(e_{2})\circ\dots\circ g(e_{n})
. That is,
z_{i}=g(e_{i})
for every
i\in[n]
. Let
X
and
Y
be random variables over
\{0,1\}
that take a seed
(x,r)\in\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
of length
2n
and are defined as follows.

•

X
outputs
1
if
C(x\oplus r)\oplus C(x)\neq C(r)
. That is,
X
is the indicator random variable of
T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}}
. By
Proposition

3.12
,

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X]\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C,\mathsf{BLR}})+3\eta\leq\varepsilon+\delta+5\eta.

(4.39)

•

Y
outputs
1
if
C(x)\neq\langle x,z\rangle\bmod 2
. Note that for every assignment
r
to the second part of its seed,
Y|_{r}
is the indicator random variable of
T_{C,z}
.

Next, we will prove that for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X|_{x}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y|_{x}]\right|\leq 4(\varepsilon+\delta)+13\eta.

(4.40)

Recall that by the definition of
X
and
D_{x,b}
, we have that for every assignment
x
to the
first
part of their seeds,
X|_{x}
is the indicator random variable of
D_{x,1}
, and for
\overline{X}\triangleq 1-X
,
\overline{X}|_{x}
is the indicator random variable of
D_{x,0}
. Therefore, by
Lemma

4.16
,

\max\{\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X|_{x}],1-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X|_{x}]\}\geq 1-4(\varepsilon+\delta+2\eta)-5\eta\geq 1-4(\varepsilon+\delta)-13\eta.

We consider the case that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X|_{x}]\geq 1-4(\varepsilon+\delta)-13\eta
, and the other case is similar. By the definition of
g
, we know that
g(x)=1
, and subsequently

\langle x,z\rangle\bmod 2=\sum_{i\in[n],x_{i}=1}g(e_{i})\bmod 2=g(x),

where the last equality follows from
Lemma

4.17
and the
2.4
(note that the induction axiom suffices as
n\in\mathsf{Log}
). For any assignment
r
to the second part of the seed, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y|_{x}|_{y}]=1
, which subsequently implies that
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y|_{x}]=1
. Therefore, for any assignment
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
to the first part of the seed,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X|_{x}]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y|_{x}]\right|\leq 4(\varepsilon+\delta)+13\eta.

By the
3.17
, we have
\left|\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X]-\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]\right|\leq 4(\varepsilon+\delta)+19\eta
, and thus

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X]+4(\varepsilon+\delta)+19\eta\leq e.

Again, by the
3.17
, there is an assignment
r\in\{0,1\}^{n}
to the second part of its seed such that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y|_{r}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]+3\eta\leq 5(\varepsilon+\delta)+27\eta.

(4.41)

Fix the assignment
r
. As mentioned above,
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y|_{r}]
is the indicator random variable of
T_{C,z}
, and thus by
Proposition

3.12
and
2.2
,

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{C,z})\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{C,z})+\delta+2\eta\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y|_{r}]+\delta+5\eta\leq 5\varepsilon+6\delta+32\eta.

It completes the proof by taking
\eta\triangleq\beta/50
.
∎

5
Witnessing Theorems and Relative Strength of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}

In this section, we prove a witnessing theorem for
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
and consider its relation to other theories of bounded arithmetic, including
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
and
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
.

5.1
Provably Total
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
Problems in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}

In this subsection, we will introduce a witnessing theorem for the
\forall\Sigma_{1}^{b}
-consequences of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
(i.e. provably total
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
problems in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
).

5.1.1
A
\mathsf{TFZPP}
Problem:
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})

We will first introduce a
\mathsf{TFZPP}
problem
23
23

23

A search problem is said to be in
\mathsf{TFZPP}
if it is a
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
problem solvable by randomized polynomial-time algorithms.
called
Refutation of Yao-Predictor Generators
; we denote it by
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
. Recall that Yao’s distinguisher-to-predictor transformation
90
(see
Section

4.1
) shows that if a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\{0,1\}^{n}
is not
\varepsilon
-pseudorandom, i.e., there is a circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
(called
distinguisher
) such that

\Big|\Pr[C(\mathcal{D})]-\Pr[C(\mathcal{U}_{n})]\Big|>\varepsilon,

then there exists
i\in[n]
and a
predictor

P_{i}:\{0,1\}^{i-1}\to\{0,1\}
such that

\Pr_{x\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[P(x_{<i})=x_{i}]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4n},

i.e.,
P
predicts the
i
-th bit of
\mathcal{D}
with advantage at least
\varepsilon/4n
.
This transformation serves as a key step in the construction and analysis of pseudorandom generators (see, e.g.,
71
;
40
): it shows that an unpredictable distribution is necessarily pseudorandom.

In the statement below, we say that a discrete probability distribution
\mathcal{D}
is
flat
if it is uniform over its support, i.e., over the set of elements with non-zero probability over
\mathcal{D}
. The
size
of the distribution is the size of its support. We will represent flat distributions explicitly as a list of strings. In the subsequent discussions, we might tacitly assume that the relevant distribution is flat and explicitly represented.

Definition 5.1
.

The search problem
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
is defined as follows.

•

(
Parameters
). Length of strings
n
, distribution size
m
, predictor size
s
, and advantage
\delta\in[0,1]
.

•

(
Input
). A circuit
G:\{0,1\}^{nm}\to[n]\times\{0,1\}^{s}
(called
predictor generator
).

•

(
Solution
). Any explicit flat distribution
\mathcal{D}\in(\{0,1\}^{n})^{m}
of size
m
such that the following holds:

Let
(i,P)\triangleq G(\mathcal{D})
, where
P:\{0,1\}^{i-1}\to\{0,1\}
is parsed as a circuit of description length
\leq s
. Then

\Pr_{x\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[P(x_{<i})=x_{i}]<\frac{1}{2}+\delta.

In other words,
P
is not a predictor of the
i
-th bit of
\mathcal{D}
with advantage
\delta
.

For concreteness, one may think of the parameter regime
m=n^{10}
,
s=n^{2}
, and
\delta=0.1
. In this case, a random distribution of
m
strings of length
n
is likely
o(1)
-pseudorandom against any circuit of size
s
, and thus must be a solution of
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
no matter the input circuit
G
.

At a high level,
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
asks to generate a distribution
\mathcal{D}
that is unpredictable against a
given
predictor generator
G
— a deterministic algorithm that aims to output a predictor
P
for
\mathcal{D}
. The distribution
\mathcal{D}
is not necessarily an unpredictable (or equivalently, pseudorandom) distribution against
small circuits
; it suffices to fool the given
deterministic
predictor generator
G
. This makes it a special case of constructing targeted PRGs, which is known to be
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}
-complete (see, e.g.,
33
;
23
;
64
).

5.1.2
Connection to
\mathsf{LossyCode}

A closely related
\mathsf{TFZPP}
relation is the
Lossy Code Problem
; we denote it by
\mathsf{LossyCode}
. Inspired by the literature in bounded arithmetic (see
(
46
, Section 3.1)
and the discussion below), the problem is defined in
55
as a more feasible variant of the Range Avoidance Problem; see
56
and references therein for an introduction to this line of work.

Definition 5.2
.

The search problem
\mathsf{LossyCode}
is defined as follows.

•

(
Input
). Circuits
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{n-1}
and
D:\{0,1\}^{n-1}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
. These two circuits are called
compressor
and
decompressor
, respectively.

•

(
Output
). A string
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
D(C(x))\neq x
.

It is clear that
\mathsf{LossyCode}\in\mathsf{TFZPP}
. Indeed, Wilkie (unpublished) and Thapen
85
proved that the problem captures the
\forall\Sigma_{1}^{b}
-fragment of the theory
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
.

Theorem 5.3

(
(
43
, Proposition 1.14)
, also see
(
64
, Theorem D.1)
)
.

Let
\phi(x,y)
be a quantifier-free formula in the language of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
that only has
x
and
y
as open variables. If
\mathsf{APC}_{1}\vdash\forall x~\exists y~\phi(x,y)
, then there is a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from the following problem to
\mathsf{LossyCode}
: Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, output
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\phi(n,m)
is true in the standard model.

Moreover, it has been recently discovered that some natural
\mathsf{TFZPP}
-search problems admit deterministic reductions to
\mathsf{LossyCode}
or its variants: constructing large prime numbers with factoring oracles
55
and the simulation of catalytic logspace machines
26
. Variants of
\mathsf{LossyCode}
are relevant to both full and partial derandomizations of
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}
; see
64
for a comprehensive introduction.

Note that assuming
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
, both
\mathsf{LossyCode}
and
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
are in
{\mathsf{FP}}
. Nevertheless, it is interesting to discover the relative hardness of their derandomization. By adapting an idea from
55
, we show that
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
admits a deterministic polynomial-time reduction to
\mathsf{LossyCode}
. Therefore, showing that
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})\in{\mathsf{FP}}
is necessary before proving that
\mathsf{LossyCode}\in{\mathsf{FP}}
.

Theorem 5.4

(Implicit in the proof of
(
55
, Corollary 41)
)
.

There is a deterministic polynomial-time mapping reduction from
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
with parameters
(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m\geq s+\lceil\log n\rceil+1
to
\mathsf{LossyCode}
.

Proof.

Note that we can encode
m
-bit strings with Hamming weight at most
k
by
\log_{2}\binom{m}{k}+O(\log k)
bits, where the encoding and decoding algorithms run in polynomial time (see, e.g.,
(
19
, Lemma 5.4)
). In particular, when
k\triangleq(1/2-\delta)\cdot m
and
m
is sufficiently large, the encoding length is

\displaystyle~\log_{2}\binom{m}{(1/2-\delta)\cdot m}+O(\log(1/2-\delta)+\log m)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~m+\log_{2}(e^{-(2\delta)^{2}m/4})+O(\log(1/2-\delta)+\log m)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~m-(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m,

where the first inequality follows from the Chernoff bound.

Now we describe the reduction. Given any predictor generator
G:\{0,1\}^{nm}\to[n]\times\{0,1\}^{s}
, consider the following compressor
C:\{0,1\}^{nm}\to\{0,1\}^{nm-1}
and decompressor
D:\{0,1\}^{nm-1}\to\{0,1\}^{nm}
:

•

(
Compressor
). Given any
\mathcal{D}\in\{0,1\}^{nm}
, the compressor parses it as a distribution over
n
-bit strings of size
m
. It computes
(i,P)\triangleq G(\mathcal{D})
. If
P
fails to predict the
i
-th bit of
\mathcal{D}
with advantage
\delta
, it fails and aborts. Otherwise,

\Pr_{x\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[P(x_{<i})=x_{i}]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\delta.

Let
y
be the
m
-bit string defined as
y_{j}\triangleq P(x_{<i}^{(j)})\oplus x_{i}^{(j)}
, where
x^{(j)}
is the
j
-th string in
\mathcal{D}
. Then
y
is a string of Hamming weight at most
(1/2-\delta)\cdot m
, and thus can be efficiently encoded using
m-(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m
bits.

Let
\hat{y}
be its encoding, and
\mathcal{D}_{-i}\in\{0,1\}^{m(n-1)}
be the distribution
\mathcal{D}
after removing the
i
-th bit from all strings. The compressor outputs the tuple
(i,P,\hat{y},\mathcal{D}_{-i})
, which is of length at most

\lceil\log n\rceil+s+(m-(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m)+(nm-m)<nm

due to the assumption on parameters.

•

(
Decompressor
). When the compressor does not fail, the decompressor can recover
\mathcal{D}
from
(i,P,\hat{y},\mathcal{D}_{-i})
by first recovering
y
then computing the missing bits

x^{(j)}_{i}\triangleq P(x_{<i}^{(j)})\oplus y_{j}.

Given a predictor generator
G
and parameters as above, the mapping reduction from
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
to
\mathsf{LossyCode}
outputs
(C,D)
as an instance of
\mathsf{LossyCode}
.

It suffices to prove that the reduction is correct. Given any string
\mathcal{D}
such that
D(C(\mathcal{D}))\neq\mathcal{D}
, we know by the discussion above that the compressor must fail. In other words,
G(\mathcal{D})
fails to produce a predictor with advantage
\delta
. This means that
\mathcal{D}
is a solution to the
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
instance and thus concludes the proof.
∎

5.1.3
The Witnessing Theorem

We are now ready to show the following witnessing theorem for
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
: any provably total
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
problem in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is deterministically reducible to
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
.

See
1.9

Note that the inequality
(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m\geq s+\lceil\log n\rceil+1
implies that the
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
instance reduces to
\mathsf{LossyCode}
and, in particular, it is a total search problem. A more refined analysis of our proof may lead to an improved trade-off between the parameters, which we leave for future work.

To prove this witnessing theorem, we will need the standard Herbrand’s theorem for universal first-order theories and a lemma that extracts a predictor from an
{\mathsf{APX}}
proof. The latter requires a proof-theoretic analysis and is deferred to the end of the section (see
Section

5.4
).

Theorem 5.5

(Herbrand’s Theorem; see, e.g.,
15
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a universal first-order theory and
\varphi(x,y)
be a quantifier-free formula with only
x
and
y
as open variables. If
\mathcal{T}\vdash\forall x~\exists y~\varphi(x,y)
, there exists a constant
c\in\mathbb{N}
and terms
t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{c}
such that

\mathcal{T}\vdash\forall x~\bigvee_{i=1}^{c}\varphi(x,t_{i}(y))\,.

Lemma 5.6

(Predictor Extraction Lemma)
.

Let
t_{1}(\vec{x})=t_{2}(\vec{x})
be an equation provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}
. Then there are polynomials
k(n)
,
m(n)
, and a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm
E
that satisfies the following conditions when
n
is sufficiently large:

•

(
Input
)
. A string
\vec{x}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and a flat distribution
\mathcal{D}\in(\{0,1\}^{k})^{m}
of size
m
over
k
-bit strings.

•

(
Simulation of Terms
)
. Recall that
t_{1},t_{2}
are interpreted as polynomial-time
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle algorithms in standard models. We will simulate the algorithms on input
\vec{x}
as follows: For every oracle query
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
, where
C:\{0,1\}^{t}\to\{0,1\}
, we will ensure that
t\leq k(n)
and answer the query by

{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)\triangleq\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{\leq t})].

(5.1)

We denote the output of
t_{1}
in the simulation as
t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x})
, and the output of
t_{2}
as
t_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x})
.

•

(
Output
)
. Suppose that
t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x})\neq t_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x})
. Then
E(\vec{x},\mathcal{D})
outputs
i\in[k(n)]
and a circuit
P\colon\{0,1\}^{i-1}\to\{0,1\}
of size at most
s
such that
P
predicts the
i
-th bit of
\mathcal{D}
with advantage
\delta
such that

(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m(n)\geq s+\lceil\log k(n)\rceil+1.

Proof of
Theorem

1.9
.

Recall that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
admits a universal axiomatization (see
Proposition

2.8
). Suppose that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}\vdash\forall x~\exists y~\varphi(x,y)
. By Herbrand’s theorem, there are terms
t_{1},\dots,t_{c}
in the language of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
such that

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}\vdash\forall x~\bigvee_{i=1}^{c}\varphi(x,t_{i}(y))\,,

for some
c\in\mathbb{N}
.

Note that the language of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is the language of
\mathsf{PV}
extended by the approximate counting oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
; therefore,
t_{1},\dots,t_{c}
are polynomial time
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle algorithms in the standard model. Let
t_{\varphi}
be a term in
{\mathsf{APX}}
such that

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}\vdash\bigvee_{i=1}^{c}\varphi(x,t_{i}(x))\leftrightarrow t_{\varphi}(x,t_{1}(x),\dots,t_{c}(x))=1.

This can be done as
\varphi
is a quantifier-free formula; see, e.g.,
(
65
, Chapter 3)
. Then we know that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves that
\forall x~t_{\varphi}(x,t_{1}(x),\dots,t_{c}(x))=1
. As
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is conservative over
{\mathsf{APX}}
(see
Proposition

2.9
), we know that
{\mathsf{APX}}\vdash t_{\varphi}(x,t_{1}(x),\dots,t_{c}(x))=1
.

By
Lemma

5.6
(instantiated with
t_{1}\triangleq t_{\varphi}(x,t_{1}(x),\dots,t_{c}(x))
and
t_{2}\triangleq 1
), there are
k=k(n)
,
m=m(n)
,
s=s(n)
,
\delta=\delta(n)
and a polynomial time
E
such that the following holds. Given
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and a distribution
\mathcal{D}\in(\{0,1\}^{k})^{m}
of size
m
,

•

either
t_{\varphi}^{\mathcal{D}}(x,t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(x),\dots,t_{k}^{\mathcal{D}}(x))=1
; or

•

E(x,\mathcal{D})
outputs
i\in[k(n)]
and
P
that predicts the
i
-th bit of
\mathcal{D}
with advantage
\delta
, where
(\delta(n)^{2}/10)\cdot m(n)\geq s(n)+\lceil\log k(n)\rceil+1
.

The reduction produces the circuit
E(x,\cdot)
as an instance of
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
, where the size-
m(n)
distribution is supported over
k(n)
-bit strings, the predictor size is
s(n)
, and the advantage is
\delta(n)
. Given
x
of length
n
, for any solution
\mathcal{D}
to the resulting instance
E(x,\cdot)
of
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
, we know by definition that
E(x,\mathcal{D})
cannot output a predictor with advantage
\delta
. As a result, the first bullet above must hold:

t_{\varphi}^{\mathcal{D}}(x,t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(x),\dots,t_{k}^{\mathcal{D}}(x))=1.

Subsequently, given any solution
\mathcal{D}
to the instance of
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
, one of
t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(x),t_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(x),\dots,t_{k}^{\mathcal{D}}(x)
must output
y
such that
\varphi(x,y)
holds. This gives a correct reduction, as simulations of
t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(x),t_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(x),\dots,t_{k}^{\mathcal{D}}(x)
can be implemented in deterministic polynomial time given
x
and the explicit description of
\mathcal{D}
.
∎

5.2
Relationship to
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
: Is
\mathsf{prBPP}=\mathsf{prP}
Feasibly Provable?

In this subsection, we introduce a few questions regarding the relative strength of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
and
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. We will discuss their importance and connection to the program of proving
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
. No meaningful progress is reported in the paper; we believe the resolution of the questions, even conditionally, would advance our understanding of feasible mathematics and derandomization.

Feasible proof of
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
.

One major open problem in complexity theory is whether derandomization is possible in general with polynomial runtime overhead. The seminal work of Nisan, Wigderson, and Impagliazzo
71
;
40
shows that
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
follows from exponential circuit lower bounds for
{\mathsf{E}}={\mathsf{DTIME}}[2^{n}]
; hence many researchers expect a positive answer. However, despite enormous efforts, both
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
and the circuit lower bounds for
{\mathsf{E}}
remain open.

From the perspective of meta-mathematics, an interesting question is to study whether
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
is
(
un
)
provable
in a weak arithmetic theory such as
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. A technical challenge is that, as the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
is designed to capture
deterministic
polynomial time computable functions, it is a priori not obvious how to formalize the statement
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
, which involves the acceptance probability of circuits over inputs from a set of exponential size.

We propose the investigation of the following related question.

Open Problem 3
.

Is there a
\mathsf{PV}
function symbol
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
such that the
2.2
,
2.2
,
2.2
, and
2.2
are provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
?

We note that an unconditional positive answer is unlikely to be obtained in the near future, as it immediately implies
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
by the soundness of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
and
Theorem

2.5
. Indeed, a positive answer shows, intuitively, that
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
admits a
deterministic polynomial-time proof
. To our knowledge, it is unclear whether a positive or negative answer is more plausible.

Feasibly provable derandomization for deterministic statements.

On the other hand, we may also consider a weaker collapse: it is in principle possible that, despite that there may not be a
\mathsf{PV}
function symbol
{\mathsf{P}}
such that the relevant axioms of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
are provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
, the introduction of the oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
does not help in proving any sentence that
does not
involve the oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
. Formally:

Open Problem 4
.

Is
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}

conservative
over
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
? In other words, is it the case that every first-order sentence in the language of
\mathsf{PV}
that is provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is also provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
?

It is clear that a positive answer to
3
implies a positive answer to
4
. Moreover, a positive answer of this open problem would immediately imply a witnessing theorem that improves
Theorem

1.9
: any
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
provably total
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
relation (expressed by a quantifier-free formula in the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
) is in
{\mathsf{FP}}
. This is because
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
provably total
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
problems are in
{\mathsf{FP}}
(see, e.g.,
(
74
, Section 3.1)
).

An interesting characteristic of
4
is that it appears to be
incomparable
with
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
. If
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
but the proof is not feasible,
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
may not be conservative over
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. More interestingly, if the answer to
4
is positive, it is still unclear to us whether
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
or any other nontrivial derandomization follows. Formally:

Open Problem 5
.

Suppose that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is conservative over
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. Does it follow that
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{P}}
,
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}=\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{ZPP}}
, or any other unknown general derandomization result hold?

At a high level, this is to ask whether it is necessary to
derandomize computations in general
if we want to
derandomize proofs in general
. We contend that these problems are fundamental and merit deeper investigation.

5.3
Relationship to
\mathsf{APC}_{1}

We now study the relative strength of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
and
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
. We will show that, in a formal sense,
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
can be viewed as an extension of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. We will then show that
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
is likely a
strict
extension of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. Finally, we introduce a few open problems related to the relative strength of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
and
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

5.3.1
An Upper Bound:
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
Extends
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}

We first prove an upper bound for
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
that is implicit in
Jeřábek
’s results on approximate counting
46
. In particular, this shows that a provable first-order sentence in the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
is also provable in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
.

We start by defining a sentence
\mathsf{Hard}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\varepsilon}(\alpha)
in the language of the relativized theory
\mathsf{PV}_{1}(\alpha)
. This sentence formalizes that
\alpha(1^{2^{n}})
outputs a truth table of length
2^{n}
that is
2^{-\varepsilon n}
-hard on average. Formally:

Definition 5.7

(
(
46
, Definition 2.1)
)
.

\mathsf{Hard}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\varepsilon}(\alpha)
is the following sentence in the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}(\alpha)
: For every
n\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
and
x
such that
||x||=n
,
\alpha(x)\in\{0,1\}^{2^{n}}
is a truth-table of a Boolean function
f
in
n
variables such that for every circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
of size at most
2^{\varepsilon n}
,

\Pr_{x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}}[C(x)=f(x)]\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2^{\varepsilon n}}.

Note that this probability is defined by a brute-force exact counting algorithm as
n\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
.

Definition 5.8

(
(
46
, Definition 2.13)
)
.

The theory
\mathsf{HARD}^{\mathsf{A}}
is defined as
\mathsf{PV}_{1}(\alpha)+\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV}(\alpha))+\mathsf{Hard}^{\mathsf{A}}_{1/4}(\alpha)
, where
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV}(\alpha))
denotes the dual Weak Pigeonhole Principle for
\mathsf{PV}(\alpha)
functions.

The following theorem can be proved using tools from
46
, where
\mathsf{NW}(\cdot,\cdot)
is an instantiation of the Nisan-Wigderson PRG
71
with the hard truth table provided by
\alpha(\cdot)
. The proof is straightforward but requires familiarity with the theory
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
; for completeness, we provide a proof of the theorem in
Section

5.5
.

Theorem 5.9

(Simulating
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
with
\mathsf{NW}(C,\Delta)
)
.

For every
\varepsilon<1/3
, there is a term
\mathsf{NW}(C,\Delta)
in the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}(\alpha)
such that
2.2
,
2.2
,
2.2
, and
2.2
are provable in
\mathsf{HARD}^{\mathsf{A}}
when the oracle
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
is replaced by
\mathsf{NW}(C,\Delta)
.

We note that by
(
46
, Theorem 2.13)
, the theory
\mathsf{HARD}^{\mathsf{A}}
is a conservative extension of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
. It then immediately follows that:

Corollary 5.10
.

Any first-order sentence in the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is also provable in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
.

5.3.2
A Conditional Separation:
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
is Likely Stronger Than
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}

As
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
is an alternative theory for polynomial-time approximate counting and probabilistic reasoning, an interesting question is whether it is
strictly
weaker than
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
. We provide a positive answer under plausible assumptions, by adapting a technique from
37
.

A main technical tool is a KPT witnessing theorem (see
57
;
74
) for the theory
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, where the “student” is implemented by polynomial-size circuits. Formally:

Definition 5.11

(KPT Witnessing with Circuits)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be an extension of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
. We say that
\mathcal{T}
satisfies the
KPT witnessing property with circuits
if the following holds. Let
\varphi(\vec{x},y,z)
be any quantifier-free formula in the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
such that
\mathcal{T}\vdash\forall\vec{x}~\exists y~\forall z~\varphi(\vec{x},y,z)
. Then there is a constant
k\in\mathbb{N}
and functions
f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k}
(in the standard model) such that the following holds.

For every vector
\vec{x}
of strings and every
z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{k}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, it holds in the standard model that:

•

either
\varphi(\vec{x},f_{1}(\vec{x}),z_{1})
is true;

•

or
\varphi(\vec{x},f_{2}(\vec{x},z_{1}),z_{2})
is true;

•

or
\varphi(\vec{x},f_{3}(\vec{x},z_{1},z_{2}),z_{3})
is true;

•

\dots
;

•

or
\varphi(\vec{x},f_{k}(\vec{x},z_{1},\dots,z_{k-1}),z_{k})
is true.

Moreover, over any fixed input length for
\vec{x}
,
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
are computable by
polynomial-size deterministic circuits
.

Theorem 5.12

(KPT Witnessing for
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
)
.

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
admits the KPT witnessing property with circuits.

The theorem can be proved using the standard KPT witnessing theorem (see, e.g.,
(
74
, Theorem 3.2)
) and the fact that the circuit acceptance probability problem is solvable by (non-uniform) polynomial-size circuits.
24
24

24

A similar KPT witnessing theorem is proved in
(
76
, Theorem 4)
(see also
(
37
, Theorem 25)
) for the theory
\mathsf{PV}_{1}+
“uniform
\mathsf{dWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
”, which might be incomparable with
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.
Put another way,
Theorem

5.12
holds as we can hard-wire a sequence of explicit pseudorandom distributions to implement the approximate counting oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
. Since the argument is standard, we defer the proof of the theorem to
Section

5.6
.

We will use the following result that is implicit in the proof of
(
37
, Theorem 24)
; we refer readers to
37
for precise statements of the assumptions.

Theorem 5.13

(Implicit in
(
37
, Theorem 24)
)
.

Assume the existence of JLS-secure
i\mathcal{O}
and that
{\mathsf{coNP}}
is not contained infinitely often in
{\mathsf{NP}}_{/{\mathsf{poly}}}
. For any theory
\mathcal{T}
extending
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
that satisfies the KPT witnessing property with circuits, there is a
\forall\Sigma_{2}^{b}
sentence in the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
that is provable in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, but is unprovable in
\mathcal{T}
.

Proof Sketch.

(
37
, Theorem 24)
only proves the theorem for a theory called
\mathsf{UAPC}_{1}
. Nevertheless, by a closer inspection, the only property of the theory used in the proof is that
\mathsf{UAPC}_{1}
satisfies the KPT witnessing property with circuits (see
(
37
, Theorem 25)
).
∎

By combining
Theorem

5.12
and
5.13
, it immediately follows that:

Corollary 5.14
.

Assume the existence of JLS-secure
i\mathcal{O}
and that
{\mathsf{coNP}}
is not contained infinitely often in
{\mathsf{NP}}_{/{\mathsf{poly}}}
. There is a
\forall\Sigma_{2}^{b}
sentence in the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
that is provable in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, but is unprovable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

5.3.3
An Open Problem: Further Separations?

Corollary

5.14
shows that
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
is likely strictly stronger than
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. In other words, under computational assumptions, there are
\forall\Sigma_{2}^{b}(\mathsf{PV})
sentences provable in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
that are not provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
. An intriguing open problem is whether
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
is strictly stronger than
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
with respect to
\forall\Sigma_{1}^{b}(\mathsf{PV})
sentences:

Open Problem 6
.

Is there a
\forall\Sigma_{1}^{b}
sentence in the language of
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
that is provable in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, but unprovable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
? In other words, is there an
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
provably total
{\mathsf{TFNP}}
problem (in the language of
\mathsf{PV}
) that is not provably total in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
?

As
\mathsf{LossyCode}
captures the
\forall\Sigma_{1}^{b}
-fragment of
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
(see
Theorem

5.3
), and
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
captures the
\forall\Sigma_{1}^{b}
-fragment of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
(see
Theorem

1.9
), a related question in the theory of pseudorandomness is whether derandomizing
\mathsf{LossyCode}
is harder than derandomizing
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
. Formally:

Open Problem 7
.

Is there a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from
\mathsf{LossyCode}
to
\mathsf{Refuter}(\mathsf{Yao})
? In other words, is there a converse to
Theorem

5.4
?

We note that these two open problems are technically incomparable. For instance, a positive answer to
7
may not give a negative answer to
6
if it does not have a feasible correctness proof. Nevertheless, it is conceivable that techniques developed for one of them are likely useful for the other.

5.4
Predictor Extraction Lemma: Proof of
Lemma

5.6

See
5.6

Before proving this lemma, we briefly explain the intuition. Recall that
{\mathsf{APX}}
is defined as the extension of
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
by additional axioms:
2.2
,
2.2
,
2.2
, and
2.2
. An
{\mathsf{APX}}
proof of the equation
t_{1}(x)=t_{2}(x)
is, at a high level, a proof of the following statement: For every interpretation of the oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
, either
t_{1}(x)=t_{2}(x)
, or the oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
does not satisfy one of the axioms.

For our specific implementation of the oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
in
Lemma

5.6
,
2.2
,
2.2
and
2.2
are always satisfied, therefore only
2.2
can be violated. In such cases, for a circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{t}\to\{0,1\}
and strings
\Delta,B
(constructed in the
{\mathsf{APX}}
proof) such that

\left|\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{\leq t})]-\frac{1}{2}\left(\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}0)]+\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}1)]\right)\right|>2\cdot\frac{1}{|\Delta|}+\frac{1}{|B|},

following a similar argument as in the proof of Yao’s lemma (see, e.g.,
(
6
, Chapter 9)
or
Section

4.1
