), we can construct a predictor from
C
via a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm.

From a conceptual point of view, the argument crucially explores that a predictor can be constructed not only from the ability to distinguish a distribution from a random string, as in the standard formulation of Yao’s lemma, but also from the ability to detect a
local inconsistency
when using the distribution as a random source for approximate counting. This is a perspective that might be of independent interest.

In order to implement this intuition, we prove the lemma using a careful proof-theoretic analysis. Formally, we will prove
Lemma

5.6
by induction on the
{\mathsf{APX}}
proof
\pi
of the equation
t_{1}(x)=t_{2}(x)
. The functions
\ell,k,m,s,\delta
and the algorithm
E
will be determined based on the last rule or axiom of
\pi
and the functions and algorithms obtained from the induction hypothesis.

Proof of
Lemma

5.6
.

We will prove a stronger statement: For any provable equation
e(x):t_{1}(\vec{x})=t_{2}(\vec{x})
, there are non-decreasing polynomials
k_{0}(n),m_{0}(n)
such that the lemma holds for every polynomials
k(n)\geq k_{0}(n)
and
m(n)\geq m_{0}(n)
, when the function
m(n)
is non-decreasing. We prove this by induction on the length of the proof.

Consider the axiom or rule used in the last line of the proof that concludes
e(x)
.

2.2
.

Suppose that
e
is a provable equation in
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
and it is introduced via the
2.2
.
25
25

25

Note that our proof does not look into the
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
proof of
e
; it works provided that
e
is a
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
provable equation.
Then, for any interpretation of
{\mathsf{P}}
and any string
x
,
e(x)
must be true. We set
k_{0}(n)
to be sufficiently large such that
t\leq k_{0}(n)
for every
C:\{0,1\}^{t}\to\{0,1\}
queried in simulations
t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x}),t_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x})
on
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
; this is possible as
t_{1},t_{2}
are polynomial-time oracle algorithms. We set other functions and
E
arbitrarily as
t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x})=t_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(x)
always holds.

Suppose that
e
is one of the equations encoding
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)\in\mathbb{Q}
,
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)\leq 1
, or
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)\geq 0
, where
C,\Delta
are open variables. Let
k_{0}(n)\triangleq n
and
m_{0}(n)=1
. Similar to the previous case, one can see that for any
k(n)\geq k_{0}(n)
and
m(n)\geq m_{0}(n)
,
e(\vec{x})
must be true if we interpret
{\mathsf{P}}
following
Equation

5.1
. We can set other functions and
E
arbitrarily as
t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x})=t_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x})
always holds.

2.2
.

Suppose that
e(\vec{x})
is an equation encoding that for any circuit
C\in B_{n}
,
\mathsf{IsConst}(C)\to{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)=\mathsf{Bool}(C)
. Note that
C
and
\Delta
are the only open variables of the equation. We can set
k_{0}(n)=n
and
m_{0}(n)=1
. For every
k(n)\geq k_{0}(n)
and
m(n)\geq m_{0}(n)
, we can set
E
arbitrarily.

This is correct as for every
(C,\Delta)\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and every distribution
\mathcal{D}
of size
m(n)
over
k(n)\geq n
bits, when we interpret
{\mathsf{P}}
following
Equation

5.1
, if
C:\{0,1\}^{t}\to\{0,1\}
is a constant circuit, we have
t\leq n\leq k(n)
and

{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)=\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{\leq t})]=\mathsf{Bool}(C).

In other words,
t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(C,\Delta)=t_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(C,\Delta)
is always true.

2.2
.

This is similar to the case for the
2.2
. Indeed, when the oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
is interpreted as
Equation

5.1
,
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta_{1})={\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta_{2})
for
any

\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
.

2.2
.

In this case,
e(\vec{x})
is an equation encoding the following sentence: For every circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{t}\to\{0,1\}
and strings
\Delta,B
, we have

\left|{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)-\frac{{\mathsf{P}}(\mathsf{Fix}_{0}(C),\Delta)+{\mathsf{P}}(\mathsf{Fix}_{1}(C),\Delta)}{2}\right|\leq\frac{2}{|\Delta|}+\frac{1}{|B|},

where
\mathsf{Fix}_{\sigma}(C)
is a
\mathsf{PV}
-term that outputs the circuit obtained from
C
by fixing the rightmost input bit to
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
. In this equation,
C,\Delta,B
are the only open variables. Let
k_{0}(n)
and
m_{0}(n)
be polynomials to be determined later.

Let
k(n)\geq k_{0}(n)
and
m(n)\geq m_{0}(n)
. Let
\vec{x}=(C,\Delta,B)\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
\mathcal{D}\in(\{0,1\}^{k(n)})^{m(n)}
. Suppose that
t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x})\neq t_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x})
. Since the oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
is interpreted following
Equation

5.1
, we have

\left|\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{\leq t})]-\frac{\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}0)]+\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}1)]}{2}\right|>\frac{2}{|\Delta|}+\frac{1}{|B|}\geq\frac{1}{n}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left|\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}u_{t})]-\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}\overline{u_{t}})]\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{\leq t})]-\frac{\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}u_{t})]+\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}\overline{u_{t}})]}{2}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{\leq t})]-\frac{\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}0)]+\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}1)]}{2}\right|\geq\frac{1}{n},

(5.2)

where in the second equality we used linearity of expectation and that for every fixed
u
,
C(u_{<t}u_{t})+C(u_{<t}\overline{u_{t}})=C(u_{<t}0)+C(u_{<t}1)
.

For simplicity, we only consider the case that

\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}u_{t})]-\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}\overline{u_{t}})]\geq\frac{2}{n},

(5.3)

and the other case can be resolved accordingly. We can rewrite the equation above as follows:

\displaystyle\left(\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}0)\oplus 1=u_{t}\land u_{t}=0]-\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}0)\oplus 1\neq u_{t}\land u_{t}=1]\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}1)\oplus 1\neq u_{t}\land u_{t}=1]-\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}1)\oplus 1=u_{t}\land u_{t}=0]\right)\geq\frac{2}{n}.

Therefore, one of the terms in the LHS must be at least
1/n
. Again, we will only consider the case that the first term is at least
1/n
, and the other term can be resolved accordingly.

Notice that

\displaystyle\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}0)\oplus 1=u_{t}\land u_{t}=0]-\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}0)\oplus 1\neq u_{t}\land u_{t}=1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}0)\oplus 1=u_{t}\land u_{t}=0]+\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}0)\oplus 1=u_{t}\land u_{t}=1]-\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[u_{t}=1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}0)\oplus 1=u_{t}]-\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[u_{t}=1]\geq\frac{1}{n}.

Subsequently, either
\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[u_{t}=1]\leq 1/2-1/(2n)
or
\Pr_{u\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}[C(u_{<t}0)\oplus 1=u_{t}]\geq 1/2+1/(2n)
. In either case – and we can efficiently determine which case holds since
\mathcal{D}
is explicitly given – we can construct a circuit of size at most
|C|\leq s\triangleq n
that predicts the
t
-th bit of
\mathcal{D}
from the first
(t-1)
bits with advantage at least
\delta\triangleq 1/(2n)
. By setting
m_{0}(n)\triangleq n^{4}
and
k_{0}(n)\triangleq n
, we can ensure that

(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m(n)\geq s+\lceil\log k(n)\rceil+1

when
n
is sufficiently large.

2.2
.

We will only consider the substitution rule
t_{1}=t_{2}\vdash t_{1}(x/t)=t_{2}(x/t)
; other logical rules can be resolved accordingly. In this case,
e(\vec{x})
is of form
t_{1}(x/t)=t_{2}(x/t)
, where
t
is a term and
x
is one of the open variables of
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, and there is a shorter proof of the premise
t_{1}=t_{2}
. Without loss of generality, we assume that the variable
x
does not occur in the term
t
.

Let
\vec{y}
be the open variables in
t
but not in
t_{1},t_{2}
,
\vec{z}
be the open variables in
t_{1},t_{2}
(excluding
x
) but not in
t
, and
\vec{w}
be the open variables in both
t
and
t_{1},t_{2}
(excluding
x
). By the induction hypothesis, there are polynomials
k_{0}^{\prime}(n),m_{0}^{\prime}(n)
such that for every polynomials
k^{\prime}(n)\geq k_{0}^{\prime}(n),m^{\prime}(n)\geq m_{0}^{\prime}(n)
, there are
\delta^{\prime}(n)
,
s^{\prime}(n)
, and an algorithm
E^{\prime}(\vec{x},\mathcal{D})
that satisfies the lemma for the equation

t_{1}(x,\vec{z},\vec{w})=t_{2}(x,\vec{z},\vec{w}).

(5.4)

Let
\ell(n)
be an upper bound on the output length of
t
when the input length is at most
n
(this is called the
bounding value
of the term, see
29
). We define
k_{0}(n)\triangleq k_{0}^{\prime}(n+\ell(n))
and
m_{0}(n)\triangleq m_{0}^{\prime}(n+\ell(n))
.

To show that this is correct, fix any
k(n)\geq k_{0}(n)
and
m(n)\geq m_{0}(n)
, and let
s(n),\delta(n)
be determined later. The algorithm
E
works as follows. Given any
(\vec{y},\vec{z},\vec{w})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
\mathcal{D}\in(\{0,1\}^{k(n)})^{m(n)}
such that

t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(t(\vec{y},\vec{w}),\vec{z},\vec{w})\neq t_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(t(\vec{y},\vec{w}),\vec{z},\vec{w}),

(5.5)

our goal is to output a predictor of a bit of
\mathcal{D}
with size
s(n)
and advantage
\delta(n)
.

The algorithm first computes
x_{\mathcal{D}}\triangleq t^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{y},\vec{w})
, which is a string of length at most
\ell(n)
. By
Equation

5.5
and the definition of the simulation,

t_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(x_{\mathcal{D}},\vec{z},\vec{w})\neq t_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(x_{\mathcal{D}},\vec{z},\vec{w}).

Subsequently, by the induction hypothesis (with functions
k^{\prime}(n+\ell(n))\geq k(n)
and
m^{\prime}(n+\ell(n))\geq m(n)
),
E^{\prime}((x_{\mathcal{D}},\vec{z},\vec{w}),\mathcal{D})
outputs
(i,P)
such that
P
is a circuit of size
s
that predicts the
i
-th bit of
\mathcal{D}
with advantage
\delta
such that

((\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m^{\prime}(n+\ell(n))\geq s+\lceil\log k^{\prime}(n+\ell(n))\rceil+1.

It suffices to define
E((\vec{y},\vec{z},\vec{w}),\mathcal{D})\triangleq E^{\prime}((x_{\mathcal{D}},\vec{z},\vec{w}),\mathcal{D})
.

2.2
.

In this case,
e
is of form
f_{1}(x,\vec{y})=f_{2}(x,\vec{y})
for
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
functions
f_{1},f_{2}
, and there are
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
functions
g,h_{0},h_{1}
such that there are shorter proofs of equations

\displaystyle f_{j}(\varepsilon,\vec{y})=g(\vec{y})

(5.6)

\displaystyle f_{j}(s_{i}(x),\vec{y})=h_{i}(x,\vec{y},f_{j}(x,\vec{y}))

(5.7)

for
j\in\{1,2\}
and
i\in\{0,1\}
. By the induction hypothesis, the lemma holds for each of the
6
equations above.

Let
k_{0}(n)
and
m_{0}(n)
are polynomials to be determined. For any polynomials
k(n)\geq k_{0}(n)
and
m(n)\geq m_{0}(n)
, we will design an algorithm
E
that, given
(x,\vec{y})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
\mathcal{D}\in(\{0,1\}^{k(n)})^{m(n)}
satisfying

\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{D}}_{1}(x,\vec{y})\neq f_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(x,\vec{y}),

it outputs
(i,P)
such that
P
is a circuit of size
s
that predicts the
i
-th bit of
\mathcal{D}
with advantage
\delta
such that
(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m(n)\geq s+\lceil\log k(n)\rceil+1
.

Case 1.

Suppose that
f_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(\varepsilon,\vec{y})\neq f_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(\varepsilon,\vec{y})
. Then there exists
j\in\{1,2\}
such that
f_{j}^{\mathcal{D}}(\varepsilon,\vec{y})\neq g^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{y})
. Note that
\vec{y}
is of length at most
n
. As
Equation

5.6
admits a shorter proof, by the induction hypothesis, there are polynomials
k_{0}^{(j)}(n^{\prime}),m_{0}^{(j)}(n^{\prime})
such that when

k(n^{\prime})\geq k_{0}^{(j)}(n^{\prime}),\quad m(n^{\prime})\geq m_{0}^{(j)}(n^{\prime}),

(5.8)

then
E_{j}(\vec{y},\mathcal{D})
outputs a size-
s
predictor with advantage
\delta
such that
(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m(n^{\prime})\geq s+\lceil\log k(n^{\prime})\rceil+1
, where
n^{\prime}
denotes the input length of
\vec{y}
. It then suffices to define

\displaystyle E((x,\vec{y}),\mathcal{D})\triangleq E_{j}(\vec{y},\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle k_{0}(n)\triangleq k_{0}^{(j)}(n)\in{\mathsf{poly}}(n),\quad m_{0}(n)\triangleq m_{0}^{(j)}(n)\geq m_{0}^{(j)}(n^{\prime}).

Case 2.

Let
t\triangleq|x|
. The algorithm
E
first finds the first index
i\leq t
such that
f_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(x_{\leq i},\vec{y})=f_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x}_{\leq i},\vec{y})
but
f_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(x_{\leq i+1},\vec{y})\neq f_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(\vec{x}_{\leq i+1},\vec{y})
; such an index must exist as
f_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(\varepsilon,\vec{y})=f_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(\varepsilon,\vec{y})
and
f_{1}^{\mathcal{D}}(x,\vec{y})\neq f_{2}^{\mathcal{D}}(x,\vec{y})
.

Let
\sigma\triangleq x_{i+1}
. Then there exists
j\in\{1,2\}
such that

f_{j}^{\mathcal{D}}(s_{\sigma}(x_{\leq i}),\vec{y})\neq h_{\sigma}^{\mathcal{D}}(x_{\leq j},\vec{y},f_{j}^{\mathcal{D}}(x_{\leq i},\vec{y})).

That is, the string
(x_{\leq j},\vec{y})
of length at most
n
violates
Equation

5.7
when the approximate counting oracle
{\mathsf{P}}
is implemented using
\mathcal{D}
following
Equation

5.1
. By the induction hypothesis applied to
Equation

5.7
, there are polynomials
k_{0}^{\prime}(n)
and
m_{0}^{\prime}(n)
such that when

k(n)\geq k_{0}^{\prime}(n),\quad m(n)\geq m_{0}^{\prime}(n),

(5.9)

there is an algorithm
E^{\prime}
such that
E^{\prime}((x_{\leq i},\vec{y}),\mathcal{D})
outputs a size-
s
predictor with advantage
\delta
such that
(\delta^{2}/10)\cdot m(n)\geq s+\lceil\log k(n)\rceil+1
. It then suffices to define
E((x,\vec{y}),\mathcal{D})\triangleq E^{\prime}((x_{\leq i},\vec{y}),\mathcal{D})
.

Wrapping up.

Finally, we set
k_{0}(n)
and
m_{0}(n)
to be sufficiently large polynomials such that when
k(n)\geq k_{0}(n)
and
m(n)\geq m_{0}(n)
, both
Equation

5.8
and (
5.9
) hold. Therefore, in either case, the algorithm
E
satisfies the requirement of the lemma.
∎

Remark 5.15
.

By looking into the proof, we note that the polynomials
k_{0}(n),m_{0}(n)
(which define the minimum size of the distribution
\mathcal{D}
) and the running time of
E
depend on the
{\mathsf{APX}}
proof; in particular, they may far exceed the running time of the terms
t_{1},t_{2}
in the equation
e
.

For instance,
k_{0}(n)
is defined as
k_{0}^{\prime}(n+\ell(n))
in the case for logical rules, where
\ell(n)
is the output length (i.e. bounding value) of a term
t
in the proof, and the term
t
does not necessarily appear in the final equation. At a high level, this is because we need to set the distribution
\mathcal{D}
to be large enough to accommodate
all oracle queries
in the
{\mathsf{APX}}
proof; we can then look through the proof and find a violation of the
2.2
, which produces a predictor.

5.5
Simulating
{\mathsf{P}}(C,\Delta)
with
\mathsf{NW}(C,\Delta)
: Proof of
Theorem

5.9

We follow the notation in
46
. A set
X
is said to be a
bounded set
defined by a circuit
C
if
X=\{x<a\mid C(x)=1\}
. We use
x\in X
to denote the formula
x<a\land C(x)=1
, and
X\subseteq b
to denote the formula
\forall x\in X~x<b
. Note that bounded definable sets are
not
objects in the theory
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, but an abbreviation in the meta-theory. For two bounded definable sets
X\subseteq a
and
Y\subseteq b
, we define

\displaystyle X\times Y\triangleq\{bx+a\mid x\in X,y\in Y\}\subseteq ab,

\displaystyle X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}Y\triangleq X\cup\{y+a\mid y\in Y\}\subseteq a+b.

We say
C:X\to Y
if
C
is a circuit from
X
to
Y
, i.e., for every
x\in X
,
C(x)\in Y
. We say
C:X\hookrightarrow Y
if the circuit
C:X\to Y
is injective, i.e., for
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
,
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
,
C(x_{1})\neq C(x_{2})
. We use
C:X\twoheadrightarrow Y
to denote that
C
is onto, i.e., for all
y\in Y
, there exists an
x\in X
such that
C(x)=y
.

Definition 5.16

(in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
)
.

Let
X,Y\subseteq 2^{n}
be definable sets, and
\varepsilon\leq 1
. We say that
X
is
\varepsilon
-approximately smaller than
Y
, denoted by
X\lesssim_{\varepsilon}Y
, if there exists a circuit
G
and
v\neq 0
such that

G:v\times(Y\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}\varepsilon 2^{n})\twoheadrightarrow v\times X.

Definition 5.17

(in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
)
.

We say that
X
and
Y
are
\varepsilon
-approximately of equal size, denoted by
X\approx_{\varepsilon}Y
, if
X\lesssim_{\varepsilon}Y
and
Y\lesssim_{\varepsilon}X
. In particular, we say that
X
is
\varepsilon
-approximately of size
s
if
X\approx_{\varepsilon}s
.

Lemma 5.18

(
(
46
, Lemma 2.10)
)
.

Let
X,Y,X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Z\subseteq 2^{n}
and
W,W^{\prime}\subseteq 2^{n}
be bounded definable sets, and
\varepsilon,\delta\leq 1
. The following statements are provable in
\mathsf{PV}_{1}
.

(1)

If
X\lesssim_{\varepsilon}Y,\varepsilon\leq\delta
, then
X\lesssim_{\delta}Y
.

(2)

If
X\lesssim_{0}Y
, then
X\lesssim_{\varepsilon}Y
.

(3)

If
X\lesssim_{\varepsilon}Y
,
Y\lesssim_{\delta}Z
, then
X\lesssim_{\delta+\varepsilon}Z
.

(4)

If
X\lesssim_{\varepsilon}X^{\prime}
,
Y\lesssim_{\delta}Y^{\prime}
, and
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
are separable by the set
W
(i.e.,
X^{\prime}\subseteq W
and
Y^{\prime}\subseteq 2^{n}\setminus W
), then
X\cup Y\lesssim_{\varepsilon+\delta}X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
.

(5)

If
X\lesssim_{\varepsilon}X^{\prime},W\lesssim_{\delta}W^{\prime}
, then
X\times W\lesssim_{\varepsilon+\delta+\varepsilon\delta}X^{\prime}\times W^{\prime}
.

Lemma 5.19

(
(
46
, Lemma 2.11)
)
.

Let
X,Y\subseteq 2^{n}
be bounded definable sets,
s,t,u\leq 2^{n}
,
\varepsilon,\delta,\eta,\xi\leq 1
, and
\xi^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. The following statements are provable in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
.

(1)

There exists
s\leq 2^{n}
such that
X\approx_{\xi}s
.

(2)

s\lesssim_{\varepsilon}X\lesssim_{\delta}t
implies
s\leq t+(\varepsilon+\delta+\xi)\cdot 2^{n}
.

(3)

X\lesssim_{\xi}Y
or
Y\lesssim_{\xi}X
.

(4)

X\lesssim_{\varepsilon}Y
implies
2^{n}\setminus Y\lesssim_{\varepsilon+\xi}2^{n}\setminus X
.

(5)

X\approx_{\varepsilon}s
,
Y\approx_{\delta}t
,
X\cap Y\approx_{\eta}u
imply
X\cup Y\approx_{\varepsilon+\delta+\eta+\xi}s+t-u
.

Lemma 5.20

(Implicit in
(
46
, Lemma 2.14)
)
.

Let
\varepsilon<1/3
. There is a
\mathsf{PV}(\alpha)
function
\mathsf{Size}(\cdot,\cdot)
such that the following sentence is provable in
\mathsf{HARD}^{\mathsf{A}}
: For every
n,\delta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and set
X\subseteq 2^{n}
defined by a circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
, the following holds:

•

0\leq\mathsf{Size}(C,1^{\delta^{-1}})\leq 2^{n}
;

•

X\approx_{\delta}\mathsf{Size}(C,1^{\delta^{-1}})
;

•

If
C
is a constant circuit that always outputs
0

(
resp.
1
)
, then
\mathsf{Size}(C,\Delta)=0

(
resp.
\mathsf{Size}(C,\Delta)=2^{n}
)
.

Proof Sketch.

We assume some familiarity with
46
.
The first two bullets of the lemma hold for a
\mathsf{PV}(\alpha)
function symbol as the only non-uniformity in
(
46
, Theorem 2.7)
is the choice of the hard function, which is given by
\alpha
. The last bullet holds as
\mathsf{Size}(C,\Delta)
is obtained by computing the acceptance probability of
C
on a pseudorandom distribution produced via the Nisan-Wigderson PRG, and for a circuit that always accepts (resp. rejects), its acceptance probability on any distribution is always
1
(resp.
0
).

We refer interested readers to
(
37
, Appendix D)
for a self-contained presentation of the proof of
(
46
, Theorem 2.7)
.
∎

See
5.9

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{Size}(\cdot,\cdot)
be the function in
Lemma

5.20
. We define
\mathsf{NW}(C,\Delta)\triangleq\mathsf{Size}(C,1^{|\Delta|})/2^{n}
, where
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
is a circuit. Note that we can encode the rational number
\mathsf{Size}(C,1^{|\Delta|})/2^{n}

precisely
using
n
binary digits so that there is no rounding issue; recall that in the definition of
{\mathsf{APX}}
, the output length of
{\mathsf{P}}
could be as large as
|C|\cdot|\Delta|^{2}>n
(see
Section

2.1
).

It suffices to verify that the axioms are provable in
\mathsf{HARD}^{\mathsf{A}}
. In the rest of the proof, we argue in
\mathsf{HARD}^{\mathsf{A}}
.

•

(
2.2
). It follows immediately from the first bullet of
Lemma

5.20
.

•

(
2.2
). It follows immediately from the third bullet of
Lemma

5.20
.

•

(
2.2
). Let
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a circuit,
\Delta,B
be strings with
\delta=1/|\Delta|
,
\beta=1/|B|
,
C_{0}\triangleq\mathsf{Fix}_{0}(C),C_{1}\triangleq\mathsf{Fix}_{1}(C)
. We need to prove that

\left|\mathsf{NW}(C,\Delta)-\frac{\mathsf{NW}(C_{0},\Delta)+\mathsf{NW}(C_{1},\Delta)}{2}\right|\leq\delta+\beta.

(5.10)

Let
X,X_{0},X_{1}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{n}
be the bounded sets defined by
C,C_{0},C_{1}
, respectively. Let
s\triangleq\mathsf{Size}(C,1^{|\Delta|})
,
s_{0}=\mathsf{Size}(C_{0},1^{|\Delta|})
,
s_{1}\triangleq\mathsf{Size}(C_{1},1^{|\Delta|})
. By the second bullet of
Lemma

5.20
, we know that

X\approx_{\delta}s,\quad X_{0}\approx_{\delta}s_{0},\quad X_{1}\approx_{\delta}s_{2}.

Note that as
X_{0}\cap X_{1}=\varnothing
, we have that
X_{0}\cap X_{1}\approx_{0}0
. By
Lemma

5.19
(
5
),
X_{0}\cup X_{1}\approx_{2\delta}s_{0}+s_{1}
.

Let
f:v_{1}(s+\delta\cdot 2^{n})\twoheadrightarrow v_{1}\times X
be the witness of
X\lesssim_{\delta}s
, and
g:v_{2}\times((X_{0}\cup X_{1})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\cup$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\cup$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\crcr}}}}2\delta\cdot 2^{n-1})\twoheadrightarrow v_{2}(s_{0}+s_{1})
be the witness of
s_{0}+s_{1}\lesssim_{2\delta}X_{0}\cup X_{1}
. We define a function
h

\displaystyle h:v_{1}v_{2}(s+2\delta\cdot 2^{n})\twoheadrightarrow v_{1}v_{2}(s_{0}+s_{1})

as follows:

(
i
)

Let
i_{1}<v_{1},i_{2}<v_{2},j<s+\delta\cdot 2^{n}
, the tuple
(i_{1},i_{2},j)\in v_{1}v_{2}(s+2\delta\cdot 2^{n})
. We compute
(i^{\prime}_{1},x)\triangleq f(i_{1},j)\in v_{1}\times X
, where
x_{\leq n-1}\in X_{0}\cup X_{1}
. The algorithm then computes
(i_{2}^{\prime},j^{\prime})\triangleq g(i_{2},x_{\leq n-1})\in v_{2}(s_{0}+s_{1})
, and outputs
h(i_{1},i_{2},j)\triangleq(i_{1}^{\prime},i_{2}^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.

(
ii
)

Let
i_{1}<v_{1},i_{2}<v_{2},s+\delta\cdot 2^{n}\leq j<s+2\delta\cdot 2^{n}
, the tuple
(i_{1},i_{2},j)\in v_{1}v_{2}(s+2\delta\cdot 2^{n})
. Note that
j-(s+\delta\cdot 2^{n})\in\delta\cdot 2^{n}=2\delta\cdot 2^{n-1}
. The algorithm then computes
(i_{2}^{\prime},j^{\prime})\triangleq g(i_{2},j-(s+\delta\cdot 2^{n}))\in v_{2}(s_{0}+s_{1})
, and outputs
h(i_{1},i_{2},j)\triangleq(i_{1},i_{2}^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.

It can be verified that the function is indeed onto, and thus by definition,
s_{0}+s_{1}\lesssim_{2\delta}s
. Similarly, we can prove that
s\lesssim_{2\delta}s_{0}+s_{1}
. Then we have

s\lesssim_{0}s\lesssim_{2\delta}s_{0}+s_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad s_{0}+s_{1}\lesssim_{0}s_{0}+s_{1}\lesssim_{2\delta}s,

by
Lemma

5.19
(
2
), we have that
s\in(s_{0}+s_{1})\pm(2\delta+\beta)\cdot 2^{n}
.
This immediately implies
Equation

5.10
as
\mathsf{NW}(C,\Delta)\triangleq\mathsf{Size}(C,1^{|\Delta|})/2^{n}
.

•

(
2.2
). Let
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a circuit,
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},B
be strings and
\delta_{1}\triangleq 1/|\Delta_{1}|,\delta_{2}\triangleq 1/|\Delta_{2}|
,
\beta\triangleq 1/|B|
. We need to prove that

|\mathsf{NW}(C,\Delta_{1})-\mathsf{NW}(C,\Delta_{2})|\leq\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\beta.

(5.11)

Let
X\subseteq\{0,1\}^{n}
be the bounded set defined by
C
, and let
s_{i}\triangleq\mathsf{Size}(C,1^{|\Delta_{i}|})
,
i\in\{1,2\}
. By the second bullet of
Lemma

5.20
,

X\approx_{\delta_{1}}s_{1},\quad X\approx_{\delta_{2}}s_{2}.

Therefore, we have
s_{1}\lesssim_{\delta_{1}}X\lesssim_{\delta_{2}}s_{2}
, and by
Lemma

5.19
(
2
),
s_{1}\leq s_{2}+(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\beta)\cdot 2^{n}
. Similarly, we can prove that
s_{2}\leq s_{1}+(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\beta)\cdot 2^{n}
. This immediately implies
Equation

5.10
as
\mathsf{NW}(C,\Delta)\triangleq\mathsf{Size}(C,1^{|\Delta|})/2^{n}
.

This completes the proof.
∎

5.6
A KPT Witnessing Theorem for
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
: Proof of
Theorem

5.12

Recall the definition of the KPT witnessing property with circuits:

See
5.11

See
5.12

To prove
Theorem

5.12
, we will need the standard KPT witnessing theorem for universal first-order theories
57
; interested readers are referred to
(
74
, Theorem 3.2)
for detailed discussions.

Theorem 5.21

(KPT witnessing theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a universal theory. Let
\varphi(\vec{x},y,z)
be a quantifier-free formula in the language of
\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathcal{T}\vdash\forall\vec{x}~\exists y~\forall z~\varphi(\vec{x},y,z)
. Then there is a constant
k\in\mathbb{N}
and terms
t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{k}
(in the language of
\mathcal{T}
) such that the following statement is provable in
\mathcal{T}
:

For every
\vec{x}
and every
z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{k}
, either
\varphi(\vec{x},t_{1}(\vec{x}),z_{1})
, or
\varphi(\vec{x},t_{2}(\vec{x},z_{1}),z_{2})
, or
\varphi(\vec{x},t_{3}(\vec{x},z_{1},z_{2}),z_{3})
,
\dots
, or
\varphi(\vec{x},t_{k}(\vec{x},z_{1},\dots,z_{k-1}),z_{k})
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.12
.

Recall that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
admits a universal axiomatization (see
Proposition

2.8
). By
Theorem

5.21
with
\mathcal{T}\triangleq{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, if
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}\vdash\forall\vec{x}~\exists y~\forall z~\varphi(\vec{x},y,z)
, there is a constant
k\in\mathbb{N}
and terms
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
such that
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
proves the following sentence:

(
\Phi
): For every
\vec{x}
and
z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{k}
, either
\varphi(\vec{x},t_{1}(\vec{x}),z_{1})
, or
\varphi(\vec{x},t_{2}(\vec{x},z_{1}),z_{2})
, or
\varphi(\vec{x},t_{3}(\vec{x},z_{1},z_{2}),z_{3})
,
\dots
, or
\varphi(\vec{x},t_{k}(\vec{x},z_{1},\dots,z_{k-1}),z_{k})
.

Note that
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
are
\mathsf{PV}({\mathsf{P}})
terms, which can be interpreted as polynomial time
{\mathsf{P}}
-oracle algorithms.

Similarly to the proof of
Theorem

1.9
, we can rewrite the universal sentence (
\Phi
) as an equation
e_{\Phi}
in
{\mathsf{APX}}
, such that it is provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}
. Therefore, we know by the soundness of
{\mathsf{APX}}
(see
Proposition

2.7
) that
e_{\Phi}
is true in any standard model
\mathbb{M}(\hat{\mathsf{P}})
.

Let
\mathsf{CAPP}
be the search problem that, given any circuit
C
and a string
\Delta
, outputs a number
p\in\Pr[C(x)]\pm 1/|\Delta|
. It is well-known that the problem is in
\mathsf{pr}{\mathsf{BPP}}
(see, e.g.,
33
), and thus can be computable by a family of deterministic polynomial-size circuits. Fix any family of circuits
F(C,\Delta)
that solves
\mathsf{CAPP}
. By definition,
\mathbb{M}(F)
is a standard model of
{\mathsf{APX}}
. Subsequently,
e_{\Phi}
is true in the model
\mathbb{M}(F)
.

The theorem follows by setting
f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k}
as
t_{1}^{\mathbb{M}(F)},t_{2}^{\mathbb{M}(F)},\dots,t_{k}^{\mathbb{M}(F)}
. Since for each fixed input length, there is a polynomial upper bound on the size of
C
and on the length of
\Delta
in the oracle calls to
F(C,\Delta)
during the computation of
t_{1}
,
\ldots
,
t_{k}
, this allows us to fix a family of polynomial-size circuits for
f_{1}
,
\ldots
,
f_{k}
.
∎

6
Reverse Mathematics of Randomized and Average-Case Lower Bounds

The retraction weak pigeonhole principle for polynomial-time functions is one of the most important combinatorial principles that is known to be provable in
\mathsf{APC}_{1}
, but unknown to be provable in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
. In this section, we explore counting variants of the retraction pigeonhole principle and characterize their equivalence class (with respect to provability in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
). We show that this class encompasses certain average-case and randomized communication complexity lower bounds, establishing that these results are all equivalent to appropriate variants of the retraction pigeonhole principle.

6.1
Variants of the Retraction Pigeonhole Principle

We start with the definition of the Retraction Weak Pigeonhole Principle
\mathsf{rWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
. For simplicity, we introduce the following notation. We use
m:\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}
to denote a
\mathsf{PV}
function symbol
m(n)
whose input and output are encoded in unary. We use
\varepsilon:\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}^{-1}
to denote a
\mathsf{PV}
function symbol
e(n)
whose input and output are encoded in unary, and
\varepsilon(n)
is an abbreviation of the rational number
1/e(n)
.

Definition 6.1

(retraction weak pigeonhole principle)
.

Let
m:\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}
. The retraction weak pigeonhole principle
\mathsf{rWPHP}[m](\mathsf{PV})
with stretch
m
denotes the following statement in the language of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
:

For every
n\in\mathsf{Log}
and circuits
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{m(n)}
,
D:\{0,1\}^{m(n)}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
, if
m(n)<n
, then there exists a string
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
D(C(x))\neq x
.

We will define two variants of
\mathsf{rWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
: an approximate counting version
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
, and a randomized compression version
\mathsf{rrWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
.

Definition 6.2

(approximate counting
\mathsf{rWPHP}
)
.

Let
m:\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log},\varepsilon:\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}^{-1}
. The approximate counting retraction weak pigeonhole principle
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[m,\varepsilon](\mathsf{PV})
with stretch
m
and error
\varepsilon
denotes the following statement in the language of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
:

For every
n\in\mathsf{Log}
and circuits
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{m(n)}
,
D:\{0,1\}^{m(n)}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
T
be the circuit such that
T(x)=1
if
D(C(x))\neq x
. Then, if
m(n)<n
and
\varepsilon(n)<1-2^{n-m(n)}
, there exists
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
such that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)>\varepsilon(n)+\delta+\beta
.

Definition 6.3

(randomized compression
\mathsf{rWPHP}
)
.

Let
m:\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}
,
\varepsilon:\mathsf{Log}\times\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}^{-1}
. The randomized compression retraction weak pigeonhole principle
\mathsf{rrWPHP}[m,\varepsilon](\mathsf{PV})
with stretch
m
and error
\varepsilon
denotes the following statement in the language of
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
:

For every
n,r\in\mathsf{Log}
and circuits
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{r}\to\{0,1\}^{m(n)}
,
D:\{0,1\}^{m(n)}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
, if
m(n)<n
and
\varepsilon(n+r)<1-2^{n-m(n)}
, then there exists an
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
such that the following holds: Let
T_{x}:\{0,1\}^{r}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit such that
T_{x}(\mathsf{sd})=1
if and only if
D(C(x,\mathsf{sd}))\neq x
. Then
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{x})>\varepsilon(n,r)+\delta+\beta
.

It follows immediately from the definition that both variants of
\mathsf{rWPHP}
are true statements in any standard model of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
, which is left as an exercise.

Proposition 6.4
.

For every
m:\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}
,
\varepsilon:\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}^{-1}
,
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[m,\varepsilon](\mathsf{PV})
and
\mathsf{rrWPHP}[m,\varepsilon](\mathsf{PV})
are true statements in any standard model
\mathbb{M}(\hat{\mathsf{P}})
of
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

These principles can be viewed as the worst-case and (weak) average-case hardness of compression-decompression algorithms. Specifically:

•

\mathsf{rWPHP}[m](\mathsf{PV})
says that for any deterministic compression-decompression pair
(C,D)
with compression rate
m
, there is an incompressible string;

•

\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[m,\varepsilon](\mathsf{PV})
says that for any deterministic compression-decompression pair
(C,D)
with compression rate
m
, there is an
\varepsilon
-fraction of incompressible strings;

•

\mathsf{rrWPHP}_{m}(\mathsf{PV})
says that for any
(C,D)
where
C
is a
randomized
compression algorithm and
D
is a
deterministic
decompression algorithm, there must be an input string over which the compression-decompression pair has
error probability

\varepsilon
.

A classical result in bounded arithmetic is that the retraction weak pigeonhole principle admits a stretch reduction in
\mathsf{PV}
. Concretely:

Theorem 6.5

(
85
;
44
)
.

For any constant
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\mathsf{PV}+\mathsf{rWPHP}[{n^{\varepsilon}}](\mathsf{PV})\vdash\mathsf{rWPHP}[{n-1}](\mathsf{PV})
.

6.2
One-Way Communication Lower Bounds

We prove an equivalence result involving
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}(\mathsf{PV})
and communication complexity lower bounds for
Set Disjointness
against
one-way protocols
with either
public randomness
or
private randomness
.

Formalization of One-Way Communication Protocols.

We start with the formalization of a communication protocol. Let
n,m,r\in\mathsf{Log}
. A pair of circuits
g_{\mathsf{A}}:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{r}\to\{0,1\}^{m}
and
d_{\mathsf{B}}:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{m}\times\{0,1\}^{r}\to\{0,1\}
defines a one-way randomized communication protocol as follows:

•

(
Public Coin Model
). On any pair of inputs
(x,y)\in\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
and a uniformly generated public random seed
\mathsf{sd}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, Alice sends the message
\mathsf{msg}\triangleq g_{\mathsf{A}}(x,\mathsf{sd})
to Bob, and Bob decides to accept if and only if
d_{\mathsf{B}}(y,\mathsf{msg},\mathsf{sd})=1
.

•

(
Private Coin Model
). On any pair of inputs
(x,y)\in\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
and uniformly generated private random seeds
\mathsf{sd}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, Alice sends the message
\mathsf{msg}\triangleq g_{\mathsf{A}}(x,\mathsf{sd})
to Bob, and Bob decides to accept if and only if
d_{\mathsf{B}}(y,\mathsf{msg},0^{r})=1
.

Let
f:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a function specified by a circuit. For every
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
T^{\mathsf{pub}}_{f,x,y}:\{0,1\}^{r}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit such that
T^{\mathsf{pub}}_{f,x,y}(\mathsf{sd})=1
if and only if the public-coin protocol outputs
1-f(x,y)
on the input
(x,y)
with seed
\mathsf{sd}
, i.e.,

T^{\mathsf{pub}}_{f,x,y}(\mathsf{sd})\triangleq\mathbb{I}[d_{\mathsf{B}}(y,g_{\mathsf{A}}(x,\mathsf{sd}),\mathsf{sd})\neq f(x,y)]\in\{0,1\}.

(6.1)

Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
. We say that a public-coin protocol
(g_{\mathsf{A}},d_{\mathsf{B}})
computes the function
f
with error
\varepsilon
if for
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T^{\mathsf{pub}}_{f,x,y})\leq\varepsilon+\delta+\beta.

(6.2)

Note that here we consider the two-sided error setting, while one can also naturally define the correctness in terms of one-sided error.

Accordingly, one may define
T^{\mathsf{priv}}_{f,x,y}:\{0,1\}^{r}\to\{0,1\}
to be the circuit such that
T^{\mathsf{pub}}_{f,x,y}(\mathsf{sd})=1
if and only if the private-coin protocol outputs
1-f(x,y)
on the input
(x,y)
with seed
\mathsf{sd}
, i.e.,

T^{\mathsf{priv}}_{f,x,y}(\mathsf{sd})\triangleq\mathbb{I}[d_{\mathsf{B}}(y,g_{\mathsf{A}}(x,\mathsf{sd}),0^{r})\neq f(x,y)]\in\{0,1\}.

(6.3)

We say that a private-coin protocol
(g_{\mathsf{A}},d_{\mathsf{B}})
computes the function
f
with error
\varepsilon
if for every
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{f,x,y}^{\mathsf{priv}})\leq\varepsilon+\delta+\beta.

(6.4)

Communication Complexity Lower Bounds.

Fix any function
f:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
,
n,m,r\in\mathsf{Log}
, and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
. We define the sentence
\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{f}[n,m,r,\varepsilon]
as follows: For every
public-coin
protocol
(g_{\mathsf{A}},d_{\mathsf{B}})
as defined above,
(g_{\mathsf{A}},d_{\mathsf{B}})
fails to compute
f
with error
\varepsilon
. In other words, there are
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
such that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T^{\mathsf{pub}}_{f,x,y})>\varepsilon+\delta+\beta
.

Accordingly, we define the sentence
\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{f}[n,m,r,\varepsilon]
as follows: For every
private-coin
protocol
(g_{\mathsf{A}},d_{\mathsf{B}})
as defined above,
(g_{\mathsf{A}},d_{\mathsf{B}})
fails to compute
f
with error
\varepsilon
.

Recall that the Set Disjointness function
\mathsf{SetDisj}(x,y)
outputs
1
if and only if for every index
i\in[n]
, either
x_{i}=0
or
y_{i}=0
, i.e.,
x
and
y
have no common
1
-index. Let
m:\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}
,
\varepsilon:\mathsf{Log}\times\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}^{-1}
be functions. We define
\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[m,\varepsilon]
as the following sentence:

For
n,r\in\mathsf{Log}
,
\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n,m(n),r,\varepsilon(n,r)]
.

In other words, every public-coin one-way protocol computing
\mathsf{SetDisj}
with communication complexity
m(n)
must have error probability at least
\varepsilon(n)
. As we will prove in
Section

6.3
, the lower bound is correct even for
m(n)=n-n^{\Omega(1)}
. Accordingly, we define
\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[m,\varepsilon]
as the following sentence:

For
n,r\in\mathsf{Log}
,
\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n,m(n),r,\varepsilon(n,r)]
.

We also consider a weaker statement that, instead of formalizing the lower bound for a specific function, formalizes the
existence
of a function for which the lower bound holds. Let
m:\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log},\varepsilon:\mathsf{Log}\times\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}^{-1}
. We define
\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\mathsf{rLB}^{\mathsf{some}}[m,\varepsilon]
as the following sentence:

For
n,r\in\mathsf{Log}
, there exists a circuit
f:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
such that
\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{f}[n,m(n),r,\varepsilon(n,r)]
holds.

In other words, there exists a function
f
such that every public-coin one-way protocol computing
f
with communication complexity
m(n)
must have a non-negligible error probability. This is implied by
\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[m,\varepsilon]
by fixing
f
to be
\mathsf{SetDisj}
. Accordingly, we can define
\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[m,\varepsilon]
as the following sentence:

For
n,r\in\mathsf{Log}
, there exists a circuit
f:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
such that
\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{f}[n,m(n),r,\varepsilon(n,r)]
holds.

Upper bound for Equality.

As a sanity check, we note that as there is a communication complexity
upper bound
for
Equality
with public randomness using linear hashing (see
Theorem

4.5
), the corresponding lower bound is unprovable in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
.

Theorem 6.6

(Upper Bound for Equality)
.

There are
\mathsf{PV}
functions
m:\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}
,
\varepsilon:\mathsf{Log}\to\mathsf{Log}^{-1}
satisfying that
m(n)=\Theta(\log n)
,
\varepsilon(n)=1-1/n^{\Theta(1)}
such that

{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}\vdash\forall n~\lnot\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{EQ}}[n,m(n),n\cdot m(n),\varepsilon(n)].

(6.5)

In particular,
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}\vdash\lnot\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\mathsf{rLB}[m,\varepsilon]
.

Proof Sketch.

We argue in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
that
Equation

6.5
holds, where
m,r,\varepsilon
will be determined later. As the cases when
n
is small can be proved in brute-force, it suffices to consider
n>n_{0}
, where
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
is a constant to be determined later.

Fix any
n>n_{0}
. The one-way communication works as follows. Let
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
be the input for Alice and
y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
be that for Bob. They parse the public randomness as a matrix
A\in\{0,1\}^{m(n)\times n}
. Alice sends
Ax\in\{0,1\}^{m(n)}
as the message, and Bob accepts if and only if
Ax=Ay
. It remains to prove that the protocol works with error at most
\varepsilon(n)
.

Fix any input
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. If
x=y
, the protocol always accepts. In other words, the circuit
T_{\mathsf{EQ},x,y}^{\mathsf{pub}}
in
Equation

6.1
is a constant circuit that always rejects. The correctness, i.e.
Equation

6.2
, follows immediately from the
2.2
. For the case that
x\neq y
, the circuit
T_{\mathsf{EQ},x,y}^{\mathsf{pub}}
is functionally equivalent to the negation of the circuit
T_{x,y}
in
Theorem

4.5
. Therefore, by
3.2.4
, the theorem holds as long as we set
m(n)=n^{O(1)}
,
\varepsilon(n)>1-0.51^{m(n)}
, and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large. This completes the proof.
∎

6.3
The Main Equivalence Result for Communication Complexity

We establish an equivalence between several statements with respect to their provability in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
.

Theorem 6.7
.

The following statements are equivalent over
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
:

(1)

\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k}](\mathsf{PV})
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
is some constant;

(2)

\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon},n^{-k}](\mathsf{PV})
, where
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
are some constants;

(3)

\mathsf{rrWPHP}[n-1,(n+r)^{-k}](\mathsf{PV})
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
is some constant;

(4)

\mathsf{rrWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon},(n+r)^{-k}](\mathsf{PV})
, where
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
are some constants;

(5)

\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n-1,(n+r)^{-k}]
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
is some constant;

(6)

\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n^{\varepsilon},(n+r)^{-k}]
, where
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
are some constants;

(7)

\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n-1,(n+r)^{-k}]
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
is some constant;

(8)

\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n^{\varepsilon},(n+r)^{-k}]
, where
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
are some constants;

(9)

\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[n-1,(n+r)^{-k}]
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
is some constant;

(10)

\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[n^{\varepsilon},(n+r)^{-k}]
, where
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
are some constants;

(11)

\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[n-1,(n+r)^{-k}]
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
is some constant;

(12)

\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[n^{\varepsilon},(n+r)^{-k}]
, where
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
are some constants.

Remark 6.8
.

In the statements above, the quantification over
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
takes place outside the theory. For instance, (
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
2
) means that for every
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a
k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon_{2}\in(0,1)
such that the sentence

\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k_{1}}](\mathsf{PV})\rightarrow\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon_{2}},n^{-k_{2}}](\mathsf{PV}).

is provable in
{\mathsf{APX}}_{1}
.

Trivial directions.

Both (
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
2
) and (
3
)
\Rightarrow
(
4
) are straightforward. Indeed, a compression-decompression pair with small stretch can be converted into one with larger stretch by padding dummy bits. It is also easy to observe that statements (
5
) to (
12
) form a lattice isomorphic to a three-dimensional Boolean cube with respect to implication over
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
, where (
5
) is the maximal element (i.e., the strongest lower bound) and (
12
) is the minimal element (i.e., the weakest lower bound). This is because lower bounds against public-coin protocols imply lower bounds against private-coin protocols;
n-1
communication lower bounds imply
n^{\Omega(1)}
communication lower bounds; and lower bounds for
\mathsf{SetDisj}
imply lower bounds for some function
f
(by fixing
f
to be
\mathsf{SetDisj}
).

Non-trivial directions.

Observe that, in order to complete the proof of
Theorem

6.7
, it suffices to establish the following implications: (
12
)
\Rightarrow
(
4
), (
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
5
), (
2
)
\Rightarrow
(
3
), and (
4
)
\Rightarrow
(
1
). The proof of these implications is provided in the subsequent sections.

6.3.1
Compression Implies Communication Upper Bound: (
12
)
\Rightarrow
(
4
)

Lemma 6.9
.

For every
\varepsilon_{12}\in(0,1)
and
k_{12}\in\mathbb{N}
, there are
\varepsilon_{4}\in(0,1)
and
k_{4}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathsf{APX}_{1}+\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[n^{\varepsilon_{12}},(n+r)^{-k_{12}}]\vdash\mathsf{rrWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon_{4}},(n+r)^{-k_{4}}](\mathsf{PV}).

Proof.

Fix any constant
\varepsilon_{6}\in(0,1)
,
k_{12}\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\varepsilon_{2}\triangleq\varepsilon_{6}
and
k_{4}\triangleq k_{12}
. Let
m_{4}(n)\triangleq n^{\varepsilon_{4}}
and
m_{12}(n)\triangleq n^{\varepsilon_{12}}
. We will prove in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
that
\lnot\mathsf{rrWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon_{4}},n^{-k_{4}}](\mathsf{PV})
implies
\lnot\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[n^{\varepsilon_{12}},n^{-k_{12}}]
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{rrWPHP}[m_{4},n^{-k_{4}}](\mathsf{PV})
does not hold. Then there are
n,r\in\mathsf{Log}
and circuits
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{r}\to\{0,1\}^{m_{2}(n)},D:\{0,1\}^{m_{2}(n)}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
such that the following holds. Let
T_{x}
be the circuit that
T_{x}(\mathsf{sd})=1
if
D(C(x,\mathsf{sd}))\neq x
. Then for every
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{x})\leq(n+r)^{-k_{4}}+\delta+\beta.

(6.6)

Fix
n,r,C,D
as described above.

We will now prove that
\lnot\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{some}}[n^{\varepsilon_{12}},n^{-k_{12}}]
. In particular, we will prove that for every
f:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
,
\mathsf{priv}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{f}[n^{\varepsilon_{12}},n^{-k_{12}}]
does not hold. Fix any circuit
f:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
. Our goal is to construct a private-coin communication protocol with communication complexity
n^{\varepsilon_{12}}
that computes
f
with error
n^{-k_{12}}
. The protocol works as follows:

•

Given
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and uniformly random seed
\mathsf{sd}
, Alice sends the message
g_{\mathsf{A}}(x,\mathsf{sd})\triangleq C(x,\mathsf{sd})
.

•

Given
y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and the message
\mathsf{msg}
, Bob accepts if and only if
d_{\mathsf{B}}(y,\mathsf{msg},\mathsf{sd})\triangleq f(D(\mathsf{msg}),y)=1
.

To prove that the protocol computes
f
with error
n^{-k_{12}}
, fix any
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
. Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later, and
T_{f,x,y}^{\mathsf{priv}}(\mathsf{sd})
be the circuit as defined in
Equation

6.3
. It can be verified that for every
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
\mathsf{sd}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, if
T_{f,x,y}^{\mathsf{priv}}(\mathsf{sd})=1
, then
T_{x}(\mathsf{sd})=1
. By the
3.1.1
,

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{f,x,y}^{\mathsf{priv}})\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{x})+3\eta\leq(n+r)^{-k_{4}}+5\eta,

where the last inequality follows from
Equation

6.6
. It then follows from the
2.2
that

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{f,x,y}^{\mathsf{priv}})\leq\delta+{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{f,x,y}^{\mathsf{priv}})\leq(n+r)^{-k_{4}}+\delta+5\eta\leq(n+r)^{-k_{4}}+\delta+\beta

by setting
\eta\triangleq\beta/5
. This completes the proof.
∎

6.3.2
Compression from Communication Upper Bound: (
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
5
)

Lemma 6.10
.

For every constant
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a
k_{5}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathsf{APX}_{1}+\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k_{1}}](\mathsf{PV})\vdash\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n-1,(n+r)^{-k_{5}}]
.

Proof.

Fix any
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
and let
k_{5}\in\mathbb{N}
be determined later. We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
that
\lnot\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n-1,n^{-k_{5}}]
implies
\lnot\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k_{1}}](\mathsf{PV})
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{pub}\text{-}\underrightarrow{\mathsf{rLB}}^{\mathsf{SetDisj}}[n-1,n^{-k_{5}}]
does not hold. Then there are
n,r\in\mathsf{Log}
and a one-way public-coin protocol
g_{\mathsf{A}}:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{r}\to\{0,1\}^{n-1}
,
d_{\mathsf{B}}:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n-1}\times\{0,1\}^{r}\to\{0,1\}
such that the following holds: For every
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, let
T_{\mathsf{SetDisj},x,y}^{\mathsf{pub}}(\mathsf{sd})
be the circuit defined as
Equation

6.1
, then

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{\mathsf{SetDisj},x,y}^{\mathsf{pub}})\leq(n+r)^{-k_{5}}+\delta+\beta.

(6.7)

Our goal is to construct a compression-decompression scheme that violates
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k_{1}}](\mathsf{PV})
.

Construction of the compression scheme.

We construct a pair of circuits
C:\{0,1\}^{n+r}\to\{0,1\}^{n+r-1},D:\{0,1\}^{n+r-1}\to\{0,1\}^{n+r}
as follows.

•

(
Compression
): The circuit
C
parses the input as
(x,\mathsf{sd})\in\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{r}
and computes
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n}
defined as

\sigma_{i}\triangleq d_{\mathsf{B}}(e_{i},g_{\mathsf{A}}(x,\mathsf{sd}),\mathsf{sd})\oplus x_{i}\oplus 1,

(6.8)

where
e_{i}
denotes the string with the
i
-th bit being its only
1
-index. If
e\neq 0^{n}
, the compression fails and it outputs
0^{n}
. Otherwise, it outputs the concatenation of
\mathsf{msg}\triangleq g_{\mathsf{A}}(x,\mathsf{sd})
and
\mathsf{sd}
.

•

(
Decompression
): The circuit
D
parses the input as the concatenation of
\mathsf{msg}
and
\mathsf{sd}
as mentioned above, computes
y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
as

y_{i}\triangleq d_{\mathsf{B}}(e_{i},\mathsf{msg},\mathsf{sd})\oplus 1,

and outputs the concatenation of
y
and
\mathsf{sd}
.

It is clear that when
\sigma=0^{n}
, the compression-decompression scheme is correct.

Analysis of the error probability.

We will prove that
(C,D)
is a compression-decompression scheme that violates
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k_{1}}](\mathsf{PV})
. Fix any
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and let
T:\{0,1\}^{n+r}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that
T(z)
outputs
1
if
D(C(z))\neq z
. Our goal is to prove that
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)\leq n^{-k_{1}}+\delta+\beta
.

Let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later, and
T^{\prime}:\{0,1\}^{n+r}\to\{0,1\}
be the following circuit: Given
(x,\mathsf{sd})\in\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{r}
, it computes
\sigma
via
Equation

6.8
, and outputs
1
if and only if
\sigma\neq 0^{n}
. As mentioned above, for every
x
and
\mathsf{sd}
,
T(x\circ\mathsf{sd})=1
implies that
T^{\prime}(x\circ\mathsf{sd})=1
. Therefore, by the
3.1.1
, we have

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T)\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{\prime})+3\eta.

(6.9)

Let
V\triangleq\{0,1\}
and
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}
be the random variables supported over
\{0,1\}^{n+r}
such that
X_{i}=1
if and only if
e_{i}\neq 0
. Let
F_{i}(x,\mathsf{sd})
be the circuit that defines
X_{i}
for every
i\in[n]
. It is clear that
T^{\prime}(x,\mathsf{sd})
is the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
X_{i}=1
for some
i\in[n]
. Let
Y
be the random variable defined by
(V,n+r,T^{\prime})
. By the
3.3.1
, we have

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{\prime})\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]+3\eta\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]+\dots+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{n}]+3\eta\cdot(n+1),

(6.10)

where the first inequality follows from
Proposition

3.12
.

In addition, for every
i\in[n]
and every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, we can see that
F_{i}(x,\cdot)
is functionally equivalent to
T_{\mathsf{SetDisj},x,e_{i}}^{\mathsf{pub}}(\cdot)
. Let
X_{i}|_{x}
be the random variable obtained by fixing the first part of the seed to be
x
. Then for
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}|_{x}]\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(F_{i}(x,\cdot))+3\eta\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{\mathsf{SetDisj},x,e_{i}}^{\mathsf{pub}})+6\eta\leq(n+r)^{-k_{5}}+8\eta,

where the last inequality follows from
Equation

6.7
. By
3.17
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{i}]\leq(n+r)^{-k_{5}}+8\eta+3\eta\leq n^{-k_{5}}+11\eta.

(6.11)

Combining the results above, we have:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T)+\delta+2\eta

(
2.2
)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T^{\prime})+\delta+5\eta

(
Equation

6.9
)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{1}]+\dots+\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{n}]+\delta+3\eta\cdot(n+1)+5\eta

(
Equation

6.10
)

\displaystyle\leq\delta+((n+r)^{-k_{5}}+11\eta)\cdot n+3\eta\cdot(n+1)+5\eta

(
Equation

6.11
)

\displaystyle\leq(n+r)^{-k_{1}}+\delta+\beta,

where the last inequality follows by setting
k_{5}\triangleq k_{1}+1
and
\eta\triangleq\beta/(50n)
. This violates
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k_{1}}](\mathsf{PV})
and thus completes the proof.
∎

6.3.3
Stretch Reduction for Compression: (
2
)
\Rightarrow
(
3
)

Lemma 6.11
.

For any
\varepsilon_{2}\in(0,1)
and
k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
k_{3}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathsf{APX}_{1}+\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon_{2}},n^{-k_{2}}](\mathsf{PV})\vdash\mathsf{rrWPHP}[n-1,(n+r)^{-k_{3}}](\mathsf{PV})
.

Proof.

Fix any constant
\varepsilon_{2}\in(0,1)
and
k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
k_{3}\in\mathbb{N}
be determined later. We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
that if
\mathsf{rrWPHP}[n-1,(n+r)^{-k_{3}}](\mathsf{PV})
does not hold, then
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon_{2}},n^{-k_{2}}](\mathsf{PV})
does not hold.

Suppose that
\mathsf{rrWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k_{3}}](\mathsf{PV})
does not hold. Then there are
n,r\in\mathsf{Log}
and circuits
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{r}\to\{0,1\}^{n-1}
,
D:\{0,1\}^{n-1}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
such that for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and every
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
, let
T_{x}:\{0,1\}^{r}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit such that
T_{x}(\mathsf{sd})=1
if and only if
D(C(x,\mathsf{sd}))\neq x
, then

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{x})\leq(n+r)^{-k_{3}}+\delta+\beta.

(6.12)

In other words, there is a one-bit randomized compression scheme that is worst-case correct with error
n^{-k_{3}}
. Our goal is to construct a deterministic and average-case compression-decompression algorithm that violates
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon_{2}},n^{-k_{2}}](\mathsf{PV})
.

Compression and decompression circuits.

Let
\ell\in\mathsf{Log}
and
d\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
be parameters to be determined later. The compression circuit takes an
(\ell+dr)
-bit string as input, parses it as
z\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and
(\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, and runs a
d
-round iterative compression algorithm.

Initialize
z_{0}\leftarrow z
. In the
i
-th round, the iterative algorithm works as follows:

1.

Parse
z_{i-1}
as
x_{1}\circ x_{2}\circ\dots\circ x_{k}\circ y_{i}
, where
k\triangleq\lfloor|z_{i-1}|/n\rfloor
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.

2.

For every
j\in[k]
, compute
x^{\prime}_{j}\triangleq C(x_{j},\mathsf{sd}_{i})
.

3.

Set
z_{i}\leftarrow x^{\prime}_{1}\circ x^{\prime}_{2}\circ\dots\circ x^{\prime}_{k}
.

Finally, the compression circuit outputs the encoding of the tuple
(z_{d},y_{1},\dots,y_{d},\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})
.

The decompression circuit takes
(z_{d},y_{1},\dots,y_{d},\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})
and works reversely via a
d
-round iterative algorithm. In the
i
-th iteration, the algorithm works as follows:

1.

Parse
z_{d+1-i}
as
x_{1}^{\prime}\circ x_{2}^{\prime}\circ\dots\circ x_{k}^{\prime}
, where
k\triangleq\lfloor|z_{d+1-i}|/(n-1)\rfloor
and
x^{\prime}_{1},\dots,x^{\prime}_{k}\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}
.

2.

For every
j\in[k]
, compute
x_{j}\triangleq D(x_{j}^{\prime})
.

3.

Set
z_{d-i}\leftarrow x_{1}\circ x_{2}\circ\dots\circ x_{k}\circ y_{d+1-i}
.

We now set the parameters
\ell
and
d
such that the compression scheme above has stretch at least
(\ell+dr)^{\varepsilon_{2}}
. Let
z_{0},z_{1},\dots,z_{d},y_{1},\dots,y_{d}
be the strings obtained by the compression algorithm, it is clear that

|z_{i}|\leq|z_{i-1}|\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right),|y_{i}|\leq n,

and thus the output length of the compression circuit is at most

\ell\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)^{d}+d\cdot(n+r).

We can set
\ell=(n+r)^{10/\varepsilon_{2}}
and
d=10\cdot n\log\ell
such that the output length is at most
O(n(n+r)\log\ell)\ll\ell^{\varepsilon_{2}}\leq(\ell+dr)^{\varepsilon_{2}}
. Therefore, the compression stretch is
(\ell+dr)^{\varepsilon_{2}}
for sufficiently large
n
and
r
; the cases when
n,r
are small can be proved by a brute-force case study.

Analysis of the error probability.

Fix
\ell\in\mathsf{Log}
and
d\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
as above. Let
C^{\prime}:\{0,1\}^{\ell+dr}\to\{0,1\}^{(\ell+dr)^{\varepsilon_{2}}}
,
D^{\prime}:\{0,1\}^{(\ell+dr)^{\varepsilon_{2}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell+dr}
be the compression and decompression algorithms mentioned above. Let
T:\{0,1\}^{\ell+dr}
be the circuit that
T(z)=1
if and only if
D^{\prime}(C^{\prime}(z))\neq z
, i.e., the compression scheme fails. Our goal is to prove that for every
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)\leq(\ell+dr)^{-k_{2}}+\delta+\beta
.

Fix any
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. Let
V\triangleq\{0,1\}
. For every
i\in[d]
and
j\leq\lfloor\ell/n\rfloor
, we define
F_{ij}(z,\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if the following holds:

•

In the
i
-th round of the compression algorithm, let
k\triangleq\lfloor|z_{i-1}|/n\rfloor
, then
j\leq k
and
D(C(x_{j},\mathsf{sd}_{i}))\neq x_{j}
.

Let
X_{ij}
be the random variable defined by
(V,\ell+dr,F_{ij})
. Let
F(z,\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
F_{ij}(z,\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})=1
for some
i\in[d]
and
j\leq\lfloor\ell/n\rfloor
, and
Y
be the random variable defined by
(V,\ell+dr,F_{ij})
. By the
3.3.1
, we have

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(F)\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]+3\eta\leq\sum_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{ij}]+3\eta\cdot(d\ell+1),

(6.13)

where the first inequality follows from
Proposition

3.12
.

It is clear that
\mathsf{PV}
proves that for every
z\in\{0,1\}^{\ell},\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, if
T(z,\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})=1
, then
F(z,\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})=1
. To see this, notice that if
F(z,\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})=0
, we can prove by induction on
i
that if we run the iterative compression algorithm on the input
z\circ\mathsf{sd}_{1}\circ\dots\circ\mathsf{sd}_{d}
for
i
rounds, and run the iterative decompression algorithm starting from the
d-i
round, it will be correctly decompressed. This can be implemented by induction on a
\mathsf{PV}
term, which is available in
\mathsf{PV}
. Subsequently, by the
3.1.1
,

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T)\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(F)+3\eta.

(6.14)

Next, we prove an upper bound on
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{ij}]
. Fix any
i\in[d]
and
j\leq\lfloor\ell/n\rfloor
. Let

\rho=(z,\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{i-1},\mathsf{sd}_{i+1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})

be an arbitrary assignment to all but the interval
\mathsf{sd}_{i}
in the seed of
X_{ij}
. Let
x_{j}
be the string in the
i
-th round of the compression algorithm on the input
z
and using
\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{i-1}
in the first
i-1
rounds. Note that
x_{j}
can be computed by a
\mathsf{PV}
term given
\rho
. Recall that
T_{x_{j}}(\mathsf{sd})
is the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
D(C(x_{j},\mathsf{sd}))\neq x_{j}
. It can be proved that
T_{x_{j}}(\mathsf{sd}_{i})=1
if and only if
F_{ij}(\rho\cup\mathsf{sd}_{i})=1
, i.e.,
X_{ij}|_{\rho}
is the indicator variable of
T_{x_{j}}(\mathsf{sd})=1
. Subsequently,

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{ij}|_{\rho}]\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{x_{j}})+6\eta\leq(n+r)^{-k_{3}}+8\eta,

(6.15)

where the first inequality follows from
Proposition

3.12
and
3.1.1
, and the second inequality follows from
Equation

6.12
.

Note that
Equation

6.15
holds for any assignment
\rho
. By
3.17
, we can further deduce that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{ij}]\leq n^{-k_{3}}+8\eta+3\eta\leq n^{-k_{3}}+11\eta.

(6.16)

Combining the results above, we can now calculate

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T)+\delta+2\eta

(
2.2
)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(F)+\delta+5\eta

(
Equation

6.14
)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{ij}]+3\eta\cdot(d\ell+1)+\delta+5\eta

(
Equation

6.13
)

\displaystyle\leq((n+r)^{-k_{3}}+11\eta)\cdot d\cdot\ell+3\eta\cdot(d\ell+1)+\delta+5\eta

(
Equation

6.16
)

\displaystyle\leq(\ell+dr)^{-k_{2}}+\delta+\beta,

where the last inequality follows by setting
\eta\triangleq\beta/(100(d\ell+1))
and
k_{3}\triangleq 100k_{2}/\varepsilon_{2}+10k_{2}+10
. This shows that the pair of circuits
C^{\prime},D^{\prime}
violates
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon_{2}},n^{-k_{2}}](\mathsf{PV})
and thus completes the proof.
∎

6.3.4
Worst-Case to Average-Case Reduction: (
4
)
\Rightarrow
(
1
)

Lemma 6.12
.

For any
\varepsilon_{4}\in(0,1)
and
k_{4}\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathsf{APX}_{1}+\mathsf{rrWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon_{4}},(n+r)^{-k_{4}}](\mathsf{PV})\vdash\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k_{1}}](\mathsf{PV})
.

We will use the iterative compression algorithm in
Lemma

6.11
to boost the stretch to
m_{2}
, while a new trick is required to construct worst-case compression from average-case compression algorithm. At a high level, we observe that the compression-decompression problem with large stretch admits
random self-reducibility
that is provably correct via the
4.5.1
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.12
.

Fix any constant
\varepsilon_{4}\in(0,1)
,
k_{4}\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
be determined later. We argue in
\mathsf{APX}_{1}
that assuming
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k_{4}}](\mathsf{PV})
does not hold,
\mathsf{rrWPHP}[n^{\varepsilon_{4}},(n+r)^{-k_{4}}](\mathsf{PV})
also does not hold. In other words, we will construct a polynomial-stretch randomized worst-case compression scheme from a one-bit deterministic average-case compression scheme.

Assume for contradiction that
\texttt{\#}\mathsf{rWPHP}[n-1,n^{-k_{1}}](\mathsf{PV})
does not hold. Then there is an
n\in\mathsf{Log}
and circuits
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{n-1}
,
D:\{0,1\}^{n-1}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
such that the following holds. Let
T
be the circuit that
T(x)=1
if
D(C(x))\neq x
. Then for every
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
,
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)\leq n^{-k_{1}}+\delta+\beta
. By the
4.5.1
, we know that for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
T_{x}^{\oplus}
be the circuit
T_{x}^{\oplus}(\mathsf{sd})\triangleq T(x\oplus\mathsf{sd})
, then

{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{x}^{\oplus})\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)+2\delta+\beta\leq n^{-k_{1}}+3\delta+2\beta.

(6.17)

Note that we can assume without loss of generality that
n
is larger than any fixed standard integer
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, as the cases when
n\leq n_{0}
can be resolved in brute force.

Compression and decompression circuits.

Let
\ell\in\mathsf{Log}
and
d\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
be parameters to be determined later. The compression circuit takes an
\ell
-bit string as input
z
, an
nd
-bit random seed
(\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, and performs the following
d
-round iterative algorithm. It initializes
z_{0}\leftarrow z
. In the
i
-th round, the algorithm works as follows:

1.

Parse
z_{i-1}
as
x_{1}\circ x_{2}\circ\dots\circ x_{k}\circ y_{i}
, where
k\triangleq\lfloor|z_{i-1}|/n\rfloor
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
;

2.

For every
j\in[k]
, compute
x_{j}^{\prime}\triangleq C(x_{j}\oplus\mathsf{sd}_{i})
.

3.

Set
z_{i}\leftarrow x_{1}^{\prime}\oplus x_{2}^{\prime}\oplus\dots\oplus x_{k}^{\prime}
.

Finally, the compression circuit outputs the encoding of the tuple
(z_{d},y_{1},\dots,y_{d},\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})
.

The decompression circuit takes
(z_{d},y_{1},\dots,y_{d},\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})
and works reversely via a
d
-round iterative algorithm. In the
i
-th iteration, the algorithm works as follows:

1.

Parse
z_{d+1-i}
as
x_{1}^{\prime}\circ x_{2}^{\prime}\circ\dots\circ x_{k}^{\prime}
, where
k\triangleq\lfloor|z_{d+1-i}|/(n-1)\rfloor
and
x^{\prime}_{1},\dots,x^{\prime}_{k}\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}
.

2.

For every
j\in[k]
, compute
x_{j}\triangleq D(x_{j}^{\prime})\oplus\mathsf{sd}_{i}
.

3.

Set
z_{d-i}\leftarrow x_{1}\circ x_{2}\circ\dots\circ x_{k}\circ y_{d+1-i}
.

Similar to the proof of
Lemma

6.11
, we can set the parameters
\ell\triangleq n^{10/\varepsilon_{4}}
and
d\triangleq 10\cdot n\log\ell
such that the compression scheme above has stretch at least
\ell^{\varepsilon_{4}}
. The length of random string of the compression scheme is
r\triangleq dn
.

Analysis of the error probability.

Fix
\ell\in\mathsf{Log}
and
d\in\mathsf{Log}\mathsf{Log}
as above. Let
C^{\prime}:\{0,1\}^{\ell}\times\{0,1\}^{dn}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell^{\varepsilon_{4}}}
,
D^{\prime}:\{0,1\}^{\ell^{\varepsilon_{4}}}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell}
be the compression and decompression algorithms mentioned above. Let
T_{z}:\{0,1\}^{dn}\to\{0,1\}
be the circuit that parses the input as
\mathsf{sd}\triangleq(\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})\in\{0,1\}^{dn}
and outputs
1
if and only if
D^{\prime}(C^{\prime}(z,\mathsf{sd}))\neq z
, i.e., the compression scheme fails on the input
z
. Our goal is to prove that for every
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
and
z\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
,
{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T_{z})\leq(\ell+dn)^{-k_{4}}+\delta+\beta
.

Fix any
\delta^{-1},\beta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log},z\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and let
\eta^{-1}\in\mathsf{Log}
be a parameter to be determined later. Let
V\triangleq\{0,1\}
. For every
i\in[d]
and
j\leq\lfloor\ell/n\rfloor
, we define
F_{ij}(\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if the following holds:

•

In the
i
-th round of the compression algorithm, let
k\triangleq\lfloor|z_{i-1}|/n\rfloor
, then
j\leq k
and
D(C(x_{j}\oplus\mathsf{sd}_{i}))\neq x_{j}\oplus\mathsf{sd}_{i}
.

Let
X_{ij}
be the random variable defined by
(V,dn,F_{ij})
. Let
F(\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})
be the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
F_{ij}(\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})=1
for some
i\in[d]
and
j\leq\lfloor\ell/n\rfloor
, and
Y
be the random variable defined by
(V,dn,F_{ij})
. By the
3.3.1
, we have

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(F)\leq\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[Y]+3\eta\leq\sum_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{ij}]+3\eta\cdot(d\ell+1),

(6.18)

where the first inequality follows from
Proposition

3.12
.

It is clear that
\mathsf{PV}
proves that for every
\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, if
T_{z}(\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})=1
, it follows that
F(\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})=1
. To see this, notice that if
F(\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})=0
, we can prove by induction on
i
that if we run the iterative compression algorithm on the input
z
for
i
rounds, and run the iterative decompression algorithm starting from the
d-i
round, it will be correctly decompressed. This can be implemented by induction on a
\mathsf{PV}
term, which is available in
\mathsf{PV}
. Subsequently, by the
3.1.1
,

{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{z})\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(F)+3\eta.

(6.19)

Next, we prove an upper bound on
\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{ij}]
. Fix any
i\in[d]
and
j\leq\lfloor\ell/n\rfloor
. Let

\rho=(\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{i-1},\mathsf{sd}_{i+1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{d})

be an arbitrary assignment to all but the interval
\mathsf{sd}_{i}
in the seed of
X_{ij}
. Let
x_{j}
be the string in the
i
-th round of the compression algorithm on the input
z
and using
\mathsf{sd}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sd}_{i-1}
in the first
i-1
rounds. Note that
x_{j}
can be computed by a
\mathsf{PV}
term given
\rho
. Recall that
T^{\oplus}_{x}(\mathsf{sd})
is the circuit that outputs
1
if and only if
D(C(x\oplus\mathsf{sd}))\neq x\oplus\mathsf{sd}
. It can be proved that
T^{\oplus}_{x_{j}}(\mathsf{sd}_{i})=1
if and only if
F_{ij}(\rho\cup\mathsf{sd}_{i})=1
, i.e.,
X_{ij}|_{\rho}
is the indicator variable of
T_{x_{j}}^{\oplus}(\mathsf{sd})=1
. Subsequently,

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{ij}|_{\rho}]\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T_{x_{j}}^{\oplus})+6\eta\leq n^{-k_{1}}+11\eta,

(6.20)

where the first inequality follows from
Proposition

3.12
and
3.1.1
, and the second inequality follows from
Equation

6.17
.

Note that
Equation

6.20
holds for any assignment
\rho
. By
3.17
, we can further deduce that

\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{ij}]\leq n^{-k_{1}}+11\eta+3\eta\leq n^{-k_{1}}+14\eta.

(6.21)

Combining the results above, we can now calculate

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{\delta}(T)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(T)+\delta+2\eta

(
2.2
)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{P}}_{\eta}(F)+\delta+5\eta

(
Equation

6.19
)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{\eta}[X_{ij}]+3\eta\cdot(d\ell+1)+\delta+5\eta

(
Equation

6.18
)

\displaystyle\leq\left(n^{-k_{1}}+14\eta\right)\cdot d\cdot\ell+3\eta\cdot(d\ell+1)+\delta+5\eta

(
Equation

6.21
)

\displaystyle\leq n^{-k_{1}}\cdot d\ell+\delta+\beta,

where the last inequality follows by setting
\eta\triangleq\beta/(100(d\ell+1))
. Recall that
\ell=n^{10/\varepsilon_{4}}
and
d=10\cdot n\log\ell
, we have

n^{-k_{1}}\cdot d\ell=n^{-k_{1}}\cdot n^{10/\varepsilon_{4}}\cdot\frac{100n}{\varepsilon_{4}}\cdot\log n\leq n^{-k_{4}}\leq(\ell+dn)^{-k_{4}}.

by setting
k_{1}\triangleq 100/\varepsilon_{4}+10k_{4}+10
when
n
is sufficiently large. This shows that
C^{\prime},D^{\prime}
violates
\mathsf{rrWPHP}_{m_{2}}(\mathsf{PV})
and thus completes the proof.
∎
